The Goose Book

The Goose Book

Have you ever been attacked by a goose?

What Is Goose?

Goose is a load testing tool written in Rust and inspired by Locust. A load test is a small Rust program that depends on the goose crate: you build it with Cargo and run it against a host, choosing the users and the run time on the command line.

Where Locust has a user class with tasks, Goose has a scenario with transactions. Requests go through the Reqwest HTTP client. A load test of another protocol, such as raw TCP, times each operation itself and records it in Goose’s metrics.

What It Looks Like

The live dashboard of a 50 user run whose users start one a second: the requests per second climb with them and level off, the tiles other than Users cover the whole run, and each column of the heatmap is one second of response times.

That run came from the Umami example in a checkout of Goose, against a copy of the Drupal Umami demo site. --no-reset-metrics keeps the ramp up in its numbers; without it, Goose clears the metrics when the ramp up ends:

cargo run --release --features dashboard --example umami -- \
  --dashboard -H http://umami.example.com -u 50 -r 1 -t 3m --no-reset-metrics

Why Use Goose

  • One process uses every CPU core. A Goose load test runs its users as async tasks on Tokio’s multi-threaded runtime, which spreads them across a server’s cores. Locust needs a process per core to do the same.
  • Measured throughput. In Tag1’s A Goose In The Clouds (2021, an earlier Goose release), one 8 core server simulated 2,000 users making over 6,500 requests per second and sustained 2.5 Gbps; one 32 core server made 22,900 requests per second, limited by its 9 Gbps uplink.
  • Load tests are Rust code. A load test can use any crate and builds to one binary, to run in CI or a container. See Creating A Load Test.
  • Results a pipeline can act on. Thresholds fail a run on its metrics, and with a baseline the PDF, HTML and Markdown reports show each number’s change since an earlier run.

Watching A Run And Its Reports

The live dashboard above needs the dashboard feature, which is off by default; Watching a run shows how to turn it on. Goose then serves it at http://127.0.0.1:5118/ while a load test runs with --dashboard, and saves the run’s report when it ends. With --dashboard-control and a dashboard token, the dashboard can also start and stop the test and change its users and settings; Turning it on explains the token, and --dashboard-no-auth, which turns authentication off.

When the run ends, each --report-file <FILE> writes one report, its type set by the file’s extension: PDF (from a build with the pdf-reports feature), HTML, Markdown or JSON. Writing reports shows each. Goose also prints its metrics to the console.

Goose 0.19 runs on one server: Gaggle, the distributed load test, was removed in Goose 0.17.0 and will be restored in Goose 0.20, before the 1.0 release. For a distributed load test today, use Goose 0.16.4.

Brought To You By

Goose is made by Tag1 Consulting and led by Jeremy Andrews, Tag1’s CEO, with many community contributions. Tag1 is a member of the Rust Foundation.

More Documentation

  • Getting Started, to build and run a first load test

  • Blog posts at Tag1:

Coming from Goose 0.18? See Upgrading From 0.18.

Requirements

  • In order to write load tests, you must first install Rust.

  • Goose load tests are managed with Cargo, the Rust package manager.

  • Goose sets no minimum Rust version in its Cargo.toml. With the newest releases of its dependencies, Goose 0.19 needs rustc 1.88.0 or later: some of them, such as cookie_store, which Reqwest uses for cookies, require it. Use a current stable Rust.

  • Building Goose compiles aws-lc-rs, the cryptography library behind its TLS support, which needs a C compiler. See its build requirements.

TLS

Goose makes requests with Reqwest 0.13, whose default TLS backend is rustls, so a load test needs no OpenSSL. Reqwest checks certificates with rustls-platform-verifier, which uses the operating system’s certificate store, so a CA you have added to the system is trusted. --accept-invalid-certs turns certificate checks off, for test servers with self signed or expired certificates.

Goose before 0.19 used Reqwest 0.12 and native TLS by default, with rustls through the rustls-tls feature. In 0.19 the rustls-tls feature still exists, so a Cargo.toml that enables it keeps building, but HTTP requests use rustls with or without it.

Goose does not enable Reqwest’s http2 feature, so requests use HTTP/1.1.

Features

Goose has these Cargo features:

FeatureDefaultWhat it adds
cookiesonEach GooseUser keeps its own cookies; see Cookies. Build with default-features = false to leave it out.
dashboardoffThe live web dashboard, served with --dashboard.
pdf-reportsoffPDF reports, written with a .pdf --report-file.
rustls-tlsoffKept so that older Cargo.toml files build; see TLS.

For example, to build a load test with the dashboard:

[dependencies]
goose = { version = "^0.19", features = ["dashboard"] }

Chrome for PDF reports

A .pdf --report-file needs the pdf-reports feature and a Chrome or Chromium browser to render the report. Goose uses the browser the CHROME environment variable names, then an installed Chrome, Chromium or Edge, and otherwise downloads a Chrome for Testing headless shell once into the user cache directory, which needs unzip (tar on Windows) and, on Linux, glibc. See PDF report system requirements.

--pdf-print-html writes a print-ready HTML report to turn into a PDF in any browser, and needs neither Chrome nor the pdf-reports feature.

Glossary

These are the words Goose uses in a sense of its own, each with what it means in Goose and the page that explains it.

Adjusted Metrics

The metrics Goose shows with coordinated omission mitigation on, once mitigation has added any synthetic requests. Goose labels them “adjusted” and shows them beside the “raw” metrics of the requests actually sent. See Coordinated Omission Metrics.

Attack Phase

The stage a GooseAttack is in, which Goose logs as entering GooseAttack phase: and the live dashboard shows: Idle, Increase while users launch, Maintain once all are running, Decrease while they stop, and Shutdown as Goose exits. Idle is Goose waiting for a start from the dashboard or a Controller, before a run or after one. The plan overview lists Decrease as Canceling, not Decreasing, when every user finishes its iterations or when ctrl-c, the dashboard, a Controller, a killswitch or a failed threshold stops the run. See Running A Load Test.

Baseline

An earlier run’s JSON report, or the run folder that holds it, given with --baseline-file. The PDF, HTML and Markdown reports then show each number’s change from it, such as 1,234 (+200). See Comparing Against A Baseline.

Cadence

How long a GooseUser takes to run once through its scenario, leaving out any wait time: its average, fastest or slowest time, as --co-mitigation chooses. Coordinated omission mitigation uses it to tell how many requests a user missed while the server stalled. See Mitigation.

CO Event

A request made after a stall, for which coordinated omission mitigation added synthetic requests. The CO event count counts these requests, not the stalls. See CO Event Tracking.

Controller

The telnet or WebSocket interface that starts and stops a load test and changes its settings, such as its users, host, ramps, run time or test plan. The live dashboard can do the same with dashboard control. See Controllers.

Coordinated Omission

The error a load test makes when its users wait on a stalled server and so send fewer requests, which hides the stall from the metrics. Goose can correct for it with --co-mitigation. See Coordinated Omission.

Dashboard Control

What the live dashboard can do with --dashboard-control: start and stop the test, change its users and settings, turn Save report off and on, delete saved runs, and quit Goose once the test is stopped. It also needs a dashboard token or --dashboard-no-auth. Without control the dashboard is read-only: it shows the run, saves its report and lists saved runs in its runs panel, but cannot change the test. See Turning it on.

Dashboard Token

A secret set with --dashboard-auth-token. The live dashboard then requires it, and a browser gives it by opening the dashboard’s address with ?token=<TOKEN> added. See Turning it on.

Decrease Rate

How many GooseUsers stop each second at the end of a load test, set with --decrease-rate; --decrease-time spreads the stops over a time instead. With neither, all users stop at once. See Controlling the ramp-down rate.

Gaggle

Goose’s distributed load test, in which one Manager and one or more Workers ran a single load test across several servers. It was removed in Goose 0.17.0 and will be restored in Goose 0.20, before the 1.0 release; for a distributed load test today, use Goose 0.16.4. See Gaggle: Distributed Load Test.

Goose Eggs

A companion crate of helpers for validating responses and for load testing Drupal sites, with its own version of the Umami example. See Goose Eggs.

Goose Log

Goose’s own log of what it is doing, written with -G (--goose-log), apart from the logs of requests and transactions. It holds only warnings and errors unless -g (--log-level) adds info messages. See Output Files.

GooseAttack

The load test itself: the scenarios it runs and its configuration, built with GooseAttack::initialize() and run with execute(). See Creating A Load Test.

GooseConfiguration

The structure that holds every run-time option of a load test, parsed from the command line, with defaults the load test can set in code with GooseDefault. See Custom Options.

GooseDefault

The options a load test sets in its code with set_default(), such as a host or a number of users. An option given on the command line wins. See Defaults.

GooseError

The error returned by GooseAttack methods such as initialize(), set_default() and execute(), for example when a run-time option is invalid or no host is configured. A transaction returns a TransactionError instead. See Creating A Load Test.

GooseMetrics

The metrics a load test collects. execute() returns them when the run ends, and the JSON report holds them. See JSON report.

GooseRequest

A request built in a transaction with GooseRequest::builder(), for what the shortcuts such as user.get() and user.post() do not set, such as headers or the status code to expect. See GooseRequest and the Basic Authentication example.

GooseUser

A simulated user: an async task that runs one scenario over and over until the load test ends. Unless its scenario sets a wait time, it runs its transactions back to back, so it makes far more requests than one person would. --users sets how many start. See How many users to simulate.

Graph Data

The per second numbers behind a report’s graphs. When Goose will write a report at the end of the run, it keeps them in memory for every second of the run; without one, it keeps only the last 10 minutes for the live dashboard, and none when there is no dashboard. --report-graph-data also adds them to the JSON report. See Graph data and memory.

Heatmap

A chart of response times over time, on the live dashboard and in the PDF and HTML reports. Each column is a span of time and each row a range of response times; a cell’s color shows how many requests took that long, and lines show each column’s p50, p95 and p99. See The response time heatmap.

Hold

A time when the number of users stays steady: the run time after a ramp up, or a test plan step that keeps the users it has. An rps threshold, on requests per second, counts only the holds. See Thresholds.

Host

The base URL a load test sends its requests to, given with -H (--host). A request’s path, such as /about, is added to it, so one load test can run against any site. See Host to load test.

Increase Rate

How many GooseUsers start each second at the start of a load test, set with --increase-rate, one a second by default; --increase-time sets how long starting them all takes instead. See Specifying the increase rate.

Iteration

One run of a GooseUser through its whole scenario. --iterations stops each user after that many. See Specifying how long the load test will run.

Killswitch

A way to stop a running load test from your own code, by calling goose::trigger_killswitch with a reason. Goose stops the run as it does for ctrl-c, writes every report, and records the run as canceled with that reason. See Killswitch.

Live Dashboard

The web page Goose serves while a load test runs with --dashboard. Goose must be built with the dashboard feature, which is off by default. The page shows users, requests, response times and errors second by second. See Live Dashboard.

Logs

Files that record what happens during a run, each turned on with its own option, such as --request-log. The request, transaction, scenario and error logs record each one as it ends. The debug log holds what the load test writes with log_debug(), such as a failed request and its response. See Logging.

Machine Name

A scenario’s name in lower case letters and digits, with _1, _2 added when two names would be the same. --scenarios uses it to pick the scenarios that run, and --scenarios-list prints it. See Limiting Scenarios.

Metrics Reset

When the ramp up ends, Goose prints the metrics so far and then clears them, so the totals that follow leave out the ramp up. --no-reset-metrics turns the reset off, so the totals include the ramp up. A run limited by --iterations (see Iteration) or shaped by a test plan never resets. See Specifying how long the load test will run.

Plan Overview

A table of each step the run went through, such as Increasing, Maintaining, Decreasing or Canceling, with when it started and ended and how many users ran. The PDF, HTML and Markdown reports show it under their header, and the console prints it last. See Running A Load Test.

Request

One HTTP request a GooseUser makes, or an operation of another protocol recorded with GooseUser::record_custom_request, as in the TCP example. See Creating A Load Test.

Request Name

The name a request is counted under in the metrics: its path, unless the load test names the request, or its transaction names its requests with set_name_for_transaction_and_requests(). See Naming Transactions.

Run Folder

The folder a run writes into when an output flag such as --report-file is given without a path, or when Save report writes the run’s report. It is the folder --output-dir names, or else goose-runs/<YYYYMMDD-HHMMSS>, named for the run’s local start time. See Run folders.

Run Time

How long every user keeps running once all have started, set with --run-time. Starting and stopping the users takes extra time, so a run lasts longer than its run time. See Specifying how long the load test will run.

Running Metrics

The metrics so far, printed to the console every N seconds during a run with --running-metrics N, and during the ramp up also each time a user starts. See Run-Time Options.

Runs Panel

The live dashboard’s list of runs: this run’s run folder and the others beside it that hold a report, to download, compare or, with dashboard control, delete. See Save, compare and delete runs.

Save Report

A run setting that writes the run’s report into its run folder when the run ends. A --report-file given without a path turns it on, and so does --dashboard unless --no-metrics is set; the live dashboard shows it as a switch that dashboard control can turn off and on. See Save, compare and delete runs.

Scenario

A list of transactions one GooseUser runs, such as browsing anonymously or logging in to post. A load test can have many, each with a weight. See Creating A Load Test.

Scheduler

The order in which Goose assigns weighted scenarios to new users and runs a scenario’s transactions: round robin, serial or random. See Scheduling Scenarios And Transactions.

Session Data

Data a GooseUser keeps between its requests, such as a token from a login, stored with set_session_data. See Session.

Snapshot

The JSON state the live dashboard serves at /api/v1/snapshot and streams from /api/v1/events, about once a second. Its version field numbers the snapshot’s format, not the Goose release. See Dashboard Reference.

Start And Stop Transactions

Transactions that run once, outside a scenario’s loop. One marked with set_on_start() runs when each user starts, and one marked with set_on_stop() when it stops. GooseAttack::test_start() registers one that runs once before the first user starts, and test_stop() one that runs once after the last user stops. See set_on_start and test_start.

Synthetic Request

A response time that coordinated omission mitigation adds for a request a user would have sent at its usual cadence had the server not stalled. See Coordinated Omission Metrics.

Test Plan

A list of steps that shapes a load test, given with --test-plan. Each step is a number of users and the time to reach it, written users,time;users,time, to build a ramp up, a hold, a spike or a ramp down. See Test Plan.

Threshold

A pass or fail rule on a load test’s metrics, such as p95<500ms or error-rate<1%, given with --threshold. A rule that fails stops the run by default and always makes it fail. See Thresholds.

Throttle

A limit on how many requests per second all GooseUsers together send, set with --throttle-requests. See Throttle.

Transaction

A function that makes one or more requests and checks the responses, such as loading a page and its static assets, or logging in. See Creating A Load Test.

TransactionError

The error a transaction returns when it fails. A failed request is counted once, with the request errors. A custom message, a bad URL, or a response body that cannot be read or decoded is counted as a transaction error. See Transaction Error Types.

TransactionResult

The type a transaction function returns, Result<(), Box<TransactionError>>: Ok(()) on success, or a boxed TransactionError on failure. See Validating Requests.

Users Ramp

A run shaped by its users, increase rate, run time and ramp down (decrease rate), as opposed to a test plan. When the live dashboard or a Controller changes its users mid run, Goose ramps to the new count and keeps the run time and ramp down where it can. See Change users and settings.

Weight

How often Goose picks a transaction within its scenario, or what share of users a scenario gets. A transaction of weight 3 runs 3 times as often as one of weight 1. See Scheduling Scenarios And Transactions.

Getting Started

This first chapter of the Goose Book provides a high-level overview of writing and running Goose load tests. If you’re new to Goose, this is the place to start.

If you have load tests written for Goose 0.18, Upgrading From 0.18 lists the changes that can break them.

The Importance Of Load Testing

Load testing can help prevent website outages, stress test code changes, and identify bottlenecks. It can also quickly perform functional regression testing. The ability to run the same test repeatedly gives critical insight into the impact of changes to the code and/or systems.

When to Use Goose

Goose is particularly well-suited for:

  • Complex User Workflows: Testing multi-step processes like checkout flows, user registration, or content management workflows
  • API Load Testing: Validating REST APIs, GraphQL endpoints, or microservice interactions under load
  • Performance Regression Testing: Integrating into CI/CD pipelines to catch performance regressions before deployment
  • Capacity Planning: Understanding how your infrastructure scales and where bottlenecks occur
  • Coordinated Omission Detection: Identifying when server slowdowns affect more users than simple metrics suggest

Goose vs Other Load Testing Tools

Unlike tools that focus purely on HTTP request volume, Goose excels at:

  • Stateful Testing: Maintaining sessions, cookies, and authentication across requests
  • Realistic Load Patterns: Simulating actual user behavior rather than just hammering endpoints
  • Developer-Friendly: Written in Rust with type safety and excellent error handling
  • Detailed Analysis: Advanced metrics that reveal hidden performance issues
  • Flexibility: Custom logic, data-driven tests, and complex scenarios

Prerequisites

Before diving into Goose, you should have:

  • Basic Rust Knowledge: Familiarity with Rust syntax, async/await, and error handling
  • HTTP Understanding: Knowledge of HTTP methods, status codes, and web application architecture
  • Testing Mindset: Understanding of what you want to test and what constitutes success

If you are new to load testing, start with Creating a Load Test.

Creating A Load Test

Cargo

Cargo is the Rust package manager. To create a new load test, use Cargo to create a new application. You can give it any name; this page uses loadtest:

$ cargo new loadtest
    Creating binary (application) `loadtest` package
note: see more `Cargo.toml` keys and their definitions at https://doc.rust-lang.org/cargo/reference/manifest.html
$ cd loadtest/

This creates a new directory named loadtest/ containing loadtest/Cargo.toml and loadtest/src/main.rs. Edit Cargo.toml and add Goose and Tokio under the [dependencies] heading, so the file reads:

[package]
name = "loadtest"
version = "0.1.0"
edition = "2024"

[dependencies]
goose = "^0.19"
tokio = "^1"

The edition line is the one your version of Cargo wrote; leave it as it is.

You can now compile all dependencies, though the program still only prints “Hello, world!”. The first build downloads and compiles every crate Goose depends on, so it takes a while:

$ cargo run
    Updating crates.io index
     Locking 253 packages to highest Rust 1.99.0 compatible versions
      Adding generic-array v0.14.7 (available: v0.14.9)
 Downloading crates ...
  Downloaded goose v0.19.0
      ...
   Compiling goose v0.19.0
   Compiling loadtest v0.1.0 (/home/you/loadtest)
    Finished `dev` profile [unoptimized + debuginfo] target(s) in 27.70s
     Running `target/debug/loadtest`
Hello, world!

The package count, the Rust version and the build time depend on your toolchain and your machine. This debug build only checks that everything compiles; Running A Load Test builds with --release, which compiles the dependencies once more.

Creating the load test

To create an actual load test, first add the following line to the top of src/main.rs to make Goose’s functionality available to your code:

use goose::prelude::*;

Note: The prelude adds the following use statements for you, so you don’t need to write them out:

use goose::config::{GooseDefault, GooseDefaultType};
use goose::goose::{
    GooseMethod, GooseRequest, GooseUser, Scenario, Transaction, TransactionError,
    TransactionFunction, TransactionResult,
};
use goose::metrics::{GooseCoordinatedOmissionMitigation, GooseMetrics};
use goose::{scenario, transaction, GooseAttack, GooseError, GooseScheduler};

Then create a transaction function: an async function that makes requests, which Goose runs as a transaction. Goose passes every transaction function a mutable reference to a GooseUser, which makes the web requests and records their metrics. Each GooseUser has its own Reqwest client, and with Goose’s default features that client keeps the user’s cookies from one request to the next (see Cookies). A transaction function usually does not set the host: you give the host when you run the load test, so the same load test runs against different environments without recompiling.

Below the use line, add the following loadtest_index function, which loads the front page:

async fn loadtest_index(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let _goose_metrics = user.get("").await?;

    Ok(())
}

The function is declared async, so a user waiting for a page does not block a CPU core. It returns a TransactionResult, which is either an empty Ok(()) on success or a boxed TransactionError on failure. Here the GooseUser makes a GET request for the front page, given as the empty path "". The .await lets other users run while the server responds. The trailing ? returns early in two cases: Goose could not build or record the request, for example because the URL is invalid, or the run ended while the request waited on a throttle. If the request fails because the server answers with a 404 or because Goose cannot connect to it, the ? does not return. Goose records a failed request, and the function carries on.

The request returns a GooseResponse, which holds Goose’s record of the request and the server’s response. This function stores it in _goose_metrics, and the leading underscore (_) tells the compiler the variable is unused on purpose. The function then returns Ok(()), so Goose counts the transaction as a success even if its request failed. Validating Requests shows how to fail a transaction.

Replace the main() function with the following, which gives it a return type and registers loadtest_index with Goose:

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), GooseError> {
    GooseAttack::initialize()?
        .register_scenario(scenario!("LoadtestTransactions")
            .register_transaction(transaction!(loadtest_index))
        )
        .execute()
        .await?;

    Ok(())
}

scenario! creates a scenario, the list of transactions each user runs over and over, and register_transaction adds loadtest_index to it.

#[tokio::main] is a Tokio macro. Goose is an asynchronous library, and the macro lets you declare main() as async.

If you’re new to Rust, the return type Result<(), GooseError> says that main returns nothing (()) on success and a GooseError on failure. initialize() and execute() can each fail, and the ? after each one ends the program with its error. .execute() is asynchronous, so .await comes before its ?. The final line, Ok(()), returns the empty result expected on success.

The whole src/main.rs now reads:

use goose::prelude::*;

async fn loadtest_index(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let _goose_metrics = user.get("").await?;

    Ok(())
}

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), GooseError> {
    GooseAttack::initialize()?
        .register_scenario(scenario!("LoadtestTransactions")
            .register_transaction(transaction!(loadtest_index))
        )
        .execute()
        .await?;

    Ok(())
}

That is your first load test.

Paths and the host

Goose joins each path to the host the way a browser resolves a link. With a host that has no path, such as http://local.dev/, about/ and /about/ both request http://local.dev/about/. With a host that has a path, use paths that do not start with a / so they stay under it: with --host http://local.dev/site/, about/ requests http://local.dev/site/about/, while /about/ requests http://local.dev/about/. End such a host with a /. Without it, --host http://local.dev/site drops site, and about/ requests http://local.dev/about/.

Naming Transactions

A transaction without a name is listed by its number alone in the transaction metrics. Goose has two methods for naming a transaction, which you call on the transaction!(...) you register. They differ in what they do to the names of the transaction’s requests.

set_name()

Names the transaction only. Each request the transaction makes keeps its own name, which is its path unless the request was given a name.

.register_transaction(transaction!(loadtest_index).set_name("Homepage Load"))

Use when: you want a readable transaction name and still want each request listed by its own path.

set_name_for_transaction_and_requests()

Names the transaction, and every request it makes that has no name of its own. A request named with GooseRequest::builder().name() keeps its own name; every other request in the transaction is listed under the transaction’s name in the request metrics, in place of its path.

Here checkout_flow stands for a transaction function of your own that makes several requests:

.register_transaction(transaction!(checkout_flow).set_name_for_transaction_and_requests("Checkout"))

Use when: a transaction is a multistep flow, such as a login or a checkout, and you want its requests counted together under one name.

Running A Load Test runs the load test; to watch a run live in your browser, turn on the dashboard feature and add --dashboard (see Live Dashboard).

Validating Requests

Error Handling

A transaction reports an error as a TransactionError. A transaction function returns a TransactionResult, which is Result<(), Box<TransactionError>>: the ? operator and .into() box the error for you.

Transaction Error Types

TransactionError has the following variants. Only some of them are counted as transaction errors, as the next sections explain:

  • TransactionError::Custom: user defined errors, such as a failed business logic check
  • TransactionError::InvalidMethod: unsupported HTTP methods
  • TransactionError::LoggerFailed: errors sending log messages to the logger thread; source holds the flume::SendError, boxed
  • TransactionError::MetricsFailed: errors sending metrics to the parent thread
  • TransactionError::RequestCanceled: requests canceled when throttling is enabled and the test ends
  • TransactionError::RequestFailed: a request marked failed with GooseUser::set_failure, or a failed request built with GooseRequest::builder().error_on_fail(); raw_request holds the request’s GooseRequestMetric, boxed
  • TransactionError::Reqwest: network and HTTP client errors, such as connection failures and timeouts
  • TransactionError::Url: URL parsing errors when building requests

Custom Error Handling

The TransactionError::Custom variant lets a transaction function return its own error, from a string literal or a String.

Using String Literals

Return a custom error from a string literal with .into():

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
use goose::prelude::*;

async fn validate_response(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let response = user.get("/api/data").await?;
    let response_data = response.response?;
    let text = response_data.text().await?;

    if !text.contains("expected_content") {
        return Err("Missing expected content in response".into());
    }

    Ok(())
}
}

response.response is the server’s response, or the Reqwest error Goose got instead, such as a connection failure; its ? returns that error. .text().await? returns an error if the body cannot be read.

Using Formatted Strings

For an error message built at run time, use format!:

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
use goose::prelude::*;

async fn business_logic_check(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let response = user.post("/login", "").await?;
    let response_data = response.response?;

    if !response_data.status().is_success() {
        return Err(format!("Login failed with status: {}", response_data.status()).into());
    }

    Ok(())
}
}

Each example starts with its own use line so it stands alone; in your src/main.rs, keep one use goose::prelude::*; at the top. Register each function in main() as in Creating A Load Test, for example with .register_transaction(transaction!(validate_response)), and run the load test with --host.

A transaction that returns any error is counted as failed in the transaction metrics. With --transaction-log, the transaction log records it with "success":false and, unless the request was canceled, the error in its error field.

A TransactionError::Custom or Url error, or a Reqwest error from building the request or from reading or decoding the response body, is counted as a transaction error. With --error-log, Goose writes each one to the error log. The dashboard, the PDF, HTML, Markdown and JSON reports, and the console summary each count them in a transaction errors table, grouped by scenario, transaction and message, most frequent first. For example, take a scenario named ErrUser whose third transaction is registered as transaction!(flaky).set_name("flaky"). If flaky returns Err("every third call fails".into()) on one call in three, the console summary shows:

 === TRANSACTION ERRORS ===
 ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 Count       | Transaction: error
 ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 21,320        1.3 ErrUser flaky: TransactionError: custom error (every third call fails)
 ------------------------------------------------------------------------------

When a request fails, Goose counts it once, when it is made: as a request error in the Errors table, and in the error log with --error-log. If the transaction then returns RequestFailed, or a Reqwest error from sending the request, such as a connection failure or too many redirects, Goose does not count it again; only the transaction log names it. A transaction that returns its own error after a failed request is in both tables: business_logic_check against a host that answers 401 puts the POST /login request in the Errors table and its Custom error in the transaction errors table.

A transaction that returns RequestCanceled, because the run ended while its request waited on the throttle, is counted as failed and records no error.

--no-error-summary stops counting errors for the Errors and transaction errors tables: the dashboard shows both tables empty, and the reports and the console summary leave them out. The logs still get every error.

Goose Eggs

Goose Eggs is a library of helpers for writing Goose load tests. Each goose-eggs release works with one Goose release, and goose-eggs 0.7 is the release for Goose 0.19. Add it to the dependencies in your Cargo.toml:

[dependencies]
goose = "^0.19"
goose-eggs = "0.7"
tokio = "^1"

goose-eggs turns on Goose’s default features, which in Goose 0.19 is the cookies feature. To build without cookies, set default-features = false on both goose and goose-eggs.

To validate a page, bring the Validate structure and either the validate_page or the validate_and_load_static_assets function into scope:

use goose_eggs::{validate_and_load_static_assets, Validate};

The following transaction requests /goose/, checks that the response has a 200 status code and that the text Gander is on the page, and, if both checks pass, loads the page’s static assets:

async fn goose_page(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let goose = user.get("/goose/").await?;

    let validate = Validate::builder()
        .status(200)
        .text("Gander")
        .build();

    validate_and_load_static_assets(user, goose, &validate).await?;

    Ok(())
}

After its checks pass, validate_and_load_static_assets requests every src="..." link and every href="..." link to a .css file on the page, when the link is a relative path or points at the load test host. The request metrics list them as GET static asset: js for a URL containing .js, GET static asset: img for any other src link, and GET static asset: css for a stylesheet. validate_page runs the same checks without loading assets. When every check passes, both return the page’s HTML as a String.

A page that fails a check is a failed request. It is counted in the request metrics and in the Errors table with the reason, and the transaction returns RequestFailed. If /goose/ answered 200 without the text Gander, the console summary would show:

 === ERRORS ===
 ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 Count       | Error
 ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 51,013        GET /goose/: http://127.0.0.1:8001/goose/: text not found on page: Gander
 ------------------------------------------------------------------------------

While the run goes on, Goose also prints an INFO line, set_failure: ..., for each failed check; -q hides them and keeps the tables.

With --debug-log, the debug log records the check that failed with the response’s headers and body.

The Validate builder

Validate::builder() builds a Validate from any of the following checks, and build() finishes it. Text and header checks add up: every one you call must pass. There is one status check, one title check and one redirect check, so a second call to status or not_status, to title or not_title, or to redirect replaces the first:

let validate = Validate::builder()
    .status(200)
    .title("Goose")
    .text("Gander")
    .not_text("Error")
    .header("content-type")
    .header_value("content-type", "text/html")
    .redirect(false)
    .build();

Use it in place of the validate in goose_page above.

MethodPasses when
status, not_statusthe response status code is, or is not, the one given
title, not_titlethe page title contains, or does not contain, the text given, ignoring case
text, not_textthe page contains, or does not contain, the text given
texts, not_textsthe page contains every text in the list, or none of them
header, not_headerthe response has, or does not have, the header given
header_value, not_header_valuethe header’s value contains, or does not contain, the text given
redirectthe request was redirected (true) or was not (false)

Validate::none() checks nothing.

New in goose-eggs 0.7

For validation, goose-eggs 0.7 changes the following from 0.6:

  • not_header_value checks the header’s value. In 0.6 it failed whenever the header was set.
  • validate_and_load_static_assets no longer loads protocol relative asset URLs on other hosts, such as //cdn.example.com/a.js.
  • header_is_set() and valid_header_value() take an http::HeaderMap in place of reqwest::header::HeaderMap. They are the same type, so code that compiled with 0.6 still compiles.
  • The default feature turns on Goose’s default features, which in Goose 0.19 is the cookies feature, and no longer picks a TLS backend. The rustls-tls feature turns on goose/rustls-tls and nothing else.

goose-eggs 0.7 also adds wordpress and woocommerce modules, with helpers for logging in, searching, commenting, posting through the REST API, and filling a WooCommerce cart and checking out. Its CHANGELOG lists every change.

Read the goose-eggs documentation for its other helpers, including the Drupal ones.

Running A Load Test

Run the load test from Creating A Load Test with Cargo. Pass the --release option whenever you compile or run a load test: a debug build is much slower and can’t generate as much load.

$ cargo run --release
    Finished `release` profile [optimized] target(s) in 0.07s
     Running `target/release/loadtest`
17:13:37 [INFO] Output verbosity level: INFO
17:13:37 [INFO] Logfile verbosity level: WARN
17:13:37 [INFO] users defaulted to number of CPUs = 18
Error: InvalidOption { option: "--host", value: "", detail: "A host must be defined via the --host option, the GooseAttack.set_default() function, or the Scenario.set_host() function (no host defined for LoadtestTransactions)." }

The load test fails because it has not been told which host to load test.

Run it again, this time passing the --host option with the address of a site you run or are allowed to load test. The -- passes the options after it to the load test rather than to Cargo. Without -u, Goose starts one user for each CPU; without -t, it runs until you stop it. This load test sets no wait time, so each user sends its next request as soon as the last one is answered. Against your own site, start small (see How many users to simulate and Specifying how long the load test will run):

$ cargo run --release -- --host https://your-site.example -u 2 -t 30s --report-file=report.html --no-reset-metrics

--report-file with a .html file extension writes an HTML report, and --no-reset-metrics keeps the ramp up in that report (explained below). Stop a run without -t with one Ctrl-C. The run below is the same command against a test server on port 19060 of this machine, without -u and -t. It ran until Ctrl-C was pressed once, about 8 seconds after entering GooseAttack phase: Maintain:

$ cargo run --release -- --host http://localhost:19060 --report-file=report.html --no-reset-metrics
    Finished `release` profile [optimized] target(s) in 0.06s
     Running `target/release/loadtest --host 'http://localhost:19060' --report-file=report.html --no-reset-metrics`
17:13:38 [INFO] Output verbosity level: INFO
17:13:38 [INFO] Logfile verbosity level: WARN
17:13:38 [INFO] users defaulted to number of CPUs = 18
17:13:38 [INFO] no_reset_metrics = true
17:13:38 [INFO] report_file = ["report.html"]
17:13:38 [INFO] global host configured: http://localhost:19060
17:13:38 [INFO] [scheduler]: allocating transactions and scenarios with RoundRobin scheduler
17:13:38 [INFO] [scheduler]: initializing 18 user states...
17:13:38 [INFO] [controller]: Telnet listening on: 127.0.0.1:5116
17:13:38 [INFO] entering GooseAttack phase: Increase
17:13:38 [INFO] [user 1]: launching user from LoadtestTransactions
17:13:39 [INFO] [user 2]: launching user from LoadtestTransactions
17:13:40 [INFO] [user 3]: launching user from LoadtestTransactions
...
17:13:54 [INFO] [user 17]: launching user from LoadtestTransactions
17:13:55 [INFO] [user 18]: launching user from LoadtestTransactions
All 18 users launched.

17:13:56 [INFO] entering GooseAttack phase: Maintain
17:14:04 [WARN] caught ctrl-c, stopping...
17:14:04 [INFO] Killswitch triggered: SIGINT received
17:14:05 [INFO] entering GooseAttack phase: Decrease
17:14:05 [INFO] [user 14]: exiting from LoadtestTransactions
17:14:05 [INFO] [user 5]: exiting from LoadtestTransactions
...
17:14:05 [INFO] html report file written to: report.html
17:14:05 [INFO] entering GooseAttack phase: Shutdown
17:14:05 [INFO] printing final metrics after 27 seconds...

All INFO and higher-level log messages are displayed on the console while the load test runs. The entering GooseAttack phase: lines name each phase the load test enters. -q (--quiet) hides the INFO lines and leaves warnings and errors, and -v (--verbose) adds low-level debug messages. In Goose 0.19, -v against a fast server can hang the run at the metrics reset; add --no-reset-metrics with it, and see When a run never ends.

Goose launches one user a second, so this machine’s 18 users took 18 seconds to ramp up; the overview below shows this as the Increasing step. With no wait time, this run’s console metrics, not shown here, report over 9,000 requests a second against the local test server.

By default, once every user has started, Goose prints the metrics so far, resets them, and prints All 18 users launched, resetting metrics (disable with --no-reset-metrics). The results then describe the run at full load. --no-reset-metrics, used here, skips that early print of the metrics and the reset, and Goose prints only All 18 users launched. The tables and graphs in report.html then include the ramp up; the User Metrics graph shows the ramp up with or without the reset.

What the run produced

Once every user has exited, Goose writes report.html in the directory you ran it from; open it in a browser. Then Goose enters Shutdown and prints its final console metrics, which end with the plan overview. The overview lists each step of the run, when it started and stopped, how long it took, and how many users were running:

 === OVERVIEW ===
 ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 Action       Started               Stopped             Elapsed    Users
 ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 Increasing:  2026-10-28 18:13:38 - 2026-10-28 18:13:56 (00:00:18, 0 -> 18)
 Maintaining: 2026-10-28 18:13:56 - 2026-10-28 18:14:05 (00:00:09, 18)
 Canceling:   2026-10-28 18:14:05 - 2026-10-28 18:14:05 (00:00:00, 0 <- 18)

 Target host: http://localhost:19060/
 goose v0.19.0
 ------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Because Ctrl-C stopped this run, the overview lists its Decrease phase as Canceling; a run that ends at its -t time lists it as Decreasing. The log lines in the run above carry UTC times; the plan overview uses local time, an hour ahead on this machine.

To watch a run live, change the goose line in Cargo.toml to goose = { version = "^0.19", features = ["dashboard"] } and run with --dashboard (see Watching a run). Give --report-file once for each report you want; its extension sets the type (see Writing reports). A .pdf report also needs the pdf-reports feature, as in features = ["dashboard", "pdf-reports"], and Chrome, which Goose downloads if it finds none (see Requirements).

Metrics shows every view of a run and when to use each, from the live dashboard through the PDF, HTML, Markdown and JSON reports to the console metrics. Its HTML report section walks through the report you just opened, and its console metrics section explains the tables printed before the plan overview.

The examples included with Goose show larger load tests.

Run-Time Options

Every Goose load test takes the run-time options below. Three of them need a crate feature: --pdf-scale and --pdf-timeout exist only when Goose is built with pdf-reports, and --dashboard is refused unless Goose is built with dashboard. -h prints a summary of each one, and --help prints the full reference: what each option does, its default, the values it takes, the options it works with or excludes, an example, and the GooseDefault that sets it in code, where they apply. To see the summary for your own load test, run cargo run --release -- -h in its folder, or cargo run --release --example simple -- -h in a checkout of Goose.

The help on this page is from Goose 0.19.0. When you run -h yourself, its first line names the version of Goose your load test was built with, and -V (--version) prints the library’s name and version, such as goose 0.19.0. The output below comes from a build with the pdf-reports feature:

https://book.goose.rs/getting-started/runtime-options.html

Help wraps to the terminal’s width, up to 120 columns. When the output is not a terminal, it wraps to COLUMNS if that is set, also up to 120, and to 80 columns otherwise. Both outputs on this page are the help at 80 columns.

Where each option is explained

The options a load test needs first are on Common Options. Every other option is explained on the page linked beside it, or here in the table.

OptionsExplained
-H/--hostCommon Options
-u/--usersCommon Options
-t/--run-time, --iterationsCommon Options
-r/--increase-rate, -s/--increase-timeCommon Options
--decrease-rate, --decrease-timeCommon Options
--test-planTest Plan
--scenarios, --scenarios-listLimiting Scenarios
--throttle-requestsThrottle
-h/--help-h prints the summary and --help the full reference, both shown on this page.
-V/--versionPrints the Goose library’s name and version, then exits.
-l/--listPrints each scenario by its display name, with its transactions and their weights, then exits without load testing; --scenarios-list prints the names --scenarios takes.
-v/--verbose, -q/--quietWithout either, the console shows info, warning and error messages while the load test runs. -v adds debug messages and -vv trace messages; -q leaves out the info messages.
-G/--goose-logWrites Goose’s own log of the run to a file, or into the run folder when given no path; Output Files names the file.
-g/--log-levelAdds info messages to the Goose log that --goose-log writes, debug messages with -gg and trace messages with -ggg. Without -g, that log holds only warnings and errors.
--running-metricsPrints the metrics so far to the console every N seconds, and during the ramp up also each time a user starts. The metrics at the end are printed too. With --no-print-metrics, neither is printed.
--no-print-metricsCommon Options
--no-error-summaryLeaves the errors and transaction errors tables out of the console metrics and the reports. The error log still records every error.
--report-file, --pdf-print-html, --pdf-scale, --pdf-timeoutCommon Options
--baseline-fileCommon Options
--output-dir, --notesCommon Options, then Output Files
--report-graph-data, --render-reportCommon Options, then Output Files
--compressOutput Files
--no-granular-reportMetrics
--no-reset-metricsKeeps the metrics from the ramp up, which Goose otherwise clears when the ramp up ends. A test plan or --iterations run never clears them, and the option is refused with either.
--no-metricsCollects no metrics, so Goose writes no report. It cannot be used with --report-file, --pdf-print-html, --threshold, --co-mitigation, or the request, transaction, scenario and error logs.
--no-transaction-metrics, --no-scenario-metrics--no-transaction-metrics leaves each transaction’s metrics out of the console metrics and the reports, and the transaction log stays empty. --no-scenario-metrics does the same for scenarios and the scenario log.
--no-status-codesLeaves the count of each HTTP status code out of the console metrics and the reports.
--co-mitigationMitigation
--threshold, --no-threshold-abort, --threshold-abort-delayThresholds
-R/--request-log, --request-format, --request-bodyRequest Log
-T/--transaction-log, --transaction-formatTransaction Log
-S/--scenario-log, --scenario-formatScenario Log
-E/--error-log, --error-formatError Log
-D/--debug-log, --debug-format, --no-debug-bodyDebug Log
--dashboardCommon Options, then Live Dashboard
--dashboard-host, --dashboard-port, --dashboard-auth-token, --dashboard-control, --dashboard-no-auth, --dashboard-max-clients, --no-percentiles-over-timeDashboard Reference
--no-autostartControllers
--no-telnet, --telnet-host, --telnet-portTelnet Controller
--websocket, --websocket-host, --websocket-portWebSocket Controller
--timeoutTips
--no-gzipBy default requests ask for gzip compressed responses, which Goose decompresses; --no-gzip stops asking.
--sticky-followWhen a request is redirected to another host, the GooseUser sends its later requests to that host too.
--accept-invalid-certsSkips TLS certificate validation, for test servers with self-signed or expired certificates.

--no-websocket is not in the help: the WebSocket Controller is off unless --websocket turns it on, and a command line from Goose 0.18 that still passes --no-websocket runs with a warning. Upgrading From 0.18 lists every option that was removed or now behaves differently.

Full reference

cargo run --release -- --help prints:

Goose 0.19.0: an HTTP(S) load testing tool written in Rust

Usage: loadtest [OPTIONS]

Use -h for a summary of each option, --help for details, defaults and examples.

Load test:
  -H, --host <URL>
          Target host, such as http://10.21.32.33

          The scheme, host and optional port each request's path is joined to.
          It overrides a host set with Scenario::set_host or GooseDefault::Host,
          and is required unless one of those gives every scenario a host. A
          request with a full URL ignores it.

          Example: -H http://localhost:8080. Set in code with
          GooseDefault::Host.

  -u, --users <N>
          Concurrent users [default: one per CPU]

          How many users to start. Without it, Goose starts one user for each
          CPU available to it. At least 1, and at least 2 with --increase-time
          or --decrease-time. Not allowed with --test-plan, which sets its own
          user counts.

          Example: -u 100. Set in code with GooseDefault::Users.

  -r, --increase-rate <RATE>
          Users started per second [default: 1]

          How fast to start users. A decimal is allowed: 0.5 starts one user
          every two seconds. The slowest rate is one user a day. Not allowed
          with --increase-time or --test-plan.

          Example: -r 10. Set in code with GooseDefault::IncreaseRate.

  -s, --increase-time <TIME>
          Time to start all users, such as 30s

          Starts the users evenly over this time, in place of --increase-rate.
          TIME is a whole number of seconds, or hours, minutes and seconds in
          that order, such as 1h30m or 2m30s. Needs at least 2 users. Not
          allowed with --increase-rate or --test-plan.

          Example: -s 2m. Set in code with GooseDefault::IncreaseTime, in
          seconds.

  -t, --run-time <TIME>
          Run this long after all users start

          How long to keep every user running once all have started; starting
          and stopping them takes extra time. Without it, the load test runs
          until it is stopped, or until --iterations completes. TIME is as for
          --increase-time. When it ends, users stop as --decrease-rate or
          --decrease-time set, otherwise all at once. Not allowed with
          --iterations or --test-plan.

          Example: -t 20m. Set in code with GooseDefault::RunTime, in seconds.

      --decrease-rate <RATE>
          Users stopped per second

          How fast to stop users at the end of the load test. A decimal is
          allowed, as for --increase-rate. Without it or --decrease-time, every
          user stops at once. Not allowed with --decrease-time, --iterations or
          --test-plan.

          Example: --decrease-rate 5. Set in code with
          GooseDefault::DecreaseRate.

      --decrease-time <TIME>
          Time to stop all users, such as 30s

          Stops the users evenly over this time at the end of the load test, in
          place of --decrease-rate. TIME is as for --increase-time. Needs at
          least 2 users. Not allowed with --decrease-rate, --iterations or
          --test-plan.

          Example: --decrease-time 1m. Set in code with
          GooseDefault::DecreaseTime, in seconds.

      --iterations <N>
          Each user runs its scenario N times

          Each user stops after running its scenario N times, and the load test
          ends once every user has stopped. 0, the default, means no limit. Not
          allowed with --run-time, --decrease-rate, --decrease-time, --test-plan
          or --no-reset-metrics.

          Example: --iterations 5. Set in code with GooseDefault::Iterations.

      --test-plan <PLAN>
          Load shape, such as "10,60s;0,30s"

          Steps separated by semicolons, each a number of users and the time to
          reach it: "10,60s;0,30s" starts 10 users over 60 seconds, then stops
          them over 30 seconds. A step that keeps the number of users holds them
          for its time. TIME is as for --increase-time. Not allowed with
          --users, --increase-rate, --increase-time, --decrease-rate,
          --decrease-time, --run-time, --iterations or --no-reset-metrics.

          Example: --test-plan "10,1m;10,5m;0,30s". Set in code with
          GooseDefault::TestPlan.

      --scenarios <LIST>
          Only run the named scenarios

          A comma separated list of scenario machine names, as --scenarios-list
          prints them. Matching ignores case, and * matches any run of
          characters. Without it, every scenario runs.

          Example: --scenarios "checkout,browse*". Set in code with
          GooseDefault::Scenarios.

      --throttle-requests <N>
          Cap total requests per second

          The most requests per second all users together may make. 0, the
          default, means no cap.

          Example: --throttle-requests 100. Set in code with
          GooseDefault::ThrottleRequests.

Information:
  -h, --help
          Print help: -h prints a summary of each option, --help this full
          reference.

  -V, --version
          Print the Goose library version

          Prints the name and version of the Goose library the load test was
          built with, then exits.

  -l, --list
          List scenarios and their transactions

          Prints each scenario and its transactions, with their weights, then
          exits without load testing.

      --scenarios-list
          List scenario names, then exit

          Prints the machine name of each scenario, which --scenarios matches,
          and its full name, then exits without load testing.

Console and Goose log:
  -v, --verbose...
          More console output (-v, -vv)

          The console shows info messages by default. -v adds debug messages and
          -vv adds trace messages. Not allowed with --quiet.

          Example: -vv. Set in code with GooseDefault::Verbose.

  -q, --quiet...
          Less console output, warnings only

          The console shows only warnings and errors, leaving out info messages;
          -qq is the same as -q. Not allowed with --verbose.

          Example: -q. Set in code with GooseDefault::Quiet.

  -G, --goose-log [<FILE>]
          Write the Goose log to FILE

          Writes Goose's own log messages to FILE, at the detail --log-level
          sets. Without FILE, writes goose.log into the run's folder. Without
          the option, there is no Goose log.

          Example: -G goose.log. Set in code with GooseDefault::GooseLog.

  -g, --log-level...
          More Goose log detail (-g, -gg)

          The Goose log that --goose-log writes holds warnings and errors by
          default. -g adds info messages, -gg debug messages and -ggg trace
          messages.

          Example: -G goose.log -gg. Set in code with GooseDefault::LogLevel.

      --running-metrics <N>
          Print running metrics every N seconds

          Prints the metrics so far to the console every N seconds while the
          load test runs. Without it, metrics are printed only at the end.

          Example: --running-metrics 30. Set in code with
          GooseDefault::RunningMetrics.

      --no-print-metrics
          Don't print metrics at the end

          Leaves the metrics out of the console when the load test ends. Reports
          are still written.

          Set in code with GooseDefault::NoPrintMetrics.

      --no-error-summary
          Don't collect the error summary

          Leaves the summary of errors out of the metrics, on the console and in
          reports. The error log, --error-log, is still written.

          Set in code with GooseDefault::NoErrorSummary.

Reports:
      --report-file [<FILE>]
          Write a report; repeatable

          Writes a report of the load test to FILE when it ends. The extension
          picks the type: .html or .htm, .md, .json, or .pdf with the
          pdf-reports feature. Without FILE, writes report.json and report.html
          into the run's folder. Repeat the option to write several. Not allowed
          with --no-metrics.

          Example: --report-file report.html --report-file report.json. Set in
          code with GooseDefault::ReportFile.

      --output-dir <DIR>
          Run folder, or goose-runs/<start time>

          Sets the run's folder for output flags given without a path. Goose
          creates it as the run starts, and removes it at the end of a run that
          wrote nothing into it. When the folder already holds files, the run
          uses DIR-2, DIR-3 and so on. Needs an output flag given without a
          path, or --dashboard, which counts except under --no-metrics.

          Example: --output-dir goose-runs/nightly --report-file. Set in code
          with GooseDefault::OutputDir.

      --compress [<LEVEL>]
          Gzip files in the run's folder, 1 to 9

          Gzips every file Goose writes into the run's folder, each named with
          .gz added. A path given to an output flag keeps exactly that name; a
          name that ends in .gz is gzipped without this option. LEVEL is 1
          (fastest) to 9 (smallest), default 6, for every gzipped file.

          Example: --report-file -R --compress 9. Set in code with
          GooseDefault::Compress.

      --notes <TEXT>
          Label the run in its reports

          Stores TEXT in the JSON report and shows it at the top of the HTML and
          Markdown reports and in the dashboard. Needs --report-file,
          --pdf-print-html or --dashboard.

          Example: --notes "baseline before cache change". Set in code with
          GooseDefault::Notes.

      --report-graph-data
          Add graph data to the JSON report

          Adds the per second data behind the HTML report's graphs to the JSON
          report, so a report rendered from it with --render-report has its
          graphs. Needs --report-file or --dashboard.

          Set in code with GooseDefault::ReportGraphData.

      --render-report <FILE>
          Write reports from a JSON report, exit

          Writes the reports named by --report-file paths from FILE, a JSON
          report, then exits without running a load test.

          Example: --render-report report.json --report-file report.html.

      --baseline-file <PATH>
          Compare against an earlier run

          PATH is a JSON report, plain or gzipped, or a folder that holds
          report.json or report.json.gz. Every report then shows how each metric
          changed from that run.

          Example: --baseline-file baseline.json. Set in code with
          GooseDefault::BaselineFile.

      --no-granular-report
          Graph only totals in reports

          Report graphs show a line for the total and, by default, one more for
          each request, transaction or scenario. This leaves out the extra
          lines. Needs --report-file, --pdf-print-html or --dashboard.

          Set in code with GooseDefault::NoGranularData.

      --pdf-print-html <FILE>
          Write print-ready HTML for PDF

          Writes the HTML report, laid out for printing, to FILE, to turn into a
          PDF in any browser. Needs neither Chrome nor the pdf-reports feature.
          Not allowed with --no-metrics.

          Example: --pdf-print-html report-print.html.

      --pdf-scale <SCALE>
          PDF scale, 0.1 to 2.0 [default: 0.8]

          How much Chrome scales the pages of a .pdf --report-file. Only with
          the pdf-reports feature.

          Example: --pdf-scale 1.2. Set in code with GooseDefault::PdfScale.

      --pdf-timeout <SECS>
          PDF timeout, 10 to 600 s [default: 60]

          Seconds to wait for Chrome to start and to render a .pdf
          --report-file. Only with the pdf-reports feature.

          Example: --pdf-timeout 120. Set in code with GooseDefault::PdfTimeout.

Metrics:
      --no-reset-metrics
          Keep metrics from the ramp up

          By default, Goose resets the metrics once every user has started, so
          they cover only the full load. This keeps what was gathered while
          users were starting. Not allowed with --test-plan or --iterations.

          Set in code with GooseDefault::NoResetMetrics.

      --no-metrics
          Don't collect any metrics

          Turns off every metric, and with them every report. Not allowed with
          --report-file, --request-log, --transaction-log, --scenario-log,
          --error-log or --co-mitigation.

          Set in code with GooseDefault::NoMetrics.

      --no-transaction-metrics
          Don't collect transaction metrics

          Leaves out the metrics for each transaction, keeping those for
          requests and scenarios.

          Set in code with GooseDefault::NoTransactionMetrics.

      --no-scenario-metrics
          Don't collect scenario metrics

          Leaves out the metrics for each scenario, keeping those for requests
          and transactions.

          Set in code with GooseDefault::NoScenarioMetrics.

      --no-status-codes
          Don't collect status code metrics

          Leaves out the count of each HTTP status code.

          Set in code with GooseDefault::NoStatusCodes.

      --co-mitigation <MODE>
          Coordinated omission mitigation

          Experimental. While the server stalls, users send fewer requests, so
          the stall is under counted. Mitigation backfills the requests each
          user would have sent at its usual cadence. MODE is average, maximum or
          minimum, the cadence to backfill at, or disabled, the default. Not
          allowed with --no-metrics.

          Example: --co-mitigation average. Set in code with
          GooseDefault::CoordinatedOmissionMitigation.

      --threshold <RULE>
          Pass/fail rule, such as p95<500ms

          RULE is <metric><op><value>, with an optional " for <request>". The
          metric is pNN with optional decimals (a response time percentile, such
          as p95 or p99.9), avg, max, error-rate (failed requests as a percent
          of all) or rps (requests per second while the run holds its users
          steady, in Maintain). The op is <, <=, > or >=. A time takes us, ms or
          s, a bare number being ms; an error-rate takes an optional %. A rule
          ending " for <request>", named as the HTML or Markdown report names
          it, judges only that request, as in "p99<2s for GET /checkout"; the
          console cuts long names short. Without it, the rule judges all
          requests together. A rule that can never pass is refused. A failing
          rule stops the run, unless --no-threshold-abort is set, and always
          fails it: execute() returns GooseError::ThresholdsFailed, so a main
          that passes it up with ? exits nonzero. Repeat the option for several
          rules; they replace any set in code. Not allowed with --no-metrics.

          Example: --threshold "p95<500ms" --threshold "error-rate<1%". Set in
          code with GooseDefault::Threshold, once for each rule.

      --no-threshold-abort
          Judge thresholds only at the end

          By default, Goose checks every --threshold once a second from when the
          run first enters Maintain, and stops the run when one fails. With this
          set, Goose judges them only when the run ends, and a failing rule
          still fails the run. An rps rule counts only the time spent holding
          users steady, as it does without this.

          Set in code with GooseDefault::NoThresholdAbort.

      --threshold-abort-delay <TIME>
          Wait before thresholds can stop the run

          Checking starts when the run first enters Maintain, holding its users
          steady, and this waits TIME more before the first check, to let a cold
          cache warm up. TIME is as for --run-time; the default is 0. It never
          changes the judgement at the end of the run. Needs --threshold, and
          not allowed with --no-threshold-abort.

          Example: --threshold-abort-delay 30s. Set in code with
          GooseDefault::ThresholdAbortDelay, in seconds.

Logs:
  -R, --request-log [<FILE>]
          Log every request to FILE

          Writes a line for each request, in --request-format. Without FILE,
          writes requests.json, .csv or .log, as --request-format sets, into the
          run's folder. Not allowed with --no-metrics.

          Example: -R requests.log. Set in code with GooseDefault::RequestLog.

      --request-format <FMT>
          Request log format

          FMT is json, the default, csv, raw or pretty.

          Example: --request-format csv. Set in code with
          GooseDefault::RequestFormat.

      --request-body
          Add request bodies to the request log

          Includes the body of each request in --request-log.

          Set in code with GooseDefault::RequestBody.

  -T, --transaction-log [<FILE>]
          Log every transaction to FILE

          Writes a line for each transaction, in --transaction-format. Without
          FILE, writes transactions.json, .csv or .log, as --transaction-format
          sets, into the run's folder. Not allowed with --no-metrics.

          Example: -T transactions.log. Set in code with
          GooseDefault::TransactionLog.

      --transaction-format <FMT>
          Transaction log format

          FMT is json, the default, csv, raw or pretty.

          Example: --transaction-format csv. Set in code with
          GooseDefault::TransactionFormat.

  -S, --scenario-log [<FILE>]
          Log every scenario to FILE

          Writes a line for each run of a scenario, in --scenario-format.
          Without FILE, writes scenarios.json, .csv or .log, as
          --scenario-format sets, into the run's folder. Not allowed with
          --no-metrics.

          Example: -S scenarios.log. Set in code with GooseDefault::ScenarioLog.

      --scenario-format <FMT>
          Scenario log format

          FMT is json, the default, csv, raw or pretty.

          Example: --scenario-format csv. Set in code with
          GooseDefault::ScenarioFormat.

  -E, --error-log [<FILE>]
          Log every error to FILE

          Writes a line for each failed request, in --error-format. Without
          FILE, writes errors.json, .csv or .log, as --error-format sets, into
          the run's folder. Not allowed with --no-metrics.

          Example: -E errors.log. Set in code with GooseDefault::ErrorLog.

      --error-format <FMT>
          Error log format

          FMT is json, the default, csv, raw or pretty.

          Example: --error-format csv. Set in code with
          GooseDefault::ErrorFormat.

  -D, --debug-log [<FILE>]
          Write the debug log to FILE

          Writes what the load test logs with GooseUser::log_debug and
          GooseUser::set_failure, with response bodies unless --no-debug-body,
          in --debug-format. Without FILE, writes debug.json, .csv or .log, as
          --debug-format sets, into the run's folder.

          Example: -D debug.log. Set in code with GooseDefault::DebugLog.

      --debug-format <FMT>
          Debug log format

          FMT is json, the default, csv, raw or pretty.

          Example: --debug-format pretty. Set in code with
          GooseDefault::DebugFormat.

      --no-debug-body
          Leave response bodies out of debug log

          Writes --debug-log without response bodies, which keeps it small.

          Set in code with GooseDefault::NoDebugBody.

Dashboard:
      --dashboard
          Serve the live web dashboard

          Serves a page that charts the load test as it runs, on 127.0.0.1 port
          5118 unless --dashboard-host or --dashboard-port say otherwise. Needs
          Goose built with the dashboard feature.

          Set in code with GooseDefault::Dashboard.

      --dashboard-host <HOST>
          Bind host [default: 127.0.0.1]

          The address the dashboard listens on, with --dashboard. An address
          that is not a loopback address needs --dashboard-auth-token or
          --dashboard-no-auth.

          Example: --dashboard-host 0.0.0.0. Set in code with
          GooseDefault::DashboardHost.

      --dashboard-port <PORT>
          Bind port [default: 5118]

          The TCP port the dashboard listens on, with --dashboard. 0 picks a
          free port, which the log shows.

          Example: --dashboard-port 8088. Set in code with
          GooseDefault::DashboardPort.

      --dashboard-auth-token <TOKEN>
          Auth token

          The token a dashboard client must send, as an Authorization: Bearer
          header or a token query parameter. Needed when --dashboard-host is not
          a loopback address, and with --dashboard-control, unless
          --dashboard-no-auth is set.

          Set in code with GooseDefault::DashboardAuthToken.

      --dashboard-control
          Allow control from the dashboard

          Lets the dashboard start, stop and quit the load test and change its
          users. Needs --dashboard, and --dashboard-auth-token or
          --dashboard-no-auth.

          Set in code with GooseDefault::DashboardControl.

      --dashboard-no-auth
          Dashboard without a token

          Allow dashboard access and control without a token. Anyone who can
          reach the port can watch the test, and with --dashboard-control start,
          stop and redirect it; on anything but loopback Goose warns at startup.
          Needs --dashboard, and can't be used with --dashboard-auth-token.

          Set in code with GooseDefault::DashboardNoAuth.

      --dashboard-max-clients <N>
          Max live clients [default: 32]

          The most dashboard pages that may follow the live stream at once.

          Example: --dashboard-max-clients 4. Set in code with
          GooseDefault::DashboardMaxClients.

      --no-percentiles-over-time
          Don't track per-second response times

          Leaves out the response time percentiles for each second behind the
          dashboard's response time heatmap, which saves memory. Also turns off
          the report's response time heatmap.

          Set in code with GooseDefault::NoPercentilesOverTime.

Controllers:
      --no-autostart
          Wait for a Controller to start the test

          Starts idle and waits for a start command from the telnet Controller,
          the WebSocket Controller with --websocket, or the dashboard with
          --dashboard-control. Needs one of them enabled.

          Set in code with GooseDefault::NoAutoStart.

      --no-telnet
          Disable the telnet Controller

          By default, Goose accepts Controller commands over telnet, on
          --telnet-host and --telnet-port.

          Set in code with GooseDefault::NoTelnet.

      --telnet-host <HOST>
          Telnet host [default: 127.0.0.1]

          The address the telnet Controller listens on. It has no
          authentication: any other address, such as 0.0.0.0, prints a warning
          at startup, as anyone who can reach the port can start, stop and
          reconfigure the load test.

          Example: --telnet-host 0.0.0.0. Set in code with
          GooseDefault::TelnetHost.

      --telnet-port <PORT>
          Telnet port [default: 5116]

          The TCP port the telnet Controller listens on.

          Example: --telnet-port 6116. Set in code with
          GooseDefault::TelnetPort.

      --websocket
          Enable the WebSocket Controller

          Accepts Controller commands over WebSocket, on --websocket-host and
          --websocket-port. Off by default: the WebSocket Controller has no
          authentication and accepts a connection from any web page, so any
          browser that can reach the port can drive the test.

          Set in code with GooseDefault::WebSocket.

      --websocket-host <HOST>
          WebSocket host [default: 127.0.0.1]

          The address the WebSocket Controller listens on, with --websocket. It
          has no authentication: any other address, such as 0.0.0.0, prints a
          warning at startup, as anyone who can reach the port can start, stop
          and reconfigure the load test.

          Example: --websocket-host 0.0.0.0. Set in code with
          GooseDefault::WebSocketHost.

      --websocket-port <PORT>
          WebSocket port [default: 5117]

          The TCP port the WebSocket Controller listens on, with --websocket.

          Example: --websocket-port 6117. Set in code with
          GooseDefault::WebSocketPort.

HTTP client:
      --timeout <SECS>
          Request timeout in seconds [default: 60]

          How long each request may take before it fails. A decimal is allowed:
          0.5 is half a second. Rounded to the millisecond, so at least 0.0005.

          Example: --timeout 15. Set in code with GooseDefault::Timeout.

      --no-gzip
          Don't send Accept-Encoding: gzip

          By default, requests ask for gzip compressed responses, which Goose
          decompresses.

          Set in code with GooseDefault::NoGzip.

      --sticky-follow
          Keep following the host's redirect

          When a request is redirected to another host, the user sends its later
          requests to that host too.

          Set in code with GooseDefault::StickyFollow.

      --accept-invalid-certs
          Accept invalid TLS certificates

          Skips TLS certificate validation, for test servers with self signed or
          expired certificates.

          Set in code with GooseDefault::AcceptInvalidCerts.

Examples:
  loadtest -H http://localhost:8080 -u 10 -r 2 -t 5m
  loadtest -H http://localhost:8080 -u 10 -r 2 -t 5m --report-file report.html

Most options can also be set in code with GooseDefault; the command line wins. A
value that begins with a hyphen goes after =, as in --goose-log=-run.log.
https://book.goose.rs/getting-started/runtime-options.html

The developer documentation describes GooseConfiguration, the structure that holds these options.

Common Run Time Options

These are the options you need in your first week of load testing: where to send the load, how many users, how fast they start, how long to run, how to watch the run, and which reports to keep. Combine them as you need. Run-Time Options lists every option and the page that explains it.

Each example other than the host example also needs --host, unless the load test sets a host itself with GooseDefault::Host or Scenario::set_host. Options that take a time, such as --run-time and --increase-time, take a whole number of seconds, or hours, minutes and seconds in that order, such as 1h30m or 2m30s.

Host to load test

A load test’s requests usually name only a path, such as /about, so Goose must be told which host to send them to. The same load test can then run against http://local.example.com, https://qa.example.com or https://www.example.com.

Host example

Load test the https://www.example.com domain.

cargo run --release -- -H https://www.example.com

How many users to simulate

By default, Goose will launch one user per available CPU core. Often you will want to simulate considerably more users than this, and this can be done by setting the --users run time option.

(Alternatively, you can use --test-plan to build both simple and more complex traffic patterns that can include a varying number of users.)

Users example

Launch 1,000 GooseUsers.

cargo run --release -- -u 1000

At the default rate of one user a second, starting 1,000 users takes over 16 minutes; the next section shows how to start them faster.

Controlling how long it takes Goose to launch all users

Goose starts users at a rate, with --increase-rate, or over a time, with --increase-time, but not both together. (These replace --hatch-rate and --startup-time from Goose 0.18 and earlier, which no longer exist; see Upgrading From 0.18.)

Both are best effort limits: Goose will not start users faster than you configure, but the machine running Goose may not be able to start them that fast.

Specifying the increase rate

By default, Goose starts one GooseUser per second. So if you configure --users to 10 it will take ten seconds to fully start the load test. If you set --increase-rate 5 then Goose will start 5 users every second, taking two seconds to start up. If you set --increase-rate 0.5 then Goose will start 1 user every 2 seconds, taking twenty seconds to start all 10 users. The slowest rate Goose accepts is one user a day, about 0.0000116; a slower rate is an error.

Goose does not round a rate that is not a whole number: --increase-rate 1.5 starts a user every 667 milliseconds, so the increase phase of 9 users lasts 6 seconds. --decrease-rate, --increase-time, --decrease-time and the steps of a test plan are not rounded either.

Increase rate example

Launch one user every two seconds.

cargo run --release -- -r .5

Specifying the total increase time

Alternatively, you can tell Goose how long you would like it to take to start all GooseUsers. So, if you configure --users to 10 and set --increase-time 10 it will launch 1 user every second. If you set --increase-time 1m it will start 1 user every 6 seconds, starting all users over one minute. And if you set --increase-time 2s it will launch five users per second, launching all users in two seconds.

Increase time example

Launch all users in 5 seconds.

cargo run --release -- -s 5

Specifying how long the load test will run

The --run-time option is not affected by how long Goose takes to start up.
