Thus, if you configure a load test with --users 100 --increase-time 30m --run-time 5m Goose will run for a total of 35 minutes, first ramping up for 30 minutes and then running at full load for 5 minutes. If you want Goose to exit immediately after all users start, you can set a very small run time, for example --users 100 --increase-rate .25 --run-time 1s.

Instead of a run time, --iterations sets how many times each GooseUser runs through its scenario before it stops, and a --test-plan sets how long each of its steps lasts.

Goose resets its metrics when the ramp up ends. Its reports then leave the ramp up out of the totals and out of every graph but the active users graph. --no-reset-metrics keeps the ramp up in. A run limited by --iterations or shaped by a test plan never resets, and refuses --no-reset-metrics.

If you do not configure a run time, Goose runs until it is canceled with ctrl-c.

Run time example

Run the load test for 30 minutes.

cargo run --release -- -t 30m

Iterations example

Each GooseUser will take as long as it takes to fully run its assigned Scenario 5 times and then stop.

cargo run --release -- --iterations 5

Watching a run

With the dashboard feature turned on in your load test’s Cargo.toml, --dashboard serves a live web dashboard while the load test runs. Open http://127.0.0.1:5118/ in a browser to see the users, requests per second, response times and errors as they change.

A read-only dashboard 2 minutes 14 seconds into a run of 50 users with --no-reset-metrics: the users chart climbs by one user a second to 50 and holds, and the requests chart climbs with it.

By default the dashboard only watches: it cannot start, stop or change the test, and its Control card says how to turn control on. With Save report on, as it is by default, Goose writes the run’s report.html and report.json into its run folder when the run ends. --dashboard-control lets the dashboard start and stop the test, change its users and settings, and, once the test is stopped, quit Goose. It also needs a token, set with --dashboard-auth-token <TOKEN> and given by opening http://127.0.0.1:5118/?token=<TOKEN>, or --dashboard-no-auth, which lets anyone who can reach the port control the test. Live Dashboard tours every part of the page, including the truncation notice.

Dashboard example

Turn on the dashboard feature, then watch a load test in the browser.

goose = { version = "^0.19", features = ["dashboard"] }

cargo run --release -- --dashboard

Writing reports

--report-file <FILE> writes a report when the load test finishes. The file’s extension sets its type: .pdf for a PDF report, when Goose is built with the pdf-reports feature; .html or .htm for HTML; .md for Markdown; and .json for JSON. Give --report-file once for each report you want. A --report-file given without a path writes report.html and report.json into the run folder. A file that already exists at the path is overwritten. Report Formats shows what each report holds. The HTML report is the easiest to start with.

PDF report example

Build with the pdf-reports feature and write a PDF report, waiting up to two minutes for Chrome.

cargo run --release --features goose/pdf-reports -- --report-file report.pdf --pdf-timeout 120

A PDF report needs Chrome or Chromium; see Requirements. --pdf-timeout is how many seconds Goose waits for Chrome to start and to render the PDF, from 10 to 600, default 60. --pdf-scale, from 0.1 to 2.0, default 0.8, sets how much Chrome scales the pages. Both exist only with the pdf-reports feature.

HTML report example

Write an HTML report to report.html when the load test finishes.

cargo run --release -- --report-file report.html

The HTML report’s requests per second graph, from a run of 50 users with --no-reset-metrics: the total climbs through the shaded ramp up and levels off once every user is running.

Printer-friendly HTML report example

Write an HTML report laid out for printing to PDF.

cargo run --release -- --pdf-print-html print-report.html

--pdf-print-html writes the HTML report laid out for printing, to turn into a PDF with your browser’s “Print to PDF” or to print on paper. It needs neither Chrome nor the pdf-reports feature, has the same graphs and response time heatmap as the HTML report, and cannot be used with --no-metrics. It can also be written beside a --report-file:

cargo run --release -- --report-file report.html --pdf-print-html print-report.html

HTML and Markdown report example

Write an HTML and a Markdown report when the load test finishes.

cargo run --release -- --report-file report.html --report-file report.md

JSON report example

Write a JSON report, which a later run can compare against with --baseline-file.

cargo run --release -- --report-file report.json

Console output

Goose prints ASCII metrics to the console when a load test finishes, whether or not it writes a report. --no-print-metrics leaves them out, and every requested report is still written.

Run folders

An output flag given without a path, such as a bare --report-file or --goose-log, writes into the run folder: goose-runs/<YYYYMMDD-HHMMSS> from the run’s local start time, unless --output-dir names one. A run watched with --dashboard saves its report there too. --notes labels the run in its reports and on the dashboard. Output Files gives the rules for every file Goose writes.

cargo run --release -- --report-file --notes "after the cache change"

When running Goose in Docker, mount a folder for the runs:

docker run -v "$PWD/goose-runs:/goose-runs" my-load-test --host https://example.com --output-dir /goose-runs/nightly --report-file

In CI, upload goose-runs/ as a build artifact to keep every run’s reports.

Comparing Against A Baseline

To see what changed between two runs, such as before and after a deployment, give the later run an earlier run’s JSON report with --baseline-file. A run folder that holds a report.json works too, such as the one a --dashboard run or a bare --report-file writes:

cargo run --release -- --report-file --baseline-file goose-runs/20261028-120502

The numeric metrics in the PDF, HTML and Markdown reports then include a delta showing the difference from the baseline. For example, instead of just showing 1,234 requests, the HTML report shows 1,234 (+200) if the current run had 200 more requests than the baseline, or 1,234 (-50) if it had 50 fewer; the Markdown report shows its request counts without thousands separators, 1234 (+200). The JSON report holds only the current run’s values, so it can be the next run’s baseline. The console metrics show no deltas.

Deltas are calculated for:

  • Requests: number of requests, failures, average/min/max response time, requests/failures per second
  • Response time percentiles: p50, p60, p70, p80, p90, p95, p99, p100
  • Transactions: request count, failures, response times, rates
  • Scenarios: user count, iteration count, response times, rates
  • Errors: occurrence count
  • Transaction errors: occurrence count
  • Coordinated Omission: adjusted response times and standard deviation

Metrics are matched between runs by their key (HTTP method + endpoint name for requests, transaction + name for transactions, etc.). A request in the current run but not in the baseline is shown without a delta, and one only in the baseline is not shown.

The baseline must be a JSON report generated by Goose, plain or gzipped (.json.gz), or a run folder, whose report.json or report.json.gz is then used. If the file does not exist, contains invalid JSON, or has no request metrics, Goose will report an error. A load test can also set a default baseline in code with GooseDefault::BaselineFile, which --baseline-file overrides.

Baseline comparison example

Run a load test comparing against a previous run, generating both HTML and JSON reports.

cargo run --release -- --report-file report.html --report-file latest.json --baseline-file baseline.json

The resulting latest.json can then be used as the baseline for the next comparison, enabling a chain of run-over-run comparisons.

Baselines from older Goose releases

A JSON report from Goose 0.18.1 or later loads as a baseline, including one from a run that received more than one status code. A baseline without transaction errors, such as one from Goose 0.18.1, still loads, and the run’s transaction errors then show no delta.

A time’s delta is the current time minus the baseline time, each as the report prints it. A 0.19 baseline’s request, transaction and scenario times come from its microsecond timing data. A report from an older Goose holds whole milliseconds: its minimums and maximums are truncated, and its percentiles above 100 ms are rounded to 10 ms, 100 ms or whole seconds. So an unchanged time can read differently against it, such as 38 (+1).

Controlling the ramp-down rate

You can configure how quickly Goose stops users at the end of a load test with --decrease-rate or --decrease-time. These are mutually exclusive, just like their increase counterparts, and --decrease-rate has the same slowest rate of one user a day. If neither is set, Goose stops all users at once.

Decrease rate example

Stop one user every two seconds.

cargo run --release -- --decrease-rate .5

Decrease time example

Spread the stops over 30 seconds.

cargo run --release -- --decrease-time 30s

Goose divides the time by the number of users and stops one user each interval, the first at once, so with 4 users it stops one every 7.5 seconds and the last 22.5 seconds in.

Graph data and memory

Goose keeps per second graph data in memory until the run ends whenever it will write a report then: for a --report-file path, for --pdf-print-html, and while Save report is on, as it is with --dashboard or a bare --report-file. It holds every second of the run, the ramp up included. After the metrics reset, Goose keeps zeros in memory for the ramp up seconds of the request, error, response time, transaction and scenario data, and the report leaves those seconds out of its graphs. When Save report is turned on mid run, the data starts up to 10 minutes before that moment. The graph data costs about 12 bytes per request name per second, and 4 bytes more per second for each name that has failed. Goose holds it in buffers that grow by doubling, so it can take up to twice that.

Writing an HTML report costs far more while it is written: about 300 bytes more per request name for each second of the run. --pdf-print-html builds the same HTML and costs the same. A PDF report costs about three times as much in Goose, about 950 bytes per request name for each second of the run. Chrome needs memory of its own to print it, from about 400 MB for a small report to about 750 MB for a 20 minute run with 100 request names. A Markdown or JSON report adds almost nothing. With 20 and 100 request names and none failing, counting the doubling:

run length20 names, graph data20 names, writing HTML100 names, graph data100 names, writing HTML
10 minutes0.3 MB4 MB1.3 MB20 MB
1 hour1.1 MB24 MB5 MB114 MB
24 hours35 MB570 MB160 MB2.8 GB
7 days280 MB4 GB1.3 GB20 GB

The per second costs were measured on 10 to 35 minute runs of Goose 0.19, and every row is computed from them. The table leaves out the response time heatmap, which stops growing once its columns fill, after about 10 minutes. At 100 request names it takes about 5 MB when response times are fast and even, 10 MB when they are typical, and up to about 40 MB when they spread widely, or more with a --timeout over 60 seconds; --no-percentiles-over-time turns it off.

To keep the HTML cost out of a long soak test, write a JSON report with its graph data, --report-file report.json --report-graph-data, and turn it into an HTML report after the run with --render-report report.json --report-file report.html. The render needs more memory than writing the HTML during the run would, about 1.3 times as much for a 10 minute run and more for shorter ones, but it no longer competes with the load test, and it can run later or on another machine. Output Files gives the rules.

Without a report, as on a --dashboard run with Save report off, Goose keeps only the last 10 minutes of per second graph data in memory; the dashboard’s charts show the last 5 minutes of it. On a long soak test with --dashboard-control, no --report-file path and no --pdf-print-html, turn Save report off to keep memory flat, and turn it on before the part of the run you want graphed.

Other options

When you need more than this page, these come next:

  • Test Plan: a load shape of steps, such as a ramp up, a hold and a spike.
  • Throttle: a cap on requests per second.
  • Thresholds: pass and fail rules on the metrics.
  • Limiting Scenarios: running only some of a load test’s scenarios.
  • Logging: the request, transaction, scenario, error and debug logs.

Test Plan

A load test that ramps up to full strength and then runs for a set amount of time can be configured by combining the --increase-time or --increase-rate options together with the --users and --run-time options. To also configure a ramp-down, use --decrease-time or --decrease-rate. For more complex load patterns you must instead use the --test-plan option.

A test plan is defined as a series of numerical pairs that each defines a number of users, and the amount of time to ramp to this number of users. For example, 10,60s means “launch 10 users over 60 seconds”. By stringing together multiple pairs separated by a semicolon you can define more complex test plans. For example, 10,1m;10,5m;0,0s means “launch 10 users over 1 minute, continue with 10 users for 5 minutes, then shut down the load test as quickly as possible”.

The amount of time can be defined in seconds (e.g. 10,5s), minutes (e.g. 10,15m) or hours (e.g. 10,1h), or a mix of them in that order (e.g. 10,1h30m). A bare number is seconds (e.g. 10,90), and a step with no time (e.g. 0,) takes 0 seconds. The units take either case, and nothing else is allowed, so 10,15mins is refused. White space around each number is ignored.

A test plan sets every user count and time itself, so --test-plan is not allowed with --users, --increase-rate, --increase-time, --decrease-rate, --decrease-time, --run-time or --iterations. A test plan never resets metrics, so --no-reset-metrics is refused with it too. Goose refuses a test plan before any user starts when a step does not parse, when the plan never starts a user (such as 0,10s), when its users add up to more than fit in a usize, or when a time is too long to count in milliseconds. This sample runs the simple example from a Goose checkout:

% cargo run --release --example simple -- --test-plan "0,10s"
ERROR: invalid `configuration.test_plan` value: '0,10s': `configuration.test_plan` must start at least one user, and no more than fit in a usize.
error: invalid value '0,10s' for '--test-plan <PLAN>': GooseError: invalid option or value specified

For more information, try '--help'.

A test plan can also be set in code with GooseDefault::TestPlan, which refuses the same plans. Thresholds are checked from the first step that holds its users steady, then in every later step except a ramp down.

Running A Test Plan From The Dashboard Or A Controller

The live dashboard and both Controllers show a running test plan and can set one: the dashboard through its control API, the Controllers with a command.

The live dashboard

With --dashboard, the Active users chart draws each step of the plan as it runs. This run used --test-plan "20,20s;20,40s;60,20s;60,40s;20,20s;20,60s;0,0s":

Each step with users shows as a ramp or a hold: to 20 over 20 seconds, 20 for 40, to 60 over 20, 60 for 40, down to 20 over 20, then 20 for 60, the step still running when the image was taken at 190 seconds; the plan’s last step, 0,0s, would stop every user at 200 seconds.

With control off, the header names the plan: test plan: 7 steps, peak 60 users, 3 min 20 s total.

With control on, the Control card’s summary names the host, then the plan, and the Target row is read only while the plan runs, with the reason The test plan sets users; edit the plan, or stop and switch mode. To switch mode, stop the run and set a user count: that replaces the plan with a users ramp. The settings form has no test plan field. To set a plan, send it as test_plan to POST /api/v1/control/settings, or to POST /api/v1/control/start to set it and start in one step. If Goose was started with -H http://localhost:18888 --no-autostart --dashboard --dashboard-control --dashboard-no-auth, this sets a plan and starts it:

curl -X POST -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"test_plan":"10,10s;10,20s;0,0s"}' \
  http://127.0.0.1:5118/api/v1/control/start

{"ok":true,"command":"start","message":"load test started","phase":"increase","active_users":0,"target_users":10,"settings":{"host":"http://localhost:18888","hosts":["http://localhost:18888/"],"users":null,"increase_rate":null,"increase_time_secs":null,"decrease_rate":null,"decrease_time_secs":null,"run_time_secs":null,"test_plan":"10,10;10,20;0,0","hold_ends_in_secs":null,"stage":"test_plan"}}

The reply names the plan in canonical form, each step’s time in whole seconds. Run settings gives the rules for a plan sent to a running test.

Telnet and WebSocket

The test-plan command sets a plan, and start runs it. Here Goose runs with -H http://localhost:18888 --no-autostart, -H naming the target, which a start needs when the load test sets no default host. users is refused while the plan runs:

% telnet localhost 5116
goose> test-plan 10,10s;10,20s;0,0s
test-plan configured
goose> start
load test started
goose> users 20
failed to reconfigure users, the test plan sets users: edit the plan, or stop and switch mode
goose> stop
load test stopped
goose> shutdown
load test shut down

The WebSocket Controller takes the same command as JSON:

{"request":"test-plan 10,10s;10,20s;0,0s"}
{"response":"test-plan configured","success":true}

config and config-json show the plan Goose is running, each step’s time in milliseconds: on telnet config in Rust’s debug form, TestPlan { steps: [(10, 10000), (10, 20000), (0, 0)], current: 0 } over several lines, and config-json, and both commands on WebSocket, as "test_plan":{"steps":[[10,10000],[10,20000],[0,0]],"current":0}. A plan sent to a running test replaces the one running, starting from its first step at the current user count. The Controller commands give the bounds a plan must keep.

Simple Example

The following command tells Goose to start 10 users over 60 seconds and then to run for 5 minutes before shutting down:

$ cargo run --release -- -H http://local.dev/ --increase-time 1m --users 10 --run-time 5m --no-reset-metrics

The exact same behaviour can be defined with the following test plan:

$ cargo run --release -- -H http://local.dev/ --test-plan "10,1m;10,5m;0,0s"

Ramp Down Example

Unless --decrease-rate or --decrease-time is set, Goose stops a load test as quickly as it can when the specified --run-time completes. A test plan can also ramp down slowly. In the following example, Goose starts 1000 users in 2 minutes and then slowly stops them over 500 seconds (stopping 2 users per second):

$ cargo run --release -- -H http://local.dev/ --test-plan "1000,2m;0,500s"

Load Spike Example

Another possibility when specifying a test plan is to add load spikes into otherwise steady load. For example, in the following example Goose starts 500 users over 5 minutes and lets it run with a couple of traffic spikes to 2,500 users:

$ cargo run --release -- -H http://local.dev/ --test-plan "500,5m;500,5m;2500,45s;500,45s;500,5m;2500,45s;500,45s;500,5m;0,0s"

This graph comes from the simple example run with that test plan against a local server. Each step took the time it names: the first step started a user every 600 milliseconds and reached 500 users after 5 minutes (see Specifying the increase rate).

Internals

Internally, Goose converts the test plan into a vector of usize tuples, Vec<(usize, usize)>, where the first integer reflects the number of users to be running and the second integer reflects the time in milliseconds. You can see the internal representation when you start a load test, for example as follows. With --no-autostart the load test waits to be started, so press ctrl-c once the line is printed:

% cargo run --release --example simple -- --no-autostart --test-plan "100,30s;100,1h" | grep test_plan
21:00:10 [INFO] test_plan = TestPlan { steps: [(100, 30000), (100, 3600000)], current: 0 }

Throttling Requests

By default, Goose will generate as much load as it can. If this is not desirable, the throttle allows optionally limiting the maximum number of requests per second made during a load test. This can be helpful to ensure consistency when running a load test from multiple different servers with different available resources.

The throttle is specified as an integer and imposes a maximum number of requests, not a minimum number of requests. 0, the default, means no throttle. Set it in code with GooseDefault::ThrottleRequests.

An rps threshold that the throttle keeps the run from reaching is refused before the load test starts. --throttle-requests 0 turns the throttle off, so it refuses none.

Example

In this example, Goose will launch 100 GooseUser threads, but the throttle will prevent them from generating a combined total of more than 5 requests per second.

$ cargo run --release -- -H http://local.dev/ -u100 -r20 --throttle-requests 5

Thresholds

A threshold is a pass/fail rule on a load test’s metrics, such as “the 95th percentile response time stays under 500 ms” or “under 1% of requests fail”. Give one with --threshold, as often as needed. A rule that fails stops the run by default, and always makes the run fail: GooseAttack::execute() returns an error once every report is written (see Exit code in CI). Every report says which rules passed and which failed.

cargo run --release -- -H http://local.dev/ -u 50 -r 10 -t 10m \
    --threshold "p95<500ms" \
    --threshold "error-rate<1%" \
    --threshold "p99<2s for GET /checkout"

Quote each rule: < and > redirect in a shell.

You can set as many thresholds as you like. Goose checks them all once a second while the run goes, stops the run as soon as one fails, and checks them all again at the end; see Stopping the run. Each threshold covers the whole run, not just part of it. Thresholds are meant for a simple load test, one that ramps up, holds a single user count, and ramps down. To check several user counts, run a separate load test for each one. For how rps is counted, see rps counts only the time users hold steady.

Syntax

A rule is <metric><op><value>, optionally followed by for <request>.

MetricMeasuresValue
pNNA response time percentile, with optional decimals, such as p50, p95 or p99.9, greater than 0 and less than 100A time: a number with us, ms or s; a bare number is milliseconds
avgThe average response timeA time
maxThe slowest response timeA time
error-rateFailed requests as a percent of all requestsA number with an optional %, at most 100
rpsRequests per second while the run holds its users steadyA number

The operator is <, <=, > or >=. A rule passes when the comparison holds for what Goose observed: p95<500ms passes at 499 ms and fails at 500 ms, while p95<=500ms passes at 500 ms too.

Without for <request>, a rule judges all requests together, as the Aggregated row of the report’s tables does. With it, the rule judges only the request with that name: its full name as the HTML or Markdown report shows it, method and name together, such as GET /login, where the name is the path or the request’s custom name, as in POST login. The console’s tables cut long names short, so copy a name from a report. When the run ends, Goose warns about a rule for a request the run never made, suggesting the name it most likely means:

threshold p95<1s for get /: no request named "get /"; did you mean "GET /"?

Some rules:

  • p95<500ms: 95% of all responses take less than 500 ms.
  • p99.9<=2s: 99.9% of all responses take at most 2 seconds.
  • avg<150: the average response is under 150 ms.
  • error-rate<1%: fewer than 1% of requests fail.
  • rps>=200: the run makes at least 200 requests a second while it holds its users steady.
  • max<5s for GET /checkout: no GET /checkout takes 5 seconds or more.

A rule that does not parse is refused before any user starts, with a GooseError::InvalidOption whose detail says what is wrong with it:

threshold "p100<1s": the percentile must be greater than 0 and less than 100, such as p95 or p99.9.

So is a rule that can never pass: any rule with <0, such as p95<0 or error-rate<0, as well as rps<=0 and error-rate>100 (with or without %). With --throttle-requests N, which keeps the run at N requests a second or fewer, rps>M is refused when M is N or more, and rps>=M when M is more than N:

threshold "rps>=200": it can never pass; `configuration.throttle_requests` caps the run at 100 requests a second.

--throttle-requests 0 means no throttle, so it refuses no rps rule.

Thresholds judge metrics, so --threshold is not allowed with --no-metrics.

A percentile comes from the same histogram buckets as every report’s percentiles, so it can be up to 0.78% away from the exact time, while the average and the slowest time are exact.

Stopping the run

By default Goose checks every rule once a second while the load test runs, and the first rule that fails stops the run through the killswitch, with a reason that names the rule and what was observed. In this run of the simple example, a local test server, not part of Goose, answered each request in 1.5 seconds until 12 seconds after the first request it received, then failed every request:

cargo run --release --example simple -- -H http://127.0.0.1:19862 -u 50 -r 50 -t 60s \
    --threshold "p95<2s" --threshold "error-rate<1%" --no-telnet

Once 1% or more of requests had failed, the next check stopped the run, 12 seconds after the run entered Maintain:

19:44:39 [INFO] Killswitch triggered: threshold error-rate<1% failed: observed 2.54%

A threshold failure is a killswitch stop, and works as any other killswitch does. The run then fails, as below.

With --no-threshold-abort, Goose never stops the run for a threshold. It runs its full time, and judges every rule only at the end.

When checking starts and stops

Checking starts when the run first enters Maintain, the first time it holds its users steady, in every mode. A ramp up therefore never fails a rule while the run goes. A users ramp resets its metrics as it enters Maintain. Under --test-plan, --iterations or --no-reset-metrics they are not reset, so the ramp up’s requests still count in the first check, except in rps, which counts only the hold.

Once checking starts, every later Increase or Maintain step of a test plan is checked too. Checking pauses while the run stops users, in Decrease, and ends at Shutdown. The judgement at the end still counts every request since the metrics reset, or since the start of the run when there is none, including those made in Decrease. rps is the exception, below.

A test plan that never holds its users steady, such as 10,1m;0,1m, never enters Maintain, so its rules are judged only at the end, and its rps rules can not pass. Goose warns about this at startup, unless --no-threshold-abort is set and no rule is an rps rule.

A rule whose metric has no samples yet, such as a rule for a request that has not run, is skipped while the run goes. The first check can judge only a few requests, so one early error can fail error-rate. The first seconds of the first hold can also fail an rps>= rule that the whole hold meets. --threshold-abort-delay gives the run time to make more requests before the first check.

rps counts only the time users hold steady

An rps rule counts only the time the run spends holding its users steady, in Maintain. This is true in every mode, both in the judgement at the end and in any checks while the run goes. It counts the requests made while the run holds its users, over the seconds it holds them, added up over every hold of a test plan. Holds at different user counts therefore give one rate, and an rps>= rule judges that one rate. An rps rule’s row gives the rate followed by from Maintain. A ramp up, a ramp down, and an Increase step between two holds add nothing, so none of them pulls the rate down. The report’s Aggregated requests per second can differ: it covers every second since the metrics reset. A run under --test-plan, --iterations or --no-reset-metrics has no reset, so there the ramp up’s lower rate pulls that figure down, and an rps>= rule judged on it would fail although the steady load meets it.

A test plan step that stays at 0 users after a ramp down and before a later ramp up is a hold too: it adds seconds and no requests, so it pulls the rate down. In 10,10s;10,20s;0,5s;0,20s;10,10s;10,20s;0,5s, that step is 0,20s. Leave such a step out of a test plan with an rps rule: without 0,20s, the ramp down leads straight into the next ramp up, and the rate covers only the two holds at 10 users.

--iterations is refused at startup with --decrease-rate or --decrease-time, as a ramp down would stop users before they finish their iterations: the error reads `configuration.decrease_rate` can not be set with `configuration.iterations`., or the same for decrease_time. With --iterations, the run enters Maintain when the ramp up ends and stays there until the last user finishes its iterations. A run whose users all finish before the ramp up ends never enters Maintain. Once the first user finishes, the rest of the hold has fewer users making requests. That time still counts, so it pulls the rate down. A run with --run-time in place of --iterations keeps every user making requests until the hold ends, so use --run-time with an rps rule.

Until the run has held its users for a whole second there is no rate to judge. A run that never enters Maintain has no time to count, so its rps rules fail at the end as no samples.

--threshold-abort-delay

--threshold-abort-delay holds the first check back by a time after the run first enters Maintain, given as for --run-time: 30s, 2m, or a bare number of seconds. It never changes the judgement at the end of the run.

Use it when the first seconds of a run are slow for a reason you accept, such as a cold cache. With --threshold-abort-delay 1m, a warm up spike in the first minute after the run enters Maintain can not stop the run, but it still counts in the final percentiles, so a rule it breaks in the final metrics still fails at the end:

cargo run --release -- -H http://local.dev/ -u 50 -r 50 -t 10m \
    --threshold "p95<500ms" --threshold-abort-delay 1m

It needs a --threshold, and is not allowed with --no-threshold-abort, which never stops the run.

The end of the run

When the run ends, Goose judges every rule once more on the final metrics. Here a rule whose metric has no samples fails, observed as no samples: a rule for a request that never ran says nothing about that request, so it can not pass.

The rule that stopped the run always fails. Requests that finish after it stopped, and any the check had not counted yet, are counted afterwards, so by the end its value can be higher or lower than when it stopped the run. Its row shows the value at the stop and at the end, such as 2.54% at abort, 6.45% at end in the run under Stopping the run, or one value when the two are the same.

When every rule passes, execute() returns Ok(GooseMetrics) as usual. When any fails, it returns GooseError::ThresholdsFailed once every report is written. The error holds the rules that failed and the run’s metrics, as a ThresholdMetrics that reads as the GooseMetrics it holds:

match goose_attack.execute().await {
    Ok(metrics) => println!("all {} thresholds passed", metrics.thresholds.len()),
    Err(GooseError::ThresholdsFailed { failed, metrics }) => {
        for result in &failed {
            eprintln!("{} failed: observed {}", result.rule, result.observed);
        }
        eprintln!("the run lasted {} seconds", metrics.duration);
        std::process::exit(1);
    }
    Err(e) => return Err(e),
}

GooseMetrics::thresholds holds every rule’s ThresholdResult in either case: its rule, what was observed, as text and as a number, whether it passed, and whether it stopped the run.

Exit code in CI

A load test whose main returns Result<(), GooseError> and uses ? on execute(), as every example does, exits with code 1 when a threshold fails, so a CI step fails with it. This step runs the simple example from a Goose checkout; in your own load test crate, use cargo run --release -- instead:

- name: Load test
  run: |
    cargo run --release --example simple -- \
      --host http://staging.example.com --users 20 --increase-rate 5 --run-time 5m \
      --threshold "p95<500ms" --threshold "error-rate<1%" \
      --report-file report.html

The console output of the run under Stopping the run ends with the rule that failed, then the error main returns, before Goose exits with code 1:

19:44:40 [ERROR] threshold error-rate<1% failed: observed 2.54% at abort, 6.45% at end
Error: ThresholdsFailed { failed: [ThresholdResult { rule: "error-rate<1%", observed: "2.54% at abort, 6.45% at end", value: Some(6.451612903225806), passed: false, aborted: true }], metrics: ThresholdMetrics { .. } }

Reports

The PDF, HTML and Markdown reports have a Thresholds section with a row for each rule: the rule, what was observed, and the result. A failing row says FAIL, and FAIL, stopped the run for the rule that stopped it, so the result never rests on color alone. The JSON report keeps each result as an object in raw_metrics.thresholds, with rule, observed, value, passed and aborted, so a report rendered from it with --render-report shows the same section. Without thresholds there is no section. The console’s final tables have the same section; the run under Stopping the run printed:

 === THRESHOLDS ===
 ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 Rule                                         |                     Observed | Result
 ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 p95<2s                                       |                       1499ms | PASS
 error-rate<1%                                | 2.54% at abort, 6.45% at end | FAIL, stopped the run
 ------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Setting thresholds in code

Set rules in code with GooseDefault::Threshold. Each call adds one rule, so code can set several. A rule that does not parse is refused by set_default itself:

use goose::prelude::*;

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), GooseError> {
    GooseAttack::initialize()?
        .set_default(GooseDefault::Threshold, "p95<500ms")?
        .set_default(GooseDefault::Threshold, "error-rate<1%")?
        // Judge them only at the end of the run.
        .set_default(GooseDefault::NoThresholdAbort, true)?;

    Ok(())
}

Rules given with --threshold replace every rule set in code; they are not added to them. GooseDefault::ThresholdAbortDelay sets the delay, in seconds.

Overhead

Thresholds do no work per request: Goose judges each rule against the metrics it already keeps, once a second while it checks (see When checking starts and stops) and once at the end, or only at the end under --no-threshold-abort.

Killswitch

Goose provides a killswitch mechanism to programmatically stop a load test when specific conditions are met. This is useful for protecting your systems and ensuring tests stop automatically when problems are detected.

To stop a run when a metric crosses a limit, such as a percentile response time or the error rate, use a threshold instead of writing the check.

When the killswitch is triggered, Goose logs Killswitch triggered: with the reason and stops the run as for Ctrl-C: users stop, test_stop runs, every report is written, and the run is recorded as canceled with the reason given. Under --no-autostart too, a killswitch stop then shuts Goose down rather than returning it to Idle, though a connected dashboard can keep it up until Quit, as at the end of an autostarted run. Only the first reason is kept: later calls change nothing until Goose clears the killswitch as the next run starts.

A killswitch fired by your own code, as below, is not an error in itself: execute() returns Ok with the metrics of the run so far, unless a threshold fails when the run ends.

Common Use Cases

  • Health Check Integration: Monitor system health endpoints and stop on failure
  • Resource Exhaustion Detection: Stop when detecting connection pool or memory issues
  • Sitemap Traversal Completion: Stop after fully crawling a site’s pages
  • Data Set Processing: Stop when all test data has been consumed
  • External Signal Integration: Stop based on monitoring system alerts

Example: Service Unavailable Detection

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
use goose::prelude::*;

async fn check_availability(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let goose = user.get("/api/endpoint").await?;

    // Stop the test if server returns 503 Service Unavailable
    if let Ok(response) = goose.response {
        if response.status() == 503 {
            goose::trigger_killswitch("Server returned 503: Service Unavailable");
        }
    }

    Ok(())
}
}

You can also check if the killswitch has been triggered using goose::is_killswitch_triggered() to conditionally execute cleanup code or skip certain operations.

goose::reset_killswitch() clears it. Goose calls it itself as each run starts. A test suite that runs several load tests in one process can call it after a test that triggered the killswitch, so the tests after it are not canceled.

Limiting Which Scenarios Run

It can often be useful to run only a subset of the Scenarios defined by a load test. Instead of commenting them out in the source code and recompiling, the --scenarios run-time option allows you to dynamically control which Scenarios are running.

Listing Scenarios By Machine Name

To ensure that each scenario has a unique name, you must use the machine name of the scenario when filtering which are running. For example, using the Umami example enable the --scenarios-list flag:

% cargo run --release --example umami -- --scenarios-list
    Finished `release` profile [optimized] target(s) in 0.22s
     Running `target/release/examples/umami --scenarios-list`
21:02:23 [INFO] Output verbosity level: INFO
21:02:23 [INFO] Logfile verbosity level: WARN
21:02:23 [INFO] users defaulted to number of CPUs = 18
Scenarios:
 - adminuser: ("Admin user")
 - anonymousenglishuser: ("Anonymous English user")
 - anonymousspanishuser: ("Anonymous Spanish user")

What Is A Machine Name: A Scenario can have any name, and several Scenarios can share one. A machine name ensures that you can still identify each Scenario uniquely, and without any special characters that can be difficult or insecure to pass through the command line. A machine name is made up of only the ASCII letters and digits found in your Scenario’s full name, in lower case, and optionally with _ and a number appended to differentiate between multiple Scenarios that would otherwise have the same name.

In the following example, we have three very similarly named Scenarios. One simply has an extra white space between words. The second has an airplane emoticon in the name. Both the extra space and the airplane symbol are stripped away from the machine name as they are not alphanumerics, and instead _1 and _2 are appended to the end to differentiate:

Scenarios:
- loadtesttransactions: ("LoadtestTransactions")
- loadtesttransactions_1: ("Loadtest Transactions")
- loadtesttransactions_2: ("LoadtestTransactions ✈️")

Running Scenarios By Machine Name

To run a subset of the above scenarios, pass a comma separated list of machine names with the --scenarios run time option. Goose uses exact matching by default: admin does not run adminuser. To match multiple scenarios with a single pattern, use the * wildcard character which matches zero or more characters. Matching ignores case and the white space around each name, so AdminUser runs adminuser. Set the list in code with GooseDefault::Scenarios.

For example, to run only the two anonymous Scenarios, you could add --scenarios "anon*":

% cargo run --release --example umami -- --increase-rate 10 --scenarios "anon*"
    Finished `release` profile [optimized] target(s) in 0.11s
     Running `target/release/examples/umami --increase-rate 10 --scenarios 'anon*'`
21:02:35 [INFO] Output verbosity level: INFO
21:02:35 [INFO] Logfile verbosity level: WARN
21:02:35 [INFO] users defaulted to number of CPUs = 18
21:02:35 [INFO] increase_rate = 10.0
21:02:35 [INFO] scenarios = Scenarios { active: ["anon*"] }

Or, to run only the “Anonymous Spanish user” and “Admin user” Scenarios using their exact machine names, you could add --scenarios "anonymousspanishuser,adminuser":

% cargo run --release --example umami -- --increase-rate 10 --scenarios "anonymousspanishuser,adminuser"
21:02:26 [INFO] scenarios = Scenarios { active: ["anonymousspanishuser", "adminuser"] }

You can also mix exact and wildcard matching: --scenarios "adminuser,anon*" would run the exact adminuser scenario plus all scenarios starting with anon.

A name may hold only letters, digits, _ and *. Anything else is refused before the load test starts, with the reason:

% cargo run --release --example umami -- --scenarios "admin-user"
error: invalid value 'admin-user' for '--scenarios <LIST>': 'admin-user' is not a scenario name: use letters, digits and _, with * as a wildcard; --scenarios-list prints the names.

For more information, try '--help'.

A machine name with a _ suffix, such as loadtesttransactions_1 above, selects that one scenario. For example, a load test that registers two scenarios both named Visitor lists them as visitor and visitor_1, and --scenarios visitor_1 runs only the second:

% cargo run --release -- --host http://127.0.0.1:8080 -u2 -r2 -t2s --scenarios visitor_1
...
 === PER SCENARIO METRICS ===
 ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 Name                     |  # users |  # times run | scenarios/s | iterations
 ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 1: Visitor               |        0 |            0 |        0.00 |       0.00
 2: Visitor               |        2 |       115156 |       57578 |      57578
 -------------------------+----------+--------------+-------------+------------
 Aggregated               |        2 |       115156 |       57578 |      57578

The final summary still lists the first Visitor scenario, with no users.

A name that matches no scenario logs a warning, as it is most likely mistyped, and is otherwise ignored as long as another name in the list matches one. When nothing in the list matches any scenario, Goose prints the scenarios and stops with an error:

% cargo run --release --example umami -- --scenarios "nosuch"
19:55:15 [INFO] scenarios = Scenarios { active: ["nosuch"] }
19:55:15 [WARN] --scenarios "nosuch" matches no scenario; --scenarios-list prints the names
Scenarios:
 - adminuser: ("Admin user")
 - anonymousenglishuser: ("Anonymous English user")
 - anonymousspanishuser: ("Anonymous Spanish user")
Error: NoScenarios { detail: "No scenarios are enabled." }

When the load test completes, you can refer to the Scenario metrics to confirm which Scenarios were enabled, and which were not.

Custom Run Time Options

A load test can add run-time options of its own. GooseAttack::initialize() parses the command line itself, with clap, and exits with an error when it receives an option it does not know. There are two alternatives here: environment variables, or parsing the command line yourself and handing Goose its part.

Environment Variables

One option is to use environment variables. An example of this can be found in the Umami example which uses environment variables to allow the configuration of a custom username and password.

Alternatively, you can use this method to set configurable custom defaults. The earlier example can be enhanced to use an environment variable to set a custom default hostname:

use goose::prelude::*;

async fn loadtest_index(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let _goose_metrics = user.get("").await?;

    Ok(())
}
#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), GooseError> {
    // Get optional custom default hostname from `HOST` environment variable.
    let custom_host = match std::env::var("HOST") {
        Ok(host) => host,
        Err(_) => "".to_string(),
    };

    GooseAttack::initialize()?
        .register_scenario(scenario!("LoadtestTransactions")
            .register_transaction(transaction!(loadtest_index))
        )
        // Set optional custom default hostname.
        .set_default(GooseDefault::Host, custom_host.as_str())?
        .execute()
        .await?;

    Ok(())
}

This can now be used to set a custom default for the scenario, in this example with no --host set Goose will execute a load test against the hostname defined in HOST:

% HOST="https://local.dev/" cargo run --release
    Finished `release` profile [optimized] target(s) in 0.07s
     Running `target/release/loadtest`
21:04:16 [INFO] Output verbosity level: INFO
21:04:16 [INFO] Logfile verbosity level: WARN
21:04:16 [INFO] users defaulted to number of CPUs = 18
21:04:16 [INFO] host for LoadtestTransactions configured: https://local.dev/

It’s still possible to override this custom default from the command line with standard Goose options, for example here the load test will run against the hostname configured by the --host option:

% HOST="http://local.dev/" cargo run --release -- --host https://example.com/
    Finished `release` profile [optimized] target(s) in 0.07s
     Running `target/release/loadtest --host 'https://example.com/'`
21:04:19 [INFO] Output verbosity level: INFO
21:04:19 [INFO] Logfile verbosity level: WARN
21:04:19 [INFO] users defaulted to number of CPUs = 18
21:04:19 [INFO] global host configured: https://example.com/

If the HOST variable and the --host option are not set, Goose will display the expected error:

% cargo run --release
     Running `target/release/loadtest`
21:04:11 [INFO] Output verbosity level: INFO
21:04:11 [INFO] Logfile verbosity level: WARN
21:04:11 [INFO] users defaulted to number of CPUs = 18
Error: InvalidOption { option: "--host", value: "", detail: "A host must be defined via the --host option, the GooseAttack.set_default() function, or the Scenario.set_host() function (no host defined for LoadtestTransactions)." }

Command Line Arguments

To add command line arguments of your own, parse the command line yourself and give Goose its options with GooseAttack::initialize_with_config in place of GooseAttack::initialize(). initialize_with_config does not read the command line; it takes a GooseConfiguration.

Adding options to Goose’s

GooseConfiguration derives clap::Args, so a load test can flatten it into a clap::Parser of its own and add its options beside Goose’s. Every Goose option keeps working, -h and --help list both, and an option neither knows is still an error. The command line is parsed with your parser’s settings, not Goose’s, so an option given twice, such as --host a --host b, is refused where Goose keeps the last, and a negative number such as --users -1 gets no tip. Add clap to your Cargo.toml with its derive feature, such as clap = { version = "4.5", features = ["derive"] }:

use clap::Parser;
use goose::config::GooseConfiguration;
use goose::prelude::*;

/// Every Goose option, plus the load test's own.
#[derive(Parser)]
struct Options {
    #[command(flatten)]
    goose: GooseConfiguration,

    /// The ID of the product the load test buys.
    #[arg(long, default_value_t = 1)]
    product_id: u32,
}

async fn loadtest_index(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let _goose_metrics = user.get("").await?;

    Ok(())
}

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), GooseError> {
    let options = Options::parse();
    println!("product_id = {}", options.product_id);

    GooseAttack::initialize_with_config(options.goose)?
        .register_scenario(
            scenario!("User").register_transaction(transaction!(loadtest_index)),
        )
        .execute()
        .await?;

    Ok(())
}

The program above can be invoked with a command such as:

cargo run -- --host http://localhost:8080 --product-id 42

Your options must not reuse the names or short letters Goose’s take; --help lists them.

Building the configuration yourself

You can also build a GooseConfiguration without reading the command line: GooseConfiguration::default() leaves every option unset, and GooseConfiguration::parse_args_default(&["--host", "http://localhost:8080"]) parses a list of Goose options, leaving every option you do not pass unset, and returns a Result. Given -h or --help, it prints nothing and returns GooseError::InvalidOption, with the flag as option and the detail help was requested. GooseConfiguration also implements clap::Parser, so GooseConfiguration::try_parse_from(["loadtest", "--host", "http://localhost:8080"]) parses the same options, with the program name first. Each option that takes a value is an Option, None when it is not set, so a value is set with Some. Goose then applies any GooseDefault and its own defaults to every option left unset, as it does for the command line.

Options you do not set this way are not read from the command line. In particular, a host is required, from the configuration, GooseDefault::Host or Scenario::set_host:

use goose::config::GooseConfiguration;
use goose::prelude::*;

async fn loadtest_index(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let _goose_metrics = user.get("").await?;

    Ok(())
}

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), GooseError> {
    let mut config = GooseConfiguration::default();
    config.host = Some("http://localhost:8080".to_string());
    config.users = Some(10);

    GooseAttack::initialize_with_config(config)?
        .register_scenario(
            scenario!("User").register_transaction(transaction!(loadtest_index)),
        )
        .execute()
        .await?;

    Ok(())
}

Before 0.19, Goose parsed its command line with gumdrop, and a load test that called parse_args_default imported use gumdrop::Options;. Remove that import: parse_args_default and parse_args_default_or_exit are methods of GooseConfiguration itself now.

Output files

Goose writes reports and logs only when you ask for them. This page gives the rules for every file Goose writes, then three recipes. A relative path, and the goose-runs folder, are relative to the working directory, so the commands below, run with cargo run in a Goose checkout, write into the checkout.

Files

1. A run writes nothing unless a flag or the dashboard asks for it.

cargo run --release --example simple -- --host http://localhost:8080 -t 30s

This run prints its metrics and writes no file and no folder.

2. An output flag given a path writes exactly that file and nothing more.

cargo run --release --example simple -- --host http://localhost:8080 -t 30s --report-file results.html

This writes results.html, as Goose 0.18 did.

3. An output flag given without a path writes into the run’s folder. --report-file writes report.html and report.json. --goose-log, --request-log, --transaction-log, --scenario-log, --error-log and --debug-log write goose.log, requests, transactions, scenarios, errors and debug, each with its format’s extension (.json, .csv, or .log for raw and pretty), plus .gz under rule 15. An output flag followed by another option, as --report-file -R below, is given without a path. A path that begins with a hyphen goes after =, as in --goose-log=-run.log: given as its own argument it is read as options, so --goose-log -run.log is read as --goose-log -r un.log and refused because un.log is not a number. When the command line itself does not parse, as with --goose-log -x.log, the error ends with a tip to use =.

cargo run --release --example simple -- --host http://localhost:8080 -t 30s --report-file -R --request-format csv

This writes report.html, report.json and requests.csv into the run’s folder.

4. The run’s folder is --output-dir DIR, or goose-runs/<YYYYMMDD-HHMMSS> from the run’s local start time. Goose creates it as the run starts and prints its path. If the run writes nothing into it, Goose removes it when the run ends. Goose never writes a run into a folder that already exists and is not empty when the run starts. It uses DIR-2, DIR-3, DIR-4 and so on instead, always numbered from the original name (never DIR-2-2). It takes the first number whose folder is missing or empty, and prints the name it chose. Two processes that start at the same moment still get a folder each. The numbering also gives each run under --no-autostart, and each run started from the dashboard, its own folder.

While the run lasts, the folder holds a .goose-run file, which reserves it so no other run takes it. A bare --goose-log keeps the file there until the next run starts or the load test ends. A process that is killed leaves the file behind; remove it by hand to free the folder. Before the first run starts, a bare --goose-log keeps its lines in memory, up to 1 MiB, and writes them into the first run’s folder. Lines past 1 MiB are dropped, and if no run starts, none are written.

cargo run --release --example simple -- --host http://localhost:8080 -t 30s --output-dir goose-runs/nightly --report-file

The first run writes into goose-runs/nightly, the next into goose-runs/nightly-2. --output-dir needs an output flag given without a path, or --dashboard without --no-metrics; otherwise it is an error, as the folder would hold nothing. A folder Goose can’t write to stops the run before any load, with an error naming the folder. The one exception is in rule 9.

What a report holds

5. A report’s tables always count every request since the metrics reset, or from the start of the run when there is none. Its graphs, all but User Metrics, also start at the metrics reset, and cover the seconds Goose still kept when the report was written; when Save report’s window cut them shorter, the report says when its graphs start and why.

While Save report is off and no --report-file path or --pdf-print-html is given, Goose keeps only the last 10 minutes of graph data and 5 minutes of heatmap (rule 10). A report written later, after Save report is turned on or by Save report now, still has every request in its tables, and says when its graphs start and why.

6. --notes "text" is shown in the dashboard and at the top of the PDF, HTML and Markdown reports, and stored in the JSON report. It needs --report-file, --pdf-print-html or --dashboard.

cargo run --release --example simple -- --host http://localhost:8080 -t 30s --report-file --notes "baseline before cache change"

7. --report-graph-data adds the per second graph data to the JSON report. Off by default, and it needs --report-file or --dashboard. With it, a report rendered from the JSON has its graphs; without it, the rendered report says the graphs were not recorded.

cargo run --release --example simple -- --host http://localhost:8080 -t 30s --report-file results.json --report-graph-data

8. --render-report FILE.json writes the reports named by --report-file paths from an existing JSON report, then exits without running a load test. It refuses --notes and --report-graph-data, as the rendered report keeps the JSON report’s own.

cargo run --release --example simple -- --render-report results.json --report-file results.html --report-file results.md

Formats

A report’s type comes from its extension. --report-file writes PDF for .pdf, HTML for .html or .htm, Markdown for .md, and JSON for .json; PDF needs the load test built with the pdf-reports feature. Any other extension is refused before the load test starts, as in Unknown report file type: docx. Reports are written even with --no-print-metrics. Metrics shows what each format holds.

cargo run --release --example simple -- --host http://localhost:8080 -t 30s \
  --report-file results.html --report-file results.md --report-file results.json

The JSON report also holds the run’s test plan history, so a Markdown report rendered from it with --render-report has its plan overview.

--pdf-print-html FILE writes the HTML report laid out for printing, to turn into a PDF in any browser, without Chrome or the pdf-reports feature. It has the HTML report’s graphs and heatmap, with or without a --report-file, counts as a report for --notes and --no-granular-report, and is refused with --no-metrics. --pdf-scale (0.1 to 2.0, default 0.8) and --pdf-timeout (10 to 600 seconds, default 60) apply to a .pdf report. See PDF reports.

--baseline-file PATH compares the run with an earlier one in every report. PATH is a JSON report, plain or gzipped, or a run’s folder that holds report.json or report.json.gz. See Comparing against a baseline.

Each log has a format option: --request-format, --transaction-format, --scenario-format, --error-format and --debug-format each take json, the default, csv, raw or pretty. A log given without a path is named with .json, .csv, or .log for raw and pretty (rule 3). Logging describes each log.

The dashboard

The live dashboard describes the report bar and the Runs panel; Control endpoints lists the report bar’s routes, and Report endpoints lists the Runs panel’s.

9. The dashboard’s report bar, at the top of the page, shows a Save report switch: whether this run’s report.html and report.json are written to its folder. With --dashboard it starts on whether or not --report-file paths are given, except under --no-metrics. When they are given, turn it off to write only those paths. For a run without the dashboard, a bare --report-file does what the switch does. The bar also lists any --report-file paths from the command line, which are always written.

When only --dashboard asks for the run’s folder, with no output flag given without a path and no --output-dir, a folder Goose can’t write to does not stop the run. Goose prints a warning naming the folder and the reason before any load, and runs with Save report off. A banner at the top of the dashboard says why, and stays for the whole run.

cargo run --release --features dashboard --example simple -- --host http://localhost:8080 --dashboard

10. Save report can be turned on or off at any time. While it is on, Goose keeps all of the run’s graph data and heatmap since the metrics reset. While it is off and no --report-file path or --pdf-print-html is given, Goose keeps only the last 10 minutes of graph data and the last 5 minutes of heatmap. Either way, the dashboard’s charts show only the last 5 minutes. Turning it on during a run keeps graph data and heatmap from that point on, plus up to 10 minutes of graph data and 5 minutes of heatmap before it, and rule 5 labels the report.

On a long soak test with --dashboard-control, no --report-file path and no --pdf-print-html, turn Save report off to keep memory flat, and turn it on before the part of the run you want graphed. Without --dashboard-control, Save report stays on, and memory grows slowly. In a 60 minute run, the process’s resident memory grew by 544 KiB, from 35,264 KiB at 5 minutes to 35,808 KiB at 60. The run was examples/simple with 300 users against a local test server that answered / after 10 ms and every other request at once: each user logged in once during the ramp up, then the users made about 30 requests a second over 2 request names. Its command:

cargo run --release --example simple --features dashboard,pdf-reports -- -H http://127.0.0.1:19870 \
    -u 300 -r 50 -t 60m --dashboard --dashboard-port 19871 --no-telnet

11. Save report now writes the run’s report as it stands into its folder while the run continues, and marks it as in progress. It needs a run in progress. The Runs panel lists the report once it is written. If it can’t be written, the Runs panel says why. The report at the end of the run replaces it, even when Save report is off. A second switch in the bar, Include graph data, is rule 7’s flag and can also be changed at any time.

12. The Runs panel lists the folders beside the run’s folder that hold a report.json or report.json.gz. It looks in the folder that holds the run’s folder: the parent of the --output-dir folder, or goose-runs by default. Rows are newest first and show each run’s notes. A report still marked in progress shows as running for the run in progress and as incomplete for any other (a run that ended without its final report). Each row offers HTML, Markdown and JSON, rendering any format the folder lacks from its JSON. A report stored gzipped downloads as plain HTML, Markdown or JSON. With control, each row also has a Delete button. It deletes the folder’s reports and the logs Goose names there, then the folder if that leaves it empty. The folder of the run in progress can’t be deleted. Nor can the folder a bare --goose-log is open in: Goose keeps that log open in the last run’s folder until the next run starts. Nor can a folder another Goose process is writing to, which holds .goose-run (rule 4).

With --output-dir goose-runs/nightly, the panel lists the folders in goose-runs. It lists only folders whose name is a letter or digit followed by any mix of letters, digits, ., _ and -, so a run written to goose-runs/my run is not listed. For such a folder, the banner at the end of the run says where the report was saved and offers no downloads.

13. Save report now, Delete, and changing either switch need --dashboard-control and the control token, or --dashboard-no-auth (see control without a token), like Start and Stop. Without control the switches are shown read only. Save report then stays on, except under --no-metrics or with a folder Goose can’t write to (rule 9): every --dashboard run writes report.html and report.json into its folder, and Goose keeps all of the run’s graph data (rule 10).

TOKEN=$(openssl rand -hex 16)
cargo run --release --features dashboard --example simple -- --host http://localhost:8080 \
  --dashboard --dashboard-control --dashboard-auth-token "$TOKEN"

Compression

14. Any report or log whose name ends in .gz is written gzipped. A report’s format comes from the name before .gz. A log keeps the format its --request-format or other format option sets. Goose reads a .json.gz report anywhere it reads a JSON report: --baseline-file, --render-report and the Runs panel. --pdf-print-html writes plain HTML whatever its name.

cargo run --release --example simple -- --host http://localhost:8080 -t 30s --report-file results.json.gz -R requests.csv.gz --request-format csv

15. --compress gzips every file Goose writes into the run’s folder under rules 3 and 9, each named with .gz added. A path given to an output flag keeps exactly that name, and is gzipped only when that name ends in .gz (rule 14). --compress LEVEL sets the level from 1 (fastest) to 9 (smallest), default 6, for every gzipped file, including rule 14’s.

cargo run --release --example simple -- --host http://localhost:8080 -t 30s --report-file -R --compress 9

This writes report.html.gz, report.json.gz and requests.json.gz into the run’s folder.

Recipes

One report file

cargo run --release --example simple -- --host http://localhost:8080 -t 5m --report-file results.html

Goose writes results.html and nothing else.

See a run made without the dashboard in the dashboard

Write the run’s report into a folder under goose-runs:

cargo run --release --example simple -- --host http://localhost:8080 -t 5m \
  --output-dir goose-runs/nightly --report-file

Later, start the dashboard from the same working directory:

cargo run --release --features dashboard --example simple -- --host http://localhost:8080 --dashboard --no-autostart

Its Runs panel lists nightly. With --no-autostart, Goose runs no new load test until a Controller starts one, because without --dashboard-control the dashboard has no Start button.

Keep files small

Add --compress to gzip every file in the run’s folder, or name a file with .gz:

cargo run --release --example simple -- --host http://localhost:8080 -t 10m \
  --report-file --report-file summary.html -R --compress

summary.html stays plain: a path keeps its name. To read the gzipped files:

gunzip -k goose-runs/*/report.html.gz    # then open report.html in a browser
gzip -dc goose-runs/*/report.json.gz | jq .
gzip -dc goose-runs/*/requests.json.gz | head

The dashboard’s Runs panel also opens a gzipped report. A gzipped log is whole once its run ends. A gzipped Goose log is also whole between runs, so gzip -dc reads it while the dashboard waits for the next run. During a run, and after a process killed with SIGKILL, gzip -dc reads a gzipped log up to the last part written, then reports an unexpected end of file. To follow a run as it goes, use a plain log.

Metrics

Goose can show a load test’s results six ways. The dashboard and the PDF need a Cargo feature as well as an option; the HTML, Markdown and JSON reports take one option each; the console metrics, like the log lines, need no flag.

Choosing a view

ViewWhat it holdsWho it is forHow to get it
Live dashboardLive charts and tablesYou, while the run is goingThe dashboard feature, then --dashboard, and open http://127.0.0.1:5118/
PDF reportThe HTML report’s sections in one tall, fixed page; its line graphs can stop short in this releaseSomeone who reads a finished run on screen or as an attachmentThe pdf-reports feature and Chrome, which Goose downloads if it finds none, then --report-file report.pdf; for paper, or without the feature or Chrome, --pdf-print-html print.html and a browser’s Print, which breaks the report into pages
HTML reportThe tables, graphs to zoom and filter, and the response time heatmap; the graphs load from a CDN when the report is openedAn engineer studying the run--report-file report.html
Markdown reportTables onlyAn issue, a pull request or a wiki page--report-file report.md
JSON reportEvery table’s numbers, for programsA script, a CI job, or a later run’s --baseline-file--report-file report.json
Console metricsText tablesA quick look in the terminal, or a CI logPrinted at the end of every run, unless --no-print-metrics or --no-metrics

A feature is turned on for the goose dependency in your load test’s Cargo.toml, as in goose = { version = "^0.19", features = ["dashboard", "pdf-reports"] }; the command below runs an example in the Goose repository, so it uses --features. A report’s type comes from the extension of its --report-file, so repeat the option for each report you want. If you keep the JSON report, --render-report can write any of the other reports from it later, without running the load test, as in --render-report report.json --report-file report.pdf (see What a report holds). Without a JSON report, another report means running the load test again. So add --report-file report.json --report-graph-data to any run you may want to report on later: a report rendered from a JSON report has graphs only when the run had --report-graph-data or the dashboard’s Include graph data switch on. Where reports go when no path is given is in Output Files.

Every image and excerpt on this page comes from one run of the Umami example against a Drupal 11 site installed with the Umami demo profile, reached at http://localhost:18888. The site was changed as the example needs: search and contact enabled, a Website feedback contact form added, an alias added for one renamed article, image styles generated ahead of the run, and CSS and JS aggregation off, so each CSS and JS file is a static asset request of its own:

cargo run --release --features dashboard,pdf-reports --example umami -- \
  -H http://localhost:18888 --dashboard -u 50 -r 5 -t 3m \
  --report-file report.pdf --report-file report.html \
  --report-file report.md --report-file report.json

The run behind the images also moved the dashboard and telnet ports, with --dashboard-port 5128 --telnet-port 5126, which changes nothing shown here. No request failed. Goose launched 5 users a second up to 50, reset its metrics once all 50 were running, then ran for 3 minutes. The tables, and every graph but User Metrics, count from that reset.

The live dashboard

The dashboard shows the run as it happens: users, requests and failures per second, the response time heatmap, and tables of scenarios, transactions, requests and errors, updated every second.

The chart reads zero for the seconds the users were launching, because Goose clears it at the metrics reset; the summary under the chart gives the mean of the last 10 seconds, 502 requests a second.

With --dashboard the dashboard’s Save report switch starts on, so the run also writes its own report.html and report.json into the run’s folder, named for the run’s local start time, such as goose-runs/20261028-175916/ (see Output Files). The --report-file reports go to the paths given, relative to where you ran the command. With its report saved and a dashboard tab open when the run ends on its own, Goose stays up so you can download its HTML, Markdown and JSON reports from the Runs panel; it exits on Ctrl-C, a Controller shutdown, or the dashboard’s Quit button, which needs --dashboard-control. A threshold, the killswitch or Ctrl-C that stops the run ends Goose without waiting for the dashboard (see When the run ends). Live Dashboard tours every panel, and the Dashboard Reference covers each in detail.

PDF report

The PDF report is the HTML report with print styling, rendered by Chrome into one tall page. It has the same sections as the HTML report, controls included, though they do nothing in a PDF. Chrome loads the graphs from the HTML report’s CDN when it writes the PDF. In this release the line graphs, such as requests per second, can stop partway along their time axis, because Chrome prints them before they finish drawing; send the HTML report when the graphs matter.

The PDF is laid out as the HTML report is, in one tall page: the header, then the plan overview, then each section below them.

Name a .pdf file with --report-file report.pdf. --pdf-scale and --pdf-timeout tune how Chrome renders it; see the PDF report example. Without Chrome, --pdf-print-html writes the report laid out for printing from any browser; see the printer-friendly HTML report example.

Chrome

Requirements gives the order in which Goose looks for a browser; a Chrome for Testing headless shell it downloaded earlier is used before a new download. When the CHROME environment variable is set and not empty, Goose uses that binary and never downloads one. It must name an executable file: if it names a macOS app bundle such as /Applications/Google Chrome.app, Goose stops with an error that names the binary inside it, Contents/MacOS/Google Chrome.

Before the load test starts, Goose finds the browser, downloading it if needed, and renders a small test page, which must finish within --pdf-timeout. If that fails, the load test does not start, so a Chrome problem never costs a finished run. The log names the browser it will use:

16:59:19 [INFO] PDF reports will use Chrome 156.0.8078.48 at /Applications/Google Chrome.app/Contents/MacOS/Google Chrome (installed); checked in 3.0s

A downloaded browser is kept in goose/chrome under the user cache directory: ~/.cache (or $XDG_CACHE_HOME) on Linux, ~/Library/Caches on macOS, and %LOCALAPPDATA% on Windows. Once a week Goose checks for a newer stable Chrome for Testing; when there is one, it downloads it and removes the older ones, and when the check fails it logs a warning and uses the one it has. Goose never updates or removes an installed browser or the one CHROME names. Goose built for musl, such as on Alpine, never downloads Chrome, because Chrome for Testing needs glibc: install Chromium (apk add chromium) or set CHROME.

Troubleshooting PDF reports

  • Without a browser, Goose needs network access to download one, and unzip on PATH (tar on Windows). If a firewall blocks the download, or unzip is missing, install Chrome or Chromium or set CHROME.
  • On Linux, a downloaded headless shell needs the shared libraries Chrome uses, such as libnss3. Installing Chromium from your distribution brings them in.
  • When Chrome won’t start, the error names the binary and how Goose found it. On Linux it also says when the binary’s ELF interpreter is missing, usually a glibc binary on a musl system, and lists the shared libraries ldd can’t find. An “Exec format error” means the binary was built for another architecture.
  • If the PDF fails at the end of a run, the other reports are still written, and the empty PDF file is removed.

HTML report

--report-file report.html writes the HTML report, one file of about 730 KB for this run. Its graphs use the ECharts library, loaded from a CDN when the report is opened, so they appear only where the CDN can be reached. Click a name in a graph’s legend to hide or show its line, and drag the handles under a graph to zoom.

Header and plan overview. The report opens with any --notes, then the number of users, the target host and the Goose version, then the plan overview: each step of the run, when it started and stopped, how long it took, and how many users were running. The PDF image above shows both.

Sections that depend on the run and its options. Thresholds follows the plan overview when --threshold is set. When coordinated omission mitigation added synthetic times, an adjusted table follows Request Metrics and another follows Response Time Metrics. With mitigation on, the report ends with Coordinated Omission Event Metrics. Errors and Transaction Errors follow User Metrics when a request failed or a transaction returned an error that is not a request error. --no-error-summary leaves them out, and --no-status-codes, --no-transaction-metrics and --no-scenario-metrics leave out Status Code, Transaction and Scenario Metrics. With --baseline-file, each number in the request, response time, transaction, scenario and error tables is followed by its change from the earlier run. With mitigation on, the request counts and rates and the status code counts include the synthetic requests (see Reading the event summary).

Request Metrics

A graph of requests per second, with a line for all requests and one for each request, then a table of each request: how many were made and failed, the average, minimum and maximum response time, and requests and failures per second. --no-granular-report draws only the totals, in this graph and in every other line graph of the report; the heatmap keeps its menu of request names.

Most of the 500 requests a second were static assets, the line just under the total; the page requests are the thin lines near zero.

Response time breakdowns by status code

When one request name gets more than one status code, such as some 200 and some 500 responses, a row for each status code follows its row, starting └─. In the PDF, HTML and Markdown reports the row gives that code’s count, share of the responses, and average, minimum and maximum times; in the console metrics it follows the request in the table of times, with the share in its name and - for Median, because Goose keeps only those figures for each code. A request that got no response, such as one that could not connect, counts under status code 0. The rows hold measured times only, never coordinated omission synthetic ones. They are in the PDF, HTML and Markdown reports, in the JSON report as each request’s status_code_timings, and in the console metrics.

Response Time Metrics

A graph of the average response time each second, then the response time heatmap of the run since the metrics reset, then a table of percentiles from 50% to 100%: the 95%ile column is the slowest of the fastest 95% of each request’s responses. The console metrics use other percentiles, from 50% to 99.99%.

The p50 line holds near 9 ms throughout; the p95 line sits near 80 ms for the first half minute, then near 25 ms with spikes to 80 ms and more.

The heatmap uses the dashboard’s rows and percentiles, with p50, p95 and p99 drawn as colored lines, but covers the whole run since the metrics reset, while the dashboard’s heatmap shows the last 5 minutes. A column starts one second wide. Once a series holds 600 columns for all requests, or 60 for one request name, adjacent pairs merge, and the heatmap says how wide each column is. This 3 minute run kept one second columns for all requests, while each request name’s columns merged to 4 seconds. A menu picks all requests or one of the first 100 request names Goose recorded. --no-percentiles-over-time leaves the heatmap out.

Status Code Metrics

A table of every status code each request received.

Every request in this run got 200; a request that also got other codes lists each with its count.

Transaction Metrics

A graph of transactions per second and a table of each transaction, grouped by scenario. A transaction fails only when its code returns an error, so a transaction can succeed while a request it made failed; Transaction Log explains which errors are counted where. Scenarios and transactions are numbered from 1 on the dashboard, in every report’s transaction errors table (the JSON report’s transaction_errors too) and in the console metrics. The transaction tables of the PDF, HTML, Markdown and JSON reports number them from 0, as 0.0. So transaction 0.2 in those tables is 1.3 on the dashboard and in the transaction errors tables, and transaction 3 under scenario 1 in the console metrics.

Each transaction loads a page and its static assets, and each form transaction also loads a second page, so about 30 transactions a second carried the 500 requests a second above.

Scenario Metrics

A graph of scenarios per second and a table of each scenario. # Users is how many users ran the scenario since the metrics reset, or since the start in a run with no reset, whether or not they finished an iteration. # Times Run counts finished iterations, and the times are how long one iteration took. Iterations is # Times Run divided by # Users, in the Aggregated row too. A scenario no user ran shows an average and Iterations of 0.

The one Admin user took 34 to 43 seconds for each iteration, so it finished 4 in the 3 minutes, while the 40 Anonymous English users finished 254.

User Metrics

A graph of how many users were running.

This is the only graph that shows the ramp up; the others count from the metrics reset.

Markdown report

--report-file report.md writes the Markdown report: the HTML report’s tables, as Markdown tables, in the same order. It has no graphs or heatmap, no header lines for the user count, host or version, and no coordinated omission adjusted tables. Its error sections are headed Error Metrics and Transaction Error Metrics. Its coordinated omission section, Coordinated Omission Metrics, adds tables of events by user and by scenario when there are events; see Coordinated Omission metrics. Its scenario table’s first column is headed Scenario, as in the HTML report. The start of its Request Metrics:

## Request Metrics

| Method | Name | # Requests | # Fails | Average (ms) | Min (ms) | Max (ms) | RPS | Failures/s |
| ------ | ---- | ---------: | ------: | -----------: | -------: | -------: | --: | ---------: |
| GET | / | 528 | 0 | 32 | 8.55 | 267 | 2.93 | 0.00 |
| GET | /en/about-umami | 254 | 0 | 27 | 9.15 | 107 | 1.41 | 0.00 |
| GET | /en/articles/ | 265 | 0 | 39 | 10 | 256 | 1.47 | 0.00 |

JSON report
