--report-file report.json writes the JSON report: every table the other reports show, as indented JSON (383 KB for this run), with the graph data only when asked for (below). Its top level, with each array and object shown by its size:

jq 'map_values(if type == "array" then "\(length) rows" elif type == "object" then "{\(keys | length) keys}" else . end)' report.json

{
  "goose_version": "0.19.0",
  "test": "umami",
  "started": "2026-10-28T16:59:19Z",
  "ended": "2026-10-28T17:02:30Z",
  "ended_by": "completed",
  "in_progress": false,
  "raw_metrics": "{14 keys}",
  "raw_request_metrics": "143 rows",
  "raw_response_metrics": "143 rows",
  "co_request_metrics": null,
  "co_response_metrics": null,
  "scenario_metrics": "4 rows",
  "transaction_metrics": "27 rows",
  "status_code_metrics": "143 rows",
  "errors": null,
  "coordinated_omission_metrics": null
}

The JSON report’s times, which end Z, are UTC, as are the log’s; the run’s folder and the plan overview in the other reports and the console metrics use local time, an hour ahead here, as in Running A Load Test.

  • raw_metrics is Goose’s own record of the run, GooseMetrics, with the test plan steps the run went through, in history, and each --threshold result. Each transaction’s transaction_name there is an object that says how it was named, such as {"TransactionOnly":"anon /"}.
  • raw_request_metrics, raw_response_metrics, scenario_metrics, transaction_metrics and status_code_metrics are the tables the other reports show; raw_request_metrics has a row for each request and the Aggregated row, and each request in raw_metrics holds status_code_timings in place of the └─ breakdown rows.
  • errors is null without errors, and transaction_errors appears only when a transaction returned an error that is not a request error.
  • The co_ keys are null until coordinated omission mitigation adds synthetic times, and coordinated_omission_metrics is null with mitigation off.
  • graphs, with the per second data behind the graphs and the heatmap, appears only when the run had --report-graph-data or the dashboard’s Include graph data switch on.

Minimums, maximums and percentiles in raw_request_metrics, raw_response_metrics and the other tables are whole milliseconds, truncated, so a 37.999 ms time is 37; averages keep their fraction of a millisecond, as 31.861234848484848 for GET /. A request or scenario average with no times to average is 0, and each scenario’s heading row in transaction_metrics has null averages and rates. The raw timing data keeps microseconds, in fields ending _us. A JSON report loads as a baseline with --baseline-file (see Baselines from older Goose releases), and --render-report writes the other reports from it.

Console metrics

While the load test runs, Goose logs what it is doing; Running A Load Test walks through those log lines. Once every user has started, it prints the tables so far and resets its metrics, after which every table, errors included, counts from zero. A run with --iterations or a test plan never resets, and --no-reset-metrics keeps the ramp up in the tables. With --running-metrics N it prints the tables again every N seconds, ramp up included. During the ramp up it also prints them each time a user starts at least N milliseconds after the last print, because in Goose 0.19 the user launch code reads N as milliseconds. When the run ends, it prints the final metrics in this order: scenarios, with their times; transactions, with their times; requests, with their response times, percentiles and status codes; errors, transaction errors and thresholds, when there are any; the coordinated omission event summary, whenever mitigation is on; and last the plan overview, as Running A Load Test shows. From this run, up to the plan overview, with ... for the rows and tables left out:

--no-print-metrics leaves out every table the console prints, at the reset, with --running-metrics and at the end; the reports are still written.

How Goose measures response times

Goose measures time to first byte: from sending a request until its response headers arrive, including connecting and following redirects, but not reading the body. Every request Goose sends is timed this way. A load test that needs the time to the last byte reads the body and times it; see Measuring complete download time.

Goose keeps request, transaction and scenario times to the microsecond. The tables of the dashboard, the PDF, HTML and Markdown reports and the console metrics show times under 10 ms to the hundredth of a millisecond and longer times in whole milliseconds, rounded half up. The heatmap labels truncate instead, as the Dashboard Reference explains, and so do the minimums, maximums and percentiles of the JSON report. Percentiles come from buckets never more than 0.78% off the times they hold, so 2.2 ms and 2.5 ms are told apart, and the fastest and slowest times are kept exactly.

Measuring complete download time

To measure the time to the last byte, read the body yourself and record the time it took with record_custom_request_duration, which records it as a request of its own:

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
use goose::prelude::*;
use std::time::Instant;

async fn measure_full_download(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let started = Instant::now();
    let goose = user.get("/large-file").await?;

    // Read the entire response body.
    let success = match goose.response {
        Ok(response) => response.bytes().await.is_ok(),
        Err(_) => false,
    };

    // Record the time to the last byte as a request of its own.
    user.record_custom_request_duration(
        "DOWNLOAD",
        "/large-file",
        started.elapsed(),
        success,
        None,
        None,
    )
    .await?;

    Ok(())
}
}

The GET /large-file row then holds the time to the first byte, and the DOWNLOAD /large-file row the time to the last. A custom request is counted like any other, in its own row and in the Aggregated row; with no status code given, the status code table shows it under [0], as in the TCP example. Reading whole bodies uses more memory and can lower the load one Goose process generates.

Developer documentation on how metrics are collected and stored is in the goose::metrics module.

Tips

The tips follow a load test’s life: writing it, checking it, the first runs, scaling up, reading the results and debugging. If you are back with a problem, start here:

  • A request that should fail passes: Check the page, not only the status code.
  • The machine running Goose is the limit: Check that Goose is not the bottleneck.
  • Requests fail to connect once you add users: Raise the open file limit.
  • The reports are missing: Write the reports, and keep each run.
  • A CI job should fail: Make a CI job fail on slow responses.
  • Response times look too good: When response times look too good.
  • Requests time out: When requests time out.
  • A run never ends: When a run never ends.
  • A command from an older post fails: Options renamed since the older posts.

Writing a load test

Handle every error

A request method such as get returns Ok even when the request fails, with the failure inside the response, so ? on the call does not stop the transaction. Goose still counts the request as failed; see A failed request does not fail its transaction. Under load, an unwrap() on goose.response panics the first time a request gets no response, as when it times out, and one on its body panics when the body cannot be read. Handle those with if let or match, and return other errors with ?. Goose records an error a transaction returns and keeps the user running.

async fn website_index(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let goose = user.get("/").await?;
    if let Ok(response) = goose.response {
        let _text = response.text().await;
    }
    Ok(())
}

Name requests that carry an ID

Goose groups requests by name, and a request’s name is its path unless you give one. A test that requests /node/1, /node/2 and so on gets a row for every node; one name for them all gives one row whose numbers cover every node.

let path = format!("/node/{nid}");
let _goose = user.get_named(&path, "/node/%nid").await?;

Give users a wait time

A scenario without a wait time sends each user’s next request as soon as the last one ends, so each user loads the server as hard as it can. set_wait_time pauses each user for a random time in a range after each transaction, as a person would. With a wait time, the same load needs more users.

scenario!("WebsiteUser")
    .set_wait_time(Duration::from_secs(5), Duration::from_secs(15))?

Stop the run from your own code

goose::trigger_killswitch stops a run as Ctrl-C does, with every report written; see Killswitch. A threshold, set on the command line or in code with GooseDefault::Threshold, stops the run when a metric such as the error rate crosses a limit.

goose::trigger_killswitch("Server returned 503: Service Unavailable");

Checking a load test

Check the page, not only the status code

A 200 with an error page is still a 200. Check the response for the content you expect, and mark the request failed with set_failure when it is not there. Pass the page to set_failure so the debug log keeps it; Request failures explains set_failure, and Validating Requests shows other checks. In Goose 0.19 the request log still shows such a request as a success, with "success":true; see List every failed request.

let mut goose = user.get("/").await?;
if let Ok(response) = goose.response {
    let text = response.text().await.unwrap_or_default();
    if !text.contains("<title>Home</title>") {
        return user.set_failure("title not found", &mut goose.request, None, Some(&text));
    }
}

Start with one user and a debug log

Run one user for a short time with a debug log before adding load, so each request is easy to follow. Goose writes an entry to the debug log each time the load test calls log_debug and each time set_failure marks a request as failed; a test that does neither gets an empty debug log. Add -R for a line for every request as well. Without file names, -D writes debug.json and -R writes requests.json into the run’s folder, goose-runs/<start time>/; see Output Files.

cargo run --release -- -H http://localhost:8080 -u 1 -t 30s -D -R

First runs

Build with --release

cargo run without --release builds a debug binary, which sends far less load: Tag1 measured about 13 times the traffic from a release build in The golden Goose egg. Every cargo run on this page uses --release.

cargo run --release -- -H http://localhost:8080

Always name the host

A load test can set a default host in its code, as the Umami example does, and a run without -H (--host) then loads that host. A load test with no default host refuses to start without -H. Pass it every time.

cargo run --release -- -H https://staging.example.com

Stop with one Ctrl-C

Ctrl-C stops the run cleanly: users stop, every report is written and the final metrics print. A second Ctrl-C exits at once and writes none of them, so wait for the first to finish. The run in Running A Load Test shows the line Goose prints:

17:14:04 [WARN] caught ctrl-c, stopping...

Start the run from the dashboard

With the dashboard feature, --no-autostart starts Goose idle, so you can check the settings in the dashboard, at http://127.0.0.1:5118/, before you press Start. Pressing Start needs --dashboard-control, which in turn needs --dashboard-auth-token or --dashboard-no-auth; the command below uses --dashboard-no-auth, which suits a test on your own machine only. See Live Dashboard.

cargo run --release --features goose/dashboard -- -H http://localhost:8080 \
  --dashboard --dashboard-control --dashboard-no-auth --no-autostart

Scaling up

Check that Goose is not the bottleneck

When the machine running Goose runs out of CPU, the results measure Goose, not the server: response times grow and requests per second stop rising as you add users. Watch the Goose process in top or htop while the test runs; replace loadtest below with your binary’s name, which is the crate’s name unless you set another. Goose uses every core, so top can show it at up to 100% times the number of cores; near that, or with every core busy in htop, Goose is the limit. Build with --release, send less load from this machine with fewer users or --throttle-requests, or use a larger machine.

top -p $(pgrep -nf target/release/loadtest)      # Linux
top -pid $(pgrep -nf target/release/loadtest)    # macOS

Raise the open file limit

By default each user holds its own connections, and each connection is an open file. On Linux and macOS the default limit is often 1,024 or lower, and Goose does not raise it. Past the limit, requests fail with status code 0 and client error (Connect), and nothing in the output names open files. A test with no wait time can also run on past -t, and its first Ctrl-C does not stop it; a second ends it, with no reports. Raise the limit in the shell that runs Goose; A Goose in the clouds mentions raising it for a large run.

ulimit -n 100000

Ramp up slowly

Goose starts one user a second by default. A high -r (--increase-rate, users a second) starts many users at once, which can overwhelm a cold server and measure its warm up, not its steady state. For a large run, spread the start over time with -s (--increase-time), or give the run a shape with a test plan.

cargo run --release -- -H http://localhost:8080 -u 500 --increase-time 5m -t 30m

Cap the request rate

--throttle-requests caps the requests Goose sends each second, whatever the number of users. Use it to stay under a rate limit, or to hold a steady load while you change something else; see Throttle.

cargo run --release -- -H http://localhost:8080 -u 100 --throttle-requests 200

Use every core of one machine

One Goose process runs its users across every CPU core; set -u for the load you need rather than starting more processes. Distributed load tests with Gaggles, which the older posts describe, return in Goose 0.20; see Gaggle.

cargo run --release -- -H http://localhost:8080 -u 1000 -s 5m

Reading the results

Write the reports, and keep each run

A run writes no report files unless you ask for them. A --report-file without a file name, or --dashboard, writes report.html and report.json into the run’s folder, goose-runs/<start time>/ under the directory you ran Goose from; Goose logs the folder as the run starts (see Output Files). A file name you give is written where you name it, relative to the directory you ran Goose from. Reports are also missing after a second Ctrl-C (see Stop with one Ctrl-C), and --report-file is refused with --no-metrics. --notes labels the run, and --baseline-file compares a later run with an earlier one; see Comparing against a baseline.

cargo run --release -- -H http://localhost:8080 --report-file --notes "before the cache change"

The tables start once every user is running

By default Goose resets its metrics once every user has started, so the tables describe the run at full load. --no-reset-metrics keeps the ramp up in them. A run with a test plan or --iterations never resets. See Console metrics.

cargo run --release -- -H http://localhost:8080 --no-reset-metrics

A failed request does not fail its transaction

A request fails on an error status code, when no response comes back, or when your code calls set_failure. A transaction fails only when its function returns an error. This run of the simple example, with a 1 second timeout, loaded a local server that answers / after 3 seconds. Both requests for / timed out, but transaction 0.1, which requests /, did not fail. The example’s transactions have no names, so its Markdown report numbers them by scenario and transaction, from 0:

cargo run --release --example simple -- -H http://127.0.0.1:19050 \
  -u 2 -r 2 -t 15s --timeout 1 --no-telnet --report-file report.md

## Request Metrics

| Method | Name | # Requests | # Fails | Average (ms) | Min (ms) | Max (ms) | RPS | Failures/s |
| ------ | ---- | ---------: | ------: | -----------: | -------: | -------: | --: | ---------: |
| GET | / | 2 | 2 | 1001 | 1001 | 1001 | 0.13 | 0.13 |
...
## Transaction Metrics

| Transaction | # Times Run | # Fails | Average (ms) | Min (ms) | Max (ms) | RPS | Failures/s |
| ----------- | ----------: | ------: | -----------: | -------: | -------: | --: | ---------: |
| **WebsiteUser** |
...
| 0.1  | 2 | 0 | 1001 | 1001 | 1001 | 0.13 | 0.00 |

To make a failed request fail its transaction, build it with GooseRequest::builder().error_on_fail() and send it with user.request, or return what set_failure returns, as Check the page does; see Validating Requests.

Make a CI job fail on slow responses

--threshold judges the run against a rule. A rule that fails makes the run fail and, by default, stops it while it runs; with --no-threshold-abort the run goes to the end and is judged then. Either way execute() returns an error, so a main that returns it with ? exits with a nonzero status; see Exit code in CI.

cargo run --release -- -H http://localhost:8080 -t 5m --threshold "p95<500ms" --threshold "error-rate<1%"

When response times look too good

First check # Fails in the request table, and that the pages are the ones you expect (see Check the page): fast failures look like fast responses. When a server stalls a request, its user waits instead of sending the requests it would have sent, so the wait shows in one response time and the requests it never sent are missing from the measured times. --co-mitigation average adds synthetic response times for those missing requests, without sending more load; Was my run affected? shows how to read the result.

cargo run --release -- -H http://localhost:8080 --co-mitigation average --report-file

Debugging

When requests time out

Goose times out a request after 60 seconds. A timeout is usually the finding, since the server could not answer in time at this load. Raise the limit only when you expect slow answers: the requests that would have timed out then count as slow responses, not failures. A timed-out request counts at the limit in the response times. In the run above, with --timeout 1, GET / shows 1,001 ms as its average, minimum and maximum. --timeout sets the limit in seconds, decimals allowed.

cargo run --release -- -H http://localhost:8080 --timeout 120

To remove the limit, build a client of your own with GooseUser::set_client_builder; for a limit no request will reach, set a very long timeout, such as --timeout 86400 for a day.

See the page a request got

Run with -D (--debug-log), as in Start with one user and a debug log: each entry in debug.json is a JSON object, and body holds the page your code passed to set_failure or log_debug, as Check the page does. jq or jaq can pull it out to open in a browser; you may need to turn off JavaScript to view it. The request log below records every request, but not the page it got.

head -n 1 goose-runs/<start time>/debug.json | jq -r .body > page.html

List every failed request

The error table, in the reports and in the console’s final metrics, says why requests failed, as in this Markdown report of the timeout run:

## Error Metrics

| Method | Name |  #  | Error |
| ------ | ---- | --: | ----- |
| GET | / | 2 | error sending request /: operation timed out |

-R (--request-log) writes a line for every request; without a file name it writes requests.json into the run’s folder, and jq can list the ones that failed. The simple example with -R, run again against the slow server for 10 seconds, gave these two lines:

cargo run --release --example simple -- -H http://127.0.0.1:19052 \
  -u 2 -r 2 -t 10s --timeout 1 --no-telnet -R -q
jq -c 'select(.success == false) | {name, status_code, error}' goose-runs/<start time>/requests.json

{"name":"/","status_code":0,"error":"error sending request /: operation timed out"}
{"name":"/","status_code":0,"error":"error sending request /: operation timed out"}

A status code of 0 means no response came back; see Response time breakdowns by status code. A request your code marks failed with set_failure still shows "success":true in the request log, so the jq command above skips it; -E (--error-log) writes a line for each of them, as for every other failed request.

When a run never ends

A run without -t or --iterations, or with a test plan whose last step still has users, goes on until Ctrl-C (end a plan with a step to 0 users, such as 0,0s), and a run past the open file limit can too (see Raise the open file limit). When -t runs out, or at the first Ctrl-C, each user first finishes its transaction: after entering GooseAttack phase: Decrease, each request left in it can wait up to the timeout, 60 seconds by default, against a stalled server, before the reports are written. Unless a threshold or the killswitch stops it, a run started from the dashboard or a Controller leaves Goose up when it ends, and so does a --dashboard run that ends on its own with its report saved and a dashboard tab open. Goose then stays up, even after the tab is closed, until Ctrl-C, a Controller shutdown, or the dashboard’s Quit button, which needs --dashboard-control (see When the run ends). In Goose 0.19, -v can hang a run partway through printing its metrics, where not even two Ctrl-Cs stop it: leave out --running-metrics when you use -v, and add --no-reset-metrics against a fast server. From another terminal, end a hung run with kill and its process ID, which writes no reports; replace loadtest with your binary’s name:

kill $(pgrep -nf target/release/loadtest)

Options renamed since the older posts

Tag1’s blog posts about Goose from 2020 and 2021, such as Using Gaggles to distribute your load testing, use options that have been renamed or removed. Their code also uses the names from before Goose 0.16: taskset! is now scenario!, register_taskset is register_scenario, task! is transaction!, register_task is register_transaction, GooseTaskSet is Scenario, GooseTaskError is TransactionError, and GooseTaskResult is TransactionResult.

In the postsNow
-c, --clients-u, --users
--hatch-rate-r, --increase-rate
--startup-time-s, --increase-time
--log-file-G, --goose-log
--debug-file, --debug-log-file-D, --debug-log
--requests-file, --metrics-file, --stats-log-file-R, --request-log
--metrics-format, --requests-format--request-format
--task-log-T, --transaction-log
--task-format--transaction-format
--print-statsthe default; --no-print-metrics turns the printing off, --no-metrics the collecting
--only-summarythe default; --running-metrics N adds running metrics
--reset-statsthe default
--no-stats--no-metrics
--status-codesthe default; --no-status-codes turns it off
--no-task-metrics--no-transaction-metrics
--manager, --worker and the other Gaggle optionsnone in Goose 0.19; Gaggles return in 0.20, see Gaggle

Logging

With logging, it’s possible to record all Goose activity. This can be useful for debugging errors, for validating the load test, and for creating graphs.

When logging is enabled, a central logging thread maintains a buffer to minimize the IO overhead, and controls the writing to ensure that multiple threads don’t corrupt each other’s messages. All log messages are sent through a channel to the logging thread and written asynchronously, minimizing the impact on the load test.

Each log option takes a file name, such as --request-log requests.log, and writes that file. Given without one, as --request-log, it writes a file named for the log and its format into the run’s folder: requests.json, requests.csv, or requests.log for raw and pretty. A name ending in .gz writes the log gzipped, and --compress gzips every log in the run’s folder. See Output files.

Every log takes a format option: --request-format, --transaction-format, --scenario-format, --error-format and --debug-format. Each accepts json (the default), csv, raw or pretty. A JSON log writes one object per line, with its keys in the order of the logged structure’s fields. A CSV log starts with a header row. The elapsed field of the request, transaction, scenario and error logs counts milliseconds since the load test started, a clock every user shares. The raw format writes Rust’s debug output of each entry on one line, and pretty spreads the same output over several indented lines.

The request, transaction, scenario and error logs are built from Goose metrics, so they can’t be combined with --no-metrics. The debug log can.

What logging costs

With Goose on two cores running examples/simple with 1,000 users and no waits, the JSON request log added about 3.4 µs of CPU to each request, a tenth of what Goose spent per request. Gzip at the default level 6 roughly doubles that. Each request writes about 500 bytes, so at 25,000 requests a second the log grows by about 45 GB an hour. Write it uncompressed unless the disk can’t keep up or space is short; then gzip it at the default level.

Request Log

Goose can optionally log details about all the requests made during the load test to a file. This log file contains the running metrics Goose generates as the load test runs. To enable, add the --request-log <request.log> command line option, where <request.log> is either a relative or absolute path of the log file to create. Any existing file that may already exist will be overwritten.

If --request-body is also enabled, the request log will include the entire body of any client requests, in the body field of the raw request.

Logs include the entire GooseRequestMetric object which also includes the entire GooseRawRequest object, both created for all client requests.

The headers field of the GooseRawRequest lists the headers set on the individual request (for example by .header(), .bearer_auth(), .json() or .form()); headers that Reqwest adds when it sends the request are not included: the client’s default headers (those set with .default_headers(), the User-Agent and Accept: */*), the Cookie header from the cookie store, Accept-Encoding when compression is enabled, and the Host and Content-Length headers. Reqwest offers no way to read them: https://github.com/tag1consulting/goose/issues/336. Request headers are only captured when the request log, the debug log or the error log is enabled, otherwise headers is empty.

Log Format

By default, logs are written in JSON Lines format. For example, from the simple example with --request-body also enabled:

{"elapsed":505,"scenario_index":0,"scenario_name":"websiteuser","transaction_index":0,"transaction_name":{"TransactionOnly":""},"raw":{"method":"Post","url":"http://127.0.0.1:8080/login","headers":["(\"content-type\", \"application/x-www-form-urlencoded\")"],"body":"username=test_user&password=","custom_method":""},"name":"/login","final_url":"http://127.0.0.1:8080/login","redirected":false,"response_time":1,"status_code":200,"success":true,"update":false,"user":3,"error":"","coordinated_omission_elapsed":0,"user_cadence":0,"response_time_fraction_us":221}
{"elapsed":506,"scenario_index":0,"scenario_name":"websiteuser","transaction_index":1,"transaction_name":{"TransactionOnly":""},"raw":{"method":"Get","url":"http://127.0.0.1:8080/","headers":[],"body":"","custom_method":""},"name":"/","final_url":"http://127.0.0.1:8080/","redirected":false,"response_time":1,"status_code":200,"success":true,"update":false,"user":3,"error":"","coordinated_omission_elapsed":0,"user_cadence":0,"response_time_fraction_us":202}
{"elapsed":6875,"scenario_index":0,"scenario_name":"websiteuser","transaction_index":2,"transaction_name":{"TransactionOnly":""},"raw":{"method":"Get","url":"http://127.0.0.1:8080/about/","headers":[],"body":"","custom_method":""},"name":"/about/","final_url":"http://127.0.0.1:8080/about/","redirected":false,"response_time":0,"status_code":200,"success":true,"update":false,"user":2,"error":"","coordinated_omission_elapsed":0,"user_cadence":0,"response_time_fraction_us":632}

The --request-format option can be used to log in csv, json (default), raw or pretty format. The raw format is Rust’s debug output of the entire GooseRequestMetric object, one per line, and pretty is the same output spread over several indented lines per request.

The csv format starts with a header row. Its transaction_name column holds the transaction’s name only when it was set with set_name_for_transaction_and_requests; a name set with set_name, or no name as in this example, leaves it empty, and its raw column holds the debug output of the GooseRawRequest. The first two requests of the same example look like:

elapsed,scenario_index,scenario_name,transaction_index,transaction_name,raw,name,final_url,redirected,response_time,status_code,success,update,user,error,coordinated_omission_elapsed,user_cadence,response_time_us
0,0,websiteuser,0,,"GooseRawRequest { method: Post, url: ""http://127.0.0.1:8080/login"", headers: [""(\""content-type\"", \""application/x-www-form-urlencoded\"")""], body: ""username=test_user&password="", custom_method: """" }",/login,http://127.0.0.1:8080/login,false,0,200,true,false,0,,0,0,756
0,0,websiteuser,1,,"GooseRawRequest { method: Get, url: ""http://127.0.0.1:8080/"", headers: [], body: """", custom_method: """" }",/,http://127.0.0.1:8080/,false,0,200,true,false,0,,0,0,188

The transaction_name field is an object naming how the transaction was named: {"TransactionOnly":"..."} for a name set with set_name, which only the transaction carries, or {"InheritNameByRequests":"..."} for a name set with set_name_for_transaction_and_requests, which its requests take too. Goose 0.18 wrote it as a plain string, and wrote the transaction’s name in the CSV column however it was set.

response_time is in whole milliseconds, truncated. The json, raw and pretty formats also carry response_time_fraction_us, the microseconds past those milliseconds (0 to 999), and the csv format ends with a response_time_us column holding the whole time in microseconds. In the first JSON line above the /login request took 1.221 milliseconds, and in the third the /about/ request took 632 microseconds. In the first CSV row the /login request took 756 microseconds.

Transaction Log

Goose can optionally log details about each time a transaction is run during a load test. To enable, add the --transaction-log <transaction.log> command line option, where <transaction.log> is either a relative or absolute path of the log file to create. Any existing file that may already exist will be overwritten.

Logs include the entire TransactionMetric object which is created each time any transaction is run.

Log Format

By default, logs are written in JSON Lines format. For example:

{"elapsed":0,"scenario_index":0,"transaction_index":0,"name":"","run_time":0,"success":true,"user":0,"run_time_fraction_us":334,"error":""}
{"elapsed":0,"scenario_index":0,"transaction_index":1,"name":"","run_time":0,"success":true,"user":0,"run_time_fraction_us":92,"error":""}
{"elapsed":6172,"scenario_index":0,"transaction_index":2,"name":"","run_time":1,"success":true,"user":0,"run_time_fraction_us":41,"error":""}
{"elapsed":8600,"scenario_index":0,"transaction_index":2,"name":"","run_time":0,"success":true,"user":2,"run_time_fraction_us":257,"error":""}

These lines are from the simple example, whose transactions have no names, run with -u 4 -r 4 -t 12s against a local server. In the first line, GooseUser thread 0 successfully ran transaction 0, the login, in 334 microseconds. In the third line, the same thread successfully ran transaction 2, the /about/ page, in 1.041 milliseconds.

Each line ends with an error field. It is empty when the transaction succeeded. When the transaction returned an error, success is false and error holds the error as its Display writes it. This line is from a load test whose flaky transaction returns TransactionError::Custom("every third call fails") one time in three:

{"elapsed":0,"scenario_index":0,"transaction_index":2,"name":"flaky","run_time":0,"success":false,"user":0,"run_time_fraction_us":68,"error":"TransactionError: custom error (every third call fails)"}

A transaction that failed because a request made with error_on_fail, or marked failed with set_failure, failed names the request error in error, as request failed: <method> <name>: <error>; the request error itself is counted in the errors table and the error log. A transaction canceled as the run ended has no error. A transaction that returns a TransactionError::Custom or Url error, or a Reqwest error from reading the response body or building the request, is counted, by scenario, transaction and error, in the Transaction Errors tables of the metrics and reports, and is written to the error log. Any other error, such as a Reqwest error from sending a request or from an error status, is named only in this log.

The --transaction-format option can be used to log in csv, json (default), raw or pretty format. The raw format is Rust’s debug output of the entire TransactionMetric object, one per line, and pretty is the same output spread over several indented lines per transaction. In each of them the error field comes last:

TransactionMetric { elapsed: 2, scenario_index: 0, transaction_index: 2, name: "flaky", run_time: 0, success: false, user: 0, run_time_fraction_us: 101, error: "TransactionError: custom error (every third call fails)" }

For example, csv output of the simple example starts with a header row and looks like:

elapsed,scenario_index,transaction_index,name,run_time,success,user,run_time_us,error
0,0,0,,0,true,0,455,
0,0,1,,0,true,0,143,
251,0,0,,0,true,1,471,
251,0,1,,0,true,1,163,
7872,0,2,,0,true,1,220,

The error column is last, and is empty for a transaction that succeeded. A failed transaction fills it:

0,0,2,flaky,0,false,0,77,TransactionError: custom error (every third call fails)

run_time is in whole milliseconds, truncated. The json, raw and pretty formats also carry run_time_fraction_us, the microseconds past those milliseconds (0 to 999), and the csv format has a run_time_us column, before error, holding the whole time in microseconds.

Scenario Log

Goose can optionally log details about each time a scenario is run during a load test. To enable, add the --scenario-log <scenario.log> command line option, where <scenario.log> is either a relative or absolute path of the log file to create. Any existing file that may already exist will be overwritten.

Logs include the entire ScenarioMetric object which is created each time any scenario is run.

Log Format

By default, logs are written in JSON Lines format. For example:

{"elapsed":20926,"name":"WebsiteUser","index":0,"run_time":20756,"user":1,"run_time_fraction_us":471}
{"elapsed":21402,"name":"WebsiteUser","index":0,"run_time":21064,"user":2,"run_time_fraction_us":471}
{"elapsed":22791,"name":"WebsiteUser","index":0,"run_time":22285,"user":3,"run_time_fraction_us":334}
{"elapsed":24811,"name":"WebsiteUser","index":0,"run_time":24810,"user":0,"run_time_fraction_us":432}

These lines are from the simple example. In the first line, GooseUser thread 1 ran the complete WebsiteUser scenario in 20,756.471 milliseconds, which includes the wait after each transaction. In the fourth line, GooseUser thread 0 ran it in 24,810.432 milliseconds.

The --scenario-format option can be used to log in csv, json (default), raw or pretty format. The raw format is Rust’s debug output of the entire ScenarioMetric object, one per line, and pretty is the same output spread over several indented lines per scenario run.

For example, csv output of the same example starts with a header row and looks like:

elapsed,name,index,run_time,user,run_time_us
19283,WebsiteUser,0,18945,2,18945361
19724,WebsiteUser,0,18882,5,18882239
20468,WebsiteUser,0,20467,0,20467323
24027,WebsiteUser,0,23857,1,23857874

run_time is in whole milliseconds, truncated. The json, raw and pretty formats also carry run_time_fraction_us, the microseconds past those milliseconds (0 to 999), and the csv format ends with a run_time_us column holding the whole time in microseconds.

Error Log

Goose can optionally log details about all load test errors to a file. To enable, add the --error-log=<error.log> command line option, where <error.log> is either a relative or absolute path of the log file to create. Any existing file that may already exist will be overwritten.

The log holds two kinds of entries:

  • a request error: the entire GooseErrorMetric object, created any time a request results in an error, including a request the load test marks as failed with set_failure;
  • a transaction error: a GooseTransactionErrorMetric, created each time a transaction returns an error that is not already a request error: a TransactionError::Custom or Url, or a Reqwest error from reading the response body or building the request. A transaction that fails because one of its requests failed, as with set_failure, gets no entry of its own: the request error has one. It has elapsed, scenario_index, scenario_name (the machine name, as in the request log), transaction_index, transaction_name, run_time, user and error, the error as its Display writes it. Errors from test_start and test_stop are not logged.

The raw.headers field of a request error follows the same rule as in the request log: it lists only the headers set on the individual request.

Log Format

By default, logs are written in JSON Lines format. For example:

{"elapsed":0,"scenario_index":0,"scenario_name":"erruser","transaction_index":2,"transaction_name":"flaky","run_time":0,"user":0,"error":"TransactionError: custom error (every third call fails)"}
{"elapsed":0,"raw":{"method":"Get","url":"http://127.0.0.1:8080/about/","headers":[],"body":"","custom_method":""},"name":"/about/","final_url":"http://127.0.0.1:8080/about/","redirected":false,"response_time":0,"status_code":404,"user":0,"error":"404 Not Found: /about/"}

These lines are from a load test run against a local server that answers /about/ with a 404. Its ErrUser scenario requests / and /about/, and its flaky transaction returns TransactionError::Custom("every third call fails") one time in three. The first line is a transaction error, the second a request error.

The --error-format option can be used to change the log format to csv, json (default), raw or pretty format. The raw format is Rust’s debug output of the entire GooseErrorMetric or GooseTransactionErrorMetric object, one per line, and pretty is the same output spread over several indented lines per error.

The csv format starts with a header row. Its first nine columns are a request error’s fields, and its raw column holds the debug output of the GooseRawRequest. The last five columns, scenario_index, scenario_name, transaction_index, transaction_name and run_time, are a transaction error’s. A transaction error fills elapsed, user, error and those five, leaving the other request columns empty, and a request error leaves the last five empty, so every row has every column:

elapsed,raw,name,final_url,redirected,response_time,status_code,user,error,scenario_index,scenario_name,transaction_index,transaction_name,run_time
0,,,,,,,0,TransactionError: custom error (every third call fails),0,erruser,2,flaky,0
0,"GooseRawRequest { method: Get, url: ""http://127.0.0.1:8080/about/"", headers: [], body: """", custom_method: """" }",/about/,http://127.0.0.1:8080/about/,false,0,404,0,404 Not Found: /about/,,,,,

--no-error-summary leaves errors out of the errors tables of the metrics and reports, not out of this log.

Debug Log

Goose can optionally and efficiently log arbitrary details, and specifics about requests and responses for debug purposes.

To enable, add the --debug-log <debug.log> command line option, where <debug.log> is either a relative or absolute path of the log file to create. Any existing file that may already exist will be overwritten.

If --debug-log <foo> is not specified at run time, nothing will be logged and there is no measurable overhead in your load test.

Response bodies are included unless --no-debug-body is also given, which keeps the debug log small.

To write to the debug log, you must invoke log_debug from your load test transaction functions. The tag parameter allows you to record any arbitrary string: it can also identify where in the load test the log was generated, and/or why debug is being written, and/or other details such as the contents of a form the load test posts. Other parameters that can be included in the debug log are the complete Request that was made, as well as the Headers and Body of the Response.

(The recorded request headers are only those set on the individual request: client default headers, the User-Agent, cookies, Accept-Encoding, Host and Content-Length are added later by Reqwest and are not recorded, see the request log and https://github.com/tag1consulting/goose/issues/336.)

See examples/drupal_memcache for an example of how you might invoke log_debug from a load test.

Request Failures

Calls to set_failure can be used to tell Goose that a request failed even though the server returned a successful status code, and will automatically invoke log_debug for you. See examples/drupal_memcache and examples/umami for an example of how you might use set_failure to generate useful debug logs.

Log Format

By default, logs are written in JSON Lines format. For example:

{"tag":"login: no form_build_id on page: /user page","request":{"elapsed":333,"scenario_index":1,"scenario_name":"authbrowsinguser","transaction_index":0,"transaction_name":{"TransactionOnly":"(Auth) login"},"raw":{"method":"Get","url":"http://127.0.0.1:8080/user","headers":[],"body":"","custom_method":""},"name":"/user","final_url":"http://127.0.0.1:8080/user","redirected":false,"response_time":0,"status_code":200,"success":false,"update":true,"user":1,"error":"login: no form_build_id on page: /user page","coordinated_omission_elapsed":0,"user_cadence":0,"response_time_fraction_us":625},"header":"{\"server\": \"BaseHTTP Python/3.15.0\", \"date\": \"Sun, 25 Oct 2026 20:57:26 GMT\", \"content-type\": \"text/html; charset=utf-8\", \"content-length\": \"86\"}","body":"<!doctype html><html><head><title>user</title></head><body><h1>user</h1></body></html>"}

This line is from the drupal_memcache example run against a server whose login page has no form_build_id, so the login transaction called set_failure. The header field is the response headers as one string, Rust’s debug output of the header map, and header, request and body are null when the call passed none.

The --debug-format option can be used to log in csv, json (default), raw or pretty format. The raw format is Rust’s debug output of the entire GooseDebug object, one per line, and pretty is the same output spread over several indented lines per entry. The csv format starts with the header row tag,request,header,body; its request, header and body columns hold Rust’s debug output of each optional value, such as Some(...) or None.

Controlling A Running Goose Load Test

There are three ways to watch or change a load test while it runs: the Live Dashboard and two Controllers.

  • The Live Dashboard is a page in your browser that shows the test as it runs and saves its report, and, with control turned on, starts and stops it and changes its users and settings. Pick it to watch a test, or to steer one by hand.
  • The telnet Controller takes typed commands. Pick it for config and metrics at a prompt, a test plan typed by hand, or shutting Goose down mid run.
  • The WebSocket Controller takes the same commands as JSON. Pick it to drive Goose from a program.

What each can do

Live DashboardTelnetWebSocket
ObserveLive charts, KPIs and tablesmetrics, metrics-jsonmetrics, metrics-json
Start, stopWith control onstart, stopstart, stop
UsersWith control onusersusers
Host and rampsWith control on, in the Edit settings formhost, increaserate, increase-time, decreaserate, decrease-timethe same commands
Test planWith control on, through POST /api/v1/control/settings or POST /api/v1/control/start; the form has no plan fieldtest-plantest-plan
Run timeWith control on, in the Edit settings formruntimeruntime
Save reportOn by default with --dashboard; turning it off and Save report now need controlNoNo
configNoconfig, config-jsonconfig, config-json
Shut Goose downWith control on: Quit, from Idle onlyshutdown, from any phaseshutdown, from any phase
DefaultOff; --dashboard turns it on, on 127.0.0.1:5118On, on 127.0.0.1:5116Off; --websocket turns it on, on 127.0.0.1:5117
AuthenticationRead-only on loopback: none, unless a token is set. Control, or any bind off loopback: a token, or --dashboard-no-authNoneNone

The dashboard is read-only unless control is turned on with --dashboard-control and a token or --dashboard-no-auth; see Turning it on. It needs Goose built with the dashboard feature.

The telnet and WebSocket Controllers

By default, Goose launches a telnet Controller thread that listens on 127.0.0.1:5116. With --websocket it also launches a WebSocket Controller thread that listens on 127.0.0.1:5117; that one is off by default, as any web page a browser opens could reach it. Neither takes any authentication, so anyone who can reach their ports can start, stop and reconfigure the load test, which is why both listen only on loopback unless told otherwise. To control the load test from another machine, forward the port over ssh, or name a host with --telnet-host or --websocket-host, such as 0.0.0.0; a Controller listening on any address that is not loopback prints a warning at startup. Turn the telnet Controller off with --no-telnet.

Goose can be started with the --no-autostart option to keep the load test from starting on its own. A Controller’s start command then starts it, as does the dashboard’s Start under --dashboard-control. Goose refuses --no-autostart when neither is available. When Goose is started this way, a host is not required and can instead be configured through a Controller or the dashboard. A load test that a Controller or the dashboard starts again after it stopped counts its requests from zero.

With --telnet-host 0.0.0.0, Goose starts and writes this warning straight to standard error, where neither -q nor the log level hides it, and to the Goose log at warn level; the WebSocket Controller’s warning names --websocket-host and port 5117:

==============================================================================
WARNING: the Telnet Controller on 0.0.0.0:5116 has no authentication.
Anyone who can reach this port can start, stop and reconfigure the load test.
Safer: leave --telnet-host unset to listen on 127.0.0.1 only, and use an
ssh forward (ssh -L 5116:127.0.0.1:5116 host).
==============================================================================

A Controller that can’t listen on its port, because another process already holds it, logs an error and the load test runs without it:

22:10:32 [ERROR] [controller]: the Telnet Controller couldn't listen on 127.0.0.1:5116: Address already in use (os error 48); the load test runs without it

With --no-autostart, if no Controller could listen and dashboard control is off, nothing could start the load test, so Goose refuses to run:

Error: InvalidOption { option: "`configuration.no_autostart`", value: "true", detail: "`configuration.no_autostart` requires a Controller or dashboard control, and no Controller could listen on its port." }

Live Dashboard

The live dashboard is a web page Goose serves while a load test runs. It shows the test’s users, requests, response times and errors second by second. It saves each run’s report, and lists saved runs to download and compare. With control turned on it can also start and stop the test, and change its users, host, ramps and run time.

The first screen of a run with control on, explained section by section below, with the truncation notice under The tables.

Build Goose with the dashboard feature, add --dashboard, and open http://127.0.0.1:5118/. In a checkout of Goose, against your own Umami site:

cargo run --release --features dashboard --example umami -- \
  --dashboard -H http://umami.example.com -u 100 -r 1

Most images on this page come from one run of the command above, against a local Umami site at http://localhost:18888: the first image, the charts, the heatmap, the Requests table, the Control card with Edit settings open, and the Runs panel. That run added --dashboard-control --dashboard-no-auth for control, -t 10m for a 10 minute hold, --no-reset-metrics so the tables include the ramp up, --notes "100, then 150 users", which the header shows, and -q to keep the console quiet. The read-only and Errors images come from runs of their own, and their captions say what changed.

Turning it on

The dashboard is an opt in crate feature. In your own load test crate, turn it on in Cargo.toml, as Requirements shows:

goose = { version = "^0.19", features = ["dashboard"] }

Then run your load test with --dashboard, like any other option:

cargo run --release -- --dashboard -H https://staging.example.com

A build without the feature refuses --dashboard at startup. The dashboard listens on 127.0.0.1:5118; Dashboard Reference lists the flags that change that.

--dashboard alone gives a read-only dashboard. Its Control card says so, names the two ways to get control, and points to the telnet Controller, which Goose starts by default and which takes no authentication:

With --dashboard alone the card can only point elsewhere: to a restart with control, or to the telnet Controller listening on 127.0.0.1:5116.

To control the test from the page as well, add --dashboard-control and one of:

  • --dashboard-auth-token <token>, then open http://127.0.0.1:5118/?token=<token>. See Browser bootstrap with ?token=.
  • --dashboard-no-auth, for control with no token, described next.

Control without a token

With --dashboard-control --dashboard-no-auth the page reads Live dashboard · control, no token. Anyone who can reach the dashboard’s port can then start and stop the test, point the load at any host, and delete saved reports. On a shared machine use a token instead, set in the load test’s code so ps does not show it; On a shared machine shows how, and what else is open there. Control without a token in the reference gives its rules, among them the warning Goose prints off loopback.

What changes, and what doesn’t

  • The dashboard saves each run’s report.html and report.json in a run folder, goose-runs/<start time> under the working directory by default; see Saving the report. Goose still writes the reports it is asked for (PDF, HTML, Markdown and JSON), and prints the same metrics to the console, with a few lines more about the dashboard.
  • Watching adds no requests to the target. --dashboard costs Goose memory for the per second graph data, whether or not a page is open, and a little CPU for each snapshot, built about once a second while a page is open; Memory cost gives the numbers.

Watching from another machine

Keep the dashboard on loopback and use an SSH tunnel, or bind another address with --dashboard-host and a token. On a shared machine, set a token either way, in the load test’s code: a loopback dashboard with no token is open to every user of that machine. With a token set, the token goes in the URL whether or not control is on: http://127.0.0.1:5118/?token=<token> through the tunnel, or http://<address>:5118/?token=<token> on a bind off loopback. Bind and token rules says what each choice exposes, and to whom.

What it shows

The header shows the phase (idle, increase, maintain, decrease or shutdown), the hosts under load, how long the test has run, its --notes, and the connection, SSE with a green dot while the page is streaming. With control off it also shows the run settings, such as the test plan. Under the header, the report bar holds the report switches, described under Save, compare and delete runs. Below it and the Control card, the KPI row shows the active users against the target, then requests per second, the failure rate, the p95 and the average response time; all but the users cover the whole run, or the time since metrics were last reset, as The tables explains.

Active users

Users ramp to 100 at one a second, hold, then ramp to 150 after Target was set to 150 at 120 seconds, and hold again; the axis counts seconds since the test started.

Each point is one second. The charts keep the last 300 seconds of the run, so these, taken 200 seconds in, show all of it; on a longer run the start scrolls off, and the reports keep the whole run. The summary under each line chart is the mean of its last 10 seconds (the requests per second chart leaves out the newest second, still filling), with rising or falling when that is more than 10 percent above or below the 10 seconds before, and steady otherwise, once there are 20 seconds of data. So in the first image, taken as the ramp ended, the summary under the users chart reads Users 96 of 100 while the Users tile already reads 100 / 100. The step to 150 came from the Control card while the test ran, with no restart.

Requests per second

Requests per second climb with the users to about 1,000 by 100 seconds, then stop climbing; the dashed Failures/s line stays at zero.

Against the users chart, this shows the site at capacity: from 120 to 170 seconds users went from 100 to 150 and requests per second did not rise. The summary under the chart reads RPS 950.50.

The response time heatmap

From about 124 seconds, as users pass about 104, the p95 line climbs from about 20 ms to several hundred and the p99 rises to mostly between 500 ms and 1 second, while the p50 stays near 8 ms.

The card, titled Response times by second, draws each second as a column, oldest on the left, and each row as a range of response times on a logarithmic axis; a darker cell holds more requests. Three lines cross it: each second’s p50, p95 and p99. The newest two seconds are not drawn yet, as their slowest requests are still arriving. The labels at the right edge show the newest drawn second, here p95: 335 ms, and the summary under the chart the mean of the last 10 drawn seconds, p95 638 ms. Hover a cell for its second, its range, its count and that second’s percentiles, and pick a request at the top of the card to see it alone. The response time heatmap in the reference gives the rows, the color scale and how each percentile is worked out.

The tables

Below the charts, Summary gives the phase, how long the test has run, the users and total users, the Goose version, the hosts and the series window (the 300 seconds the charts keep), and Whole run the totals and response time percentiles. Scenarios lists every scenario with its users, how many times it ran and how long it took. Transactions lists every transaction with its runs, failures and times, and Requests each request with its counts, times and status codes:

Sorted by p95 with one click on its heading, the table puts the Spanish search, POST /es/search/node, first at 212 ms over the whole run, and scrolls right for p99, max and the status codes.

The table’s p95 covers the whole run, so the fast first two minutes weigh in; the heatmap’s lines are each one second.

Click a heading to sort by it. Every figure covers the whole run, or the time since metrics were last reset: as in a console run, Goose resets them when the ramp up ends, unless --no-reset-metrics or --iterations is given, or a test plan runs (see Metrics). Goose sends the dashboard only the 100 busiest request names. A site with more, such as Umami, shows the notice request rows truncated (showing top rows only). above the Control card, and the table lists those 100, so a slow request that is rarely made may not be among them. In the run above, the admin user’s login and article edits, a few requests each, are left out, though the login and the saved edits are slower than the Spanish search. The reports list every request.

Errors

From a run with ADMIN_PASSWORD set to a wrong password: the admin user’s login fails, its row names the cause, and each article the admin then tries to edit answers 403.

Each row is one error, with its request and its count, and rows appear while the run is going; the table holds the 50 most frequent, and a notice at the top of the page says when there are more. Transaction errors, below it, counts errors that a transaction’s own code returned, other than failed requests.

What you can do with it

Stop, Start, Quit, changing users or settings, the report switches, Save report now and Delete need control on. Saved runs can be downloaded and compared without it.

Stop a run that is erroring

Errors show first in the Fail % tile and as the dashed red Failures/s line in the requests per second chart, at zero in the run behind the charts above. The Errors table, below Requests, says what they are. When a run is failing, press Stop rather than waiting for it to end: the users stop at once, and Goose writes the run’s reports, as at the end of any run. See Start and Stop semantics.

Change users and settings

Type a count in Target and press Apply, or press − or + to send a change of one step at once. Goose ramps up to a higher count at the increase rate, as the change to 150 above shows, and down to a lower one at the decrease rate, or the increase rate when no decrease rate is set. An increase with no increase rate set, and a decrease with neither rate set, go at 1 user a second. A change of users never uses --increase-time or --decrease-time. A change made during the ramp up starts the hold once the new count is reached, for the full run time; a change made during the hold leaves its end where it was. The planned ramp down follows, unless the new ramp outlasts the hold, or the new count is the one the run already has: then the run holds that count until stopped. Once the ramp down has started, a new count abandons it, and Goose holds the new count until stopped. See Users on a running test.

In Idle after the charts’ run was stopped, and with a new host, ramp up and run time pending: Start reads Start with changes, the new host waits for Confirm, and the note under Target says a change of users would go with them.

Edit settings opens a form for the host, the ramp up and ramp down, and the run time. An edit stays pending until you press Apply settings, or until Start with changes starts the next run with it. The first change of host in a page load asks for confirmation before anything is sent. While a test runs, the form takes only what the run can still change, such as the run time, and says why for the rest. In Idle with settings edits pending, Target’s Apply sends nothing on its own, and − and + only change the Target count: the count goes with those edits when you press Apply settings or Start with changes. See Run settings.

Start a run

Start Goose with --no-autostart and it waits in Idle for Start; after a Stop it waits the same way. With settings edits pending, the button reads Start with changes and sends them with the start, as in the Control card above. The card shows Quit, which shuts Goose down, only in Idle, so stop a running test first.

Save, compare and delete runs

The report bar at the top of the page holds two switches. Save report sets whether this run’s report is written to its folder, and Include graph data adds the per second data to the JSON report. With control on, the switches can be changed, and the bar also holds Save report now, which writes the report mid run; without control the switches are shown read only. --report-graph-data turns Include graph data on from the command line. Without control, Save report stays on, except under --no-metrics or when the run’s folder can’t be written to.

The Runs panel at the foot of the page lists the saved reports in goose-runs, the newest 50 first, with Show all for the rest. With --output-dir <dir>, which names the run’s own folder, it lists the folder that holds <dir> instead. The list also includes console runs made without the dashboard, when they wrote to a run folder: a run given a bare --report-file, with no file name, writes report.html and report.json there (see Output Files).

Three saved runs: two from console runs given a bare --report-file, and the newest from the dashboard; the two newest are checked, so Download comparison appears.

Each row downloads its HTML, Markdown or JSON report; a format the run did not save is rendered from its JSON report. Check Compare on two rows and Download comparison gives a Markdown report of the newer run against the older. With control on, Delete removes a run’s reports and logs. See Saving the report and The runs panel.

When the run ends

When a run Goose started on its own (without --no-autostart) reaches its end, Goose exits. That is when its run time and any ramp down are over, its test plan finishes, or every user exits. If a page is connected at that moment and the report was saved, Goose stays up instead, so the report can still be downloaded, and prints:

Run finished. Download it from the dashboard at http://127.0.0.1:5118. Press Ctrl-C to exit.

With control on, it ends Press Quit in the dashboard or Ctrl-C to exit. Closing the tab does not end this wait; Ctrl-C, a Controller shutdown and Quit do.

A Stop leaves Goose in Idle with the page up, ready for the next run, and a run started from the dashboard or a Controller goes back to Idle when it ends. During a run, Ctrl-C, the killswitch, a Controller shutdown or a threshold that aborts the run ends both the run and Goose, with no wait. Once Goose exits, the page says Goose has exited. See Waiting after an autostarted run.

Reference

Dashboard Reference lists every flag and default, the bind and token rules, the control endpoints, the snapshot and health endpoints, and how to build the page from its TypeScript sources.

Dashboard Reference

This page holds every flag, rule and endpoint of the Live Dashboard, and the snapshot fields a client most needs. Start there for what the dashboard shows and how to use it.

The dashboard is an opt in crate feature: build Goose with --features dashboard. Stability says what the dashboard and its /api/v1 endpoints promise from one release to the next.

Goose can optionally serve a live web dashboard while a load test runs. The dashboard streams compact metric snapshots (including trailing RPS, users, and latency series) over Server-Sent Events (SSE), with a short poll fallback if SSE is unavailable.

Enabling the dashboard

The dashboard is off by default. Compile Goose with the dashboard crate feature, then enable observation with --dashboard:

cargo run --release --features dashboard --example simple -- \
  --dashboard \
  -H https://staging.example.com \
  -u 50 -t 10m

Then open:

http://127.0.0.1:5118/

The dashboard port opens when the Controllers’ ports do, once Goose has built a client for every user, which takes longer with more users and fewer cores. With --no-autostart users are built on Start, so the port opens at once.

Related flags

FlagDefaultPurpose
--dashboardoffEnable the live dashboard HTTP server (observe)
--dashboard-controloffEnable the control endpoints and UI: Start, Stop, users, the run settings (which can point the load at any host), Quit, the report switches, Save report now and Delete of saved reports and logs (requires --dashboard; does not auto-enable the dashboard)
--dashboard-host HOST127.0.0.1Bind address. A loopback address needs no token for metrics; any other needs a token or --dashboard-no-auth; see Bind and token rules
--dashboard-port PORT5118Bind port. 0 picks a free port, which the startup log names: [dashboard]: listening on http://127.0.0.1:60096 (read-only).
--dashboard-auth-token TOKENemptyShared secret for metric APIs when configured; required when --dashboard-control is set (even on loopback), and for any bind off loopback, unless --dashboard-no-auth is. Visible in process listings (ps) like any CLI flag; set it in the load test’s code with GooseDefault::DashboardAuthToken to keep it out of them.
--dashboard-no-authoffAllow dashboard access and control without a token. Anyone who can reach the port can watch the test and download saved reports, and with --dashboard-control start, stop and point the load at any host, and delete saved reports and logs; Goose warns at startup with control on loopback, and on anything but loopback. Needs --dashboard; refused with --dashboard-auth-token. See Control without a token.
--dashboard-max-clients N32Max concurrent SSE clients (GET /api/v1/events); further clients receive HTTP 503. 0 is refused at startup (must be at least 1). The server also holds at most twice this many connections plus 16 (80 by default); a connection beyond that is closed as soon as it is accepted.
--no-percentiles-over-timeoffTurn off the response time heatmap and the histograms behind it; the card shows the average latency chart instead. It also turns off the HTML report’s response time heatmap, and works without --dashboard. See The response time heatmap.

Defaults can also be set programmatically with GooseDefault::Dashboard, GooseDefault::DashboardControl, GooseDefault::DashboardNoAuth, GooseDefault::DashboardHost, GooseDefault::DashboardPort, GooseDefault::DashboardAuthToken, GooseDefault::DashboardMaxClients, and GooseDefault::NoPercentilesOverTime.

Feature flag: the HTTP server is compiled behind the opt-in dashboard crate feature (not in default features, same pattern as pdf-reports). Enable it with --features dashboard. Builds without that feature reject --dashboard at startup with a clear rebuild hint.

The server lives as long as the load test, with one exception: see Waiting after an autostarted run. When GooseAttack::execute() returns, whether the run finished or failed, connected browsers receive event: closed, open connections get up to one second to finish, and the listener and every connection are closed before execute() returns, so a program can run another GooseAttack on the same port right away.

Observe vs control at a glance

CapabilityLive Dashboard (observe)Live Dashboard + controlControllers (telnet / WebSocket)
Live rates, percentiles, errorsYesYesmetrics / metrics-json
Trailing RPS / users / latency chartsYesYesNo
Start / stop / set usersNoYesYes
Host / ramps / run timeNoYesYes
Test planNoThrough POST /api/v1/control/settings or POST /api/v1/control/start onlyYes (test-plan)
config / metricsNoNoYes
Process shutdownNoQuit, from Idle onlyYes (shutdown, any phase)
Default bind127.0.0.1:5118127.0.0.1:5118127.0.0.1:5116 / :5117, WebSocket only with --websocket
AuthToken on metric APIs when configuredToken for control unless --dashboard-no-auth; metrics follow observe rulesNone (see Controller docs)

When control is off, POST /api/v1/control/* returns 404 (routes are not registered). Prefer Controllers for config, metrics and shutting down a running test; use dashboard control for Start, Stop, users and the run settings from a browser.

Bind and token rules

On a shared machine

Every user of the machine running Goose can reach what Goose serves on loopback. This is what each of them can do, and how to close it:

WhoWhat they can reachTo close it
Any local processThe telnet Controller, on by default on 127.0.0.1:5116 with no authentication: start, stop, change users, the host, the ramps, the run time and the test plan, read config and metrics, and shut Goose down--no-telnet
Any local process, or any page in a local browserThe WebSocket Controller, when --websocket turns it on; it takes no authentication and accepts a connection from any web pageLeave --websocket off
Any local user, with no token setThe dashboard’s metrics (request names, hosts, error strings, rates) and the run’s details (its notes, its --report-file paths and its run settings: host, ramps, run time and test plan): snapshots, the event stream, the per request heatmap, and every saved report in the runs folder, to list and download. By holding the event stream open when an autostarted run ends, they also keep Goose and its port up until it is told to quit (Waiting after an autostarted run)A token, set in code as below
Any local user, under --dashboard-no-auth with --dashboard-controlEverything in the row above, and Start, Stop, users, the run settings (including the host the load goes to), Quit, the report switches, Save report now, and Delete of saved reports and logsA token in place of --dashboard-no-auth
Any local user, with a token setThe page’s HTML and static files, and GET /api/v1/health: among other fields, the Goose version, whether control is on, the Controllers’ addresses and how many pages are watching. They can also fill the connection cap, which every client shares, token or not: a user who keeps 2 × --dashboard-max-clients + 16 connections open, sending a request on each at least every 10 seconds, keeps your browser out until they stopThe metrics are already closed. No setting closes the lockout: run where you trust the other users, or behind a firewall when the bind is off loopback
Any local user, through the file systemThe run folders under goose-runs (reports and logs), which Goose writes as any file, with the process’s umask, so a usual umask lets every local user read themRun Goose under umask 077, or keep the runs under a folder only you can read, such as --output-dir ~/private/nightly with ~/private at mode 700: a second run with the same --output-dir, once that folder holds files, writes a new folder beside it, nightly-2
Any local user, with the token on the command lineThe token itself, in process listings (ps)Set it in the load test’s code with GooseDefault::DashboardAuthToken, as below

With the dashboard off loopback, “local” in the dashboard rows of this table becomes everyone who can reach its port (the telnet and WebSocket Controllers keep their own binds), and the token and the metrics cross the network as unencrypted http.

To keep the token off the command line, read it in the load test’s code, for example from an environment variable, and set it as a default. Make the token long and random, such as the output of openssl rand -hex 32: Goose does not limit wrong guesses on any route, and logs them only on the control routes and Delete. An empty token counts as none, so refuse one, as this does:

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
let token = std::env::var("DASHBOARD_TOKEN")
    .ok()
    .filter(|token| !token.is_empty())
    .expect("set DASHBOARD_TOKEN to a non-empty value");
GooseAttack::initialize()?
    .set_default(GooseDefault::DashboardAuthToken, token.as_str())?
    // register scenarios and run as before
}

A token written into the source instead is readable by anyone who can read the source or the binary. The token can also turn up in other places: in a reverse proxy’s access log, as ?token= on the first page load (/?token=) and on the page’s metric URLs (see Security notes); in a browser tab duplicated or restored from the one that opened the dashboard (see Browser bootstrap with ?token=); and in curl command lines, which ps shows like any other (see curl examples). An export DASHBOARD_TOKEN=... typed at a shell also lands in its history; read -rs DASHBOARD_TOKEN && export DASHBOARD_TOKEN in bash or zsh sets it without echoing it or writing it there. On Linux a process’s environment, unlike its arguments, is readable only by its own user and root.

The token also goes to whatever listens on the dashboard’s port. If another user holds that port when Goose binds it, after building its users or at once with --no-autostart, Goose logs [dashboard]: failed to bind ...: ... (is the port already in use?) and exits, and the other process keeps the port: a tunnel or a reloaded tab then sends your token to it. So does a tab left open after Goose stops without a clean exit (killed or crashed): the page keeps retrying the event stream and polling, with the token, until it gets through. Send the token only while your own Goose is up, as its listening on line shows. That is an info line, which -q hides; under -q, check that lsof -iTCP:5118 -sTCP:LISTEN names your own Goose process before you send the token. Close the dashboard tab once Goose is gone, and pick another port with --dashboard-port when the default is taken.

So on a shared machine: keep the dashboard on loopback, set a token in the load test’s code, add --no-telnet unless you need the telnet Controller, leave --websocket off, run under umask 077 or keep the run folders under a folder only you can read, reach the dashboard from your own machine over an SSH tunnel, and send the token only once your own Goose holds the port, as above.

Loopback default and auth policy

By default the dashboard binds to loopback only (127.0.0.1). That is intentional: metrics snapshots include request names, error strings, hostnames and rates, and the run’s notes, --report-file paths and run settings; none of it should be world-readable.

Goose treats a bind host as loopback when it is:

  • localhost (case-insensitive, no DNS lookup)
  • any IPv4 address in 127.0.0.0/8 (for example 127.0.0.1, 127.0.0.2)
  • IPv6 loopback ::1 (with or without brackets: [::1])

Non-loopback binds, including unspecified addresses that listen on all interfaces (0.0.0.0, ::) and LAN addresses and hostnames, require a token at startup (--dashboard-auth-token or GooseDefault::DashboardAuthToken), unless --dashboard-no-auth is set. Goose refuses to start, with an error, if neither is given.

If a token is configured, it is enforced, even on loopback, on the metric APIs: every metric and report route that What requires a token lists.

On a loopback bind every request must carry a loopback Host header (localhost, an address in 127.0.0.0/8 or [::1], with any port), whatever the token setting; anything else, a missing Host included, gets 403 with the plain text body host not allowed. This covers every route, the page, its static files and /api/v1/health included. This stops DNS rebinding: a web page that points its own domain at 127.0.0.1 sends its own domain as Host, so it cannot read snapshots from a dashboard that has no token. A reverse proxy in front of a loopback bind must pass a loopback Host (nginx proxy_pass http://127.0.0.1:5118 does by default). Whether a bind is loopback is decided by the --dashboard-host value as written, by the list above. A hostname that resolves to a loopback address does not count as loopback: this Host check does not apply to it, and it needs a token like any other non-loopback bind.

Security matrix (observe vs control)

BindObserve (--dashboard)Control (--dashboard-control)
Loopback, no tokenMetrics OK without authStartup error (control needs a token or --dashboard-no-auth)
Loopback, token setMetrics require tokenControl requires token
Loopback, --dashboard-no-authMetrics OK without authControl open without a token; cross site requests refused
Non-loopback, no tokenStartup errorStartup error
Non-loopback, token setMetrics require tokenControl requires token
Non-loopback, --dashboard-no-authMetrics open; startup warning, page banner, Host ruleControl open without a token; startup warning, page banner, Host rule, cross site requests refused
Any bind, --dashboard-no-auth with --dashboard-auth-tokenStartup errorStartup error

“Metrics” in this table means every route the token guards when one is set: the snapshot, the event stream, the per request heatmap and the saved reports, which can be listed and downloaded. What requires a token lists them. Every loopback bind also checks Host on every route, as Loopback default and auth policy explains; the “Host rule” in the table is the check off loopback under --dashboard-no-auth, in Off loopback, and “cross site requests refused” is the check in Control without a token.

Additional control rules:

  • --dashboard-control without --dashboard → startup hard-fail (control does not auto-enable the dashboard). The same goes for --dashboard-no-auth without --dashboard.
  • Without --dashboard-no-auth, control endpoints always require a non-empty --dashboard-auth-token, even on loopback, so other local processes cannot POST start/stop/users without the secret. The telnet Controller, on by default, takes the same commands from any local process with no authentication; see On a shared machine.
  • Startup log shows capability at a glance: (read-only), (read-only, no token), (control enabled) or (control enabled, no token); see Startup lines.

Startup lines

Goose says at startup what the dashboard allows and how to get control. Read-only:

[dashboard]: listening on http://127.0.0.1:5118 (read-only)
[dashboard]: read-only; for control add --dashboard-control and either --dashboard-no-auth (no token) or --dashboard-auth-token <token>

With a token already set, the second line reads read-only; for control add --dashboard-control, as --dashboard-no-auth would then be refused.

Control with a token, which is never printed; <token> is the literal placeholder:

[dashboard]: listening on http://127.0.0.1:5118 (control enabled)
[dashboard]: open http://127.0.0.1:5118/?token=<token> to control the test

On an unspecified bind, --dashboard-host 0.0.0.0 or ::, the listening line names the bind address, and the line to open names 127.0.0.1 or [::1], which a browser on the same machine can open: open http://127.0.0.1:5118/?token=<token> to control the test. From another machine, use this machine’s address instead.

Control with --dashboard-no-auth on loopback, the second line at warn level:

[dashboard]: listening on http://127.0.0.1:5118 (control enabled, no token)
[dashboard]: --dashboard-no-auth: anyone on this machine can start, stop and redirect the test

Read-only with --dashboard-no-auth, the listening line reads (read-only, no token) and the second line read-only; for control add --dashboard-control. Off loopback, --dashboard-no-auth adds the warning in Control without a token.

Control without a token

--dashboard-no-auth lets the dashboard start, stop, quit and change users, the run settings and the report switches, save the report now, and delete saved reports and logs, with no token and no ?token= URL:

cargo run --release --features dashboard --example simple -- \
  --dashboard --dashboard-control --dashboard-no-auth \
  -H https://staging.example.com \
  -u 50 -t 10m

Open http://127.0.0.1:5118/. The header reads Live dashboard · control, no token, and the Control card works as with a token. On loopback anyone on the machine can use it, as with a read-only dashboard with no token; with control on, Goose warns at startup.

--dashboard-no-auth can’t be given with --dashboard-auth-token, and needs --dashboard. Without --dashboard-control it keeps the dashboard read-only and lifts the token rule for a non-loopback bind.

With no token, nothing but the browser’s own rules keeps another web page from sending the dashboard a request, so the control routes (POST /api/v1/control/* and DELETE /api/v1/reports/{name}) refuse what another site’s page can send. A request with an Origin other than http:// followed by its own Host, or without Content-Type: application/json, gets 403 with { "ok": false, "reason": "cross_origin" }. A form, a link and a plain fetch from another site can’t send that content type without the browser first asking the server, and Goose sends no CORS headers, so the browser never lets them. The dashboard page sends Content-Type: application/json on every control request, with {} as the body of a POST with nothing else to send; its Delete sends the header with no body. Behind a reverse proxy that serves the page over https, or that rewrites Host, the page’s Origin no longer matches, and every control request is refused this way.

Under --dashboard-no-auth, control works through a proxy only when the page’s Origin still matches the Host Goose sees.
