config             display load test configuration
config-json        display load test configuration in json format
metrics            display metrics for current load test
metrics-json       display metrics for current load test in json format
goose> q
goodbye!
goose> Connection closed by foreign host.

exit, quit and q end the session, and help and ? print this list. Commands and values are not case sensitive, and most commands take aliases: increase_rate and increase-rate for increaserate, increasetime for increase-time, run_time for runtime, testplan and plan for test-plan, stats for metrics, and the like. A command takes its value after one space.

config prints the configuration, and config-json prints it as JSON, with an option that is not set shown as null and an output flag given without a path shown as (the run's folder). Neither shows the dashboard auth token: one that is set reads (set). metrics prints the metrics tables, and metrics-json prints the metrics as JSON. In that JSON each request’s raw_data holds its response times in microsecond buckets, times_us, beside minimum_time_us, maximum_time_us and total_time_us, and the whole millisecond minimum_time, maximum_time, total_time and counter; each transaction and scenario holds the same as times_us, min_time_us, max_time_us and total_time_us, beside min_time, max_time, total_time and counter. Each request also holds status_code_counts, how many responses got each status code, and status_code_timings, each status code’s count, total_time_us, min_time_us and max_time_us.

A command whose value can’t be used is refused with what the value must be, and changes nothing:

goose> users 0
users must be an integer between 1 and 100000
goose> increaserate 0
increase_rate must be a number from 0.01 to 100000
goose> runtime 31d
run_time must be a time span such as 1h30m5s, from 1 second to 30 days, or 0 to clear

Telnet, WebSocket and the dashboard share these bounds:

SettingAccepted
hostAn http:// or https:// url with no user name or password, which would show to observers and in reports
users1 to 100000
increaserate, decreaserate0.01 to 100000 users per second
increase-time, decrease-time, runtime1 second to 30 days; 0 clears the setting, so Goose’s default applies (the dashboard takes null to clear, and refuses 0)
test-planAt most 100 steps of 0 seconds to 30 days each, in at most 4096 bytes, starting 1 to 100000 users in total

host, increase-time and decrease-time are accepted only while the load test is idle, as they are read when it starts. runtime, users and test-plan also change a running load test. A runtime on a running users ramp moves the end of the hold in progress to its start plus the new run time, and starts the ramp down at once if that has passed. If the new run time puts the end of the hold earlier than the end of the ramp from a users change made during the hold, the run holds until stopped. Before the hold starts, a runtime replaces the hold ahead, and on a run holding until stopped it holds from now for the new run time, then ramps down. runtime 0 on a running test holds until stopped, and is refused while a ramp down is configured. runtime is refused during the ramp down, while stopping and while a test plan runs, as the plan sets how long it runs. See Run time on a running test. A users change on a running users ramp keeps its run time and ramp down: it ramps to the new count, holds, and ramps down from the new count. A change during the ramp up holds for the full run time; a change during the hold keeps the hold’s end. If the new ramp outlasts the hold, or the new count is the one the run already has, it holds that count until stopped. A users change during the ramp down abandons it and holds the new count until stopped. users is refused while a test plan runs, as the plan sets users. See Users on a running test.

The rates are accepted in Idle and on a running users ramp, and are refused during its ramp down, while stopping and while a test plan runs. On a running test, a rate is refused while its own time is set, since a time changes only in Idle; in Idle a rate clears its own time and a time clears its own rate. A decrease rate on a running users ramp regenerates the ramp down still ahead, or adds one where all users would stop at once when the hold ends; an increase rate is for the next users increase.

A test plan sets users, so a rate or a time is refused in Idle with set users first to switch to a users ramp, and, while the plan runs, with a test plan is running. While one is configured, users switches to a users ramp and clears the plan, and test-plan switches back and clears users, the rates and the times. The command line’s rules also apply: runtime, decreaserate, decrease-time and test-plan are refused under --iterations, test-plan under --no-reset-metrics, and in Idle increase-time or decrease-time with fewer than 2 users, or users 1 while either is set. users and test-plan are refused while a stop, shutdown or Ctrl-C is ramping the load test down. A command that gets no answer within 15 seconds is answered control timed out, it may still apply: check before retrying: check the configuration or metrics before sending it again.

Example

One possible use-case for the controller is to dynamically reconfigure the number of users being simulated by the load test. In the following example, the load test was launched with the following parameters:

% cargo run --release --example umami -- --no-autostart --telnet-host 0.0.0.0 --host https://umami.ddev.site/ --increase-rate 50 --report-file report.html --report-file report.md

The --telnet-host 0.0.0.0 lets the telnet Controller be reached from another machine, here one that reaches the load test as loadtest, and prints the warning that it has no authentication.

Then the telnet controller is invoked as follows:

% telnet loadtest 5116
Trying loadtest...
Connected to loadtest.
Escape character is '^]'.
goose> start 
load test started
goose> users 20
users configured
goose> users 40
users configured
goose> users 80
users configured
goose> users 40 
users configured
goose> users 20
users configured
goose> users 160
users configured
goose> users 20
users configured
goose> increase_rate 5
increase_rate configured
goose> users 80
users configured
goose> users 20
users configured
goose> shutdown
load test shut down
goose> Connection closed by foreign host.

Initially the load test is configured with an increase rate of 50, so goose increases and decreases the user count by 50 user threads per second: with no decrease rate set, Goose lowers the user count at the increase rate. Later we reconfigure the increase rate to 5, slowing down the rate that goose alters the number of user threads. This graph, from the end of that load test, shows the result:

The above commands are also summarized in the plan overview of the Markdown report, report.md, here from a run of the same commands against a local server (the HTML report has the same table):

## Plan Overview

| Action | Started | Stopped | Elapsed | Users |
| ------ | ------- | ------- | ------- | ----: |
| Increasing | 26-10-25 22:12:31 | 26-10-25 22:12:31 | 00:00:00 | 0 &rarr; 18 |
| Maintaining | 26-10-25 22:12:31 | 26-10-25 22:12:43 | 00:00:12 | 18 |
| Increasing | 26-10-25 22:12:43 | 26-10-25 22:12:43 | 00:00:00 | 18 &rarr; 20 |
| Maintaining | 26-10-25 22:12:43 | 26-10-25 22:12:55 | 00:00:12 | 20 |
| Increasing | 26-10-25 22:12:55 | 26-10-25 22:12:56 | 00:00:01 | 20 &rarr; 40 |
| Maintaining | 26-10-25 22:12:56 | 26-10-25 22:13:07 | 00:00:11 | 40 |
| Increasing | 26-10-25 22:13:07 | 26-10-25 22:13:08 | 00:00:01 | 40 &rarr; 80 |
| Maintaining | 26-10-25 22:13:08 | 26-10-25 22:13:22 | 00:00:14 | 80 |
| Decreasing | 26-10-25 22:13:22 | 26-10-25 22:13:23 | 00:00:01 | 40 &larr; 80 |
| Maintaining | 26-10-25 22:13:23 | 26-10-25 22:13:34 | 00:00:11 | 40 |
| Decreasing | 26-10-25 22:13:34 | 26-10-25 22:13:34 | 00:00:00 | 20 &larr; 40 |
| Maintaining | 26-10-25 22:13:34 | 26-10-25 22:13:46 | 00:00:12 | 20 |
| Increasing | 26-10-25 22:13:46 | 26-10-25 22:13:49 | 00:00:03 | 20 &rarr; 160 |
| Maintaining | 26-10-25 22:13:49 | 26-10-25 22:14:01 | 00:00:12 | 160 |
| Decreasing | 26-10-25 22:14:01 | 26-10-25 22:14:04 | 00:00:03 | 20 &larr; 160 |
| Maintaining | 26-10-25 22:14:04 | 26-10-25 22:14:17 | 00:00:13 | 20 |
| Increasing | 26-10-25 22:14:17 | 26-10-25 22:14:22 | 00:00:05 | 20 &rarr; 43 |
| Decreasing | 26-10-25 22:14:22 | 26-10-25 22:14:27 | 00:00:05 | 20 &larr; 43 |
| Maintaining | 26-10-25 22:14:27 | 26-10-25 22:14:47 | 00:00:20 | 20 |
| Canceling | 26-10-25 22:14:47 | 26-10-25 22:14:48 | 00:00:01 | 0 &larr; 20 |

The run starts with the default users, one per CPU core (18 here). At 5 users a second, the users 80 that follows increase_rate 5 had reached 43 users when users 20 arrived about 5 seconds later, so Goose turned back from 43.

WebSocket Controller

The WebSocket Controller is off unless Goose is started with --websocket, or a load test sets GooseDefault::WebSocket. It takes no authentication and accepts a connection from any web page, so a page open in any browser that can reach its port can start, stop and reconfigure the load test.

When on, it listens on 127.0.0.1:5117 by default, so only this machine can reach it. To reach it from another machine, either forward the port over ssh to the default bind (ssh -L 5117:127.0.0.1:5117 loadtest, then connect to ws://127.0.0.1:5117), or start Goose with an explicit host such as --websocket-host 0.0.0.0, which prints a warning at startup that the Controller has no authentication.

The host and port that the WebSocket Controller listens on can be configured at start time with --websocket-host and --websocket-port, and their defaults changed with GooseDefault::WebSocketHost and GooseDefault::WebSocketPort. --no-websocket and GooseDefault::NoWebSocket, which turned it off in Goose 0.18, are still accepted and only log a warning; either one with --websocket or GooseDefault::WebSocket is refused.

Details

The WebSocket Controller supports the same commands listed in the telnet controller. Requests and Responses are in JSON format.

Requests must be made in the following format:

{"request":String}

For example, a client should send the following JSON to request the current load test metrics:

{"request":"metrics"}

A request may be at most 131,072 bytes (128 KiB), the longest single command line argument Linux takes with 4 KiB pages. A longer message is answered with a close frame, code 1009 (message too big), with the reason message too big, at most 131072 bytes, and the connection ends. A test plan sent to a Controller is at most 4096 bytes and 100 steps, as from the dashboard; see the bounds every front end shares.

Responses will always be in the following format:

{"response":String,"success":Boolean}

For example, from a load test built with the pdf-reports feature, so its configuration includes pdf_scale and pdf_timeout, which a build without that feature leaves out:

% websocat ws://127.0.0.1:5117
foo
{"response":"invalid json, see Goose book https://book.goose.rs/controller/websocket.html","success":false}
{"request":"foo"}
{"response":"unrecognized command, see Goose book https://book.goose.rs/controller/websocket.html","success":false}
{"request":"config"}
{"response":"{\"host\":null,\"users\":10,\"increase_rate\":null,\"increase_time\":null,\"run_time\":null,\"decrease_rate\":null,\"decrease_time\":null,\"iterations\":null,\"test_plan\":null,\"scenarios\":null,\"throttle_requests\":null,\"version\":false,\"list\":false,\"scenarios_list\":false,\"verbose\":0,\"quiet\":0,\"goose_log\":null,\"log_level\":0,\"running_metrics\":null,\"no_print_metrics\":false,\"no_error_summary\":false,\"report_file\":[],\"output_dir\":null,\"compress\":null,\"notes\":null,\"report_graph_data\":false,\"render_report\":null,\"baseline_file\":null,\"no_granular_report\":false,\"pdf_print_html\":null,\"pdf_scale\":0.8,\"pdf_timeout\":60,\"no_reset_metrics\":false,\"no_metrics\":false,\"no_transaction_metrics\":false,\"no_scenario_metrics\":false,\"no_status_codes\":false,\"co_mitigation\":\"Disabled\",\"threshold\":[],\"no_threshold_abort\":false,\"threshold_abort_delay\":null,\"request_log\":null,\"request_format\":\"Json\",\"request_body\":false,\"transaction_log\":null,\"transaction_format\":\"Json\",\"scenario_log\":null,\"scenario_format\":\"Json\",\"error_log\":null,\"error_format\":\"Json\",\"debug_log\":null,\"debug_format\":\"Json\",\"no_debug_body\":false,\"dashboard\":false,\"dashboard_host\":null,\"dashboard_port\":null,\"dashboard_auth_token\":null,\"dashboard_control\":false,\"dashboard_no_auth\":false,\"dashboard_max_clients\":null,\"no_percentiles_over_time\":false,\"no_autostart\":true,\"no_telnet\":false,\"telnet_host\":\"127.0.0.1\",\"telnet_port\":5116,\"websocket\":true,\"no_websocket\":false,\"websocket_host\":\"127.0.0.1\",\"websocket_port\":5117,\"timeout\":null,\"no_gzip\":false,\"sticky_follow\":false,\"accept_invalid_certs\":false}","success":true}
{"request":"stop"}
{"response":"load test not running, failed to stop","success":false}
{"request":"shutdown"}
{"response":"load test shut down","success":true}

This session is Goose started with --websocket --no-autostart -u 10. config sends the configuration as JSON, with an option that is not set shown as null, as the telnet Controller’s config-json does. A dashboard auth token, when one is set, is shown as "(set)", never the token itself. metrics sends the metrics as the telnet Controller’s metrics-json does, with each request’s response times in microsecond buckets, times_us; see Controller Commands.

After exit (or quit or q) the Controller answers goodbye!, and after shutdown it answers load test shut down; either way it then sends a close frame, code 1000, and ends the connection. A request that gets no answer from Goose within 15 seconds is answered {"response":"control timed out, it may still apply: check before retrying","success":false}.

Gaggle: Distributed Load Test

NOTE: Goose 0.19 cannot run a Gaggle. Gaggle support was removed in Goose 0.17.0 (see PR 529) and will be restored in Goose 0.20, before the 1.0 release. The gaggle feature, the --manager and --worker flags and the other options in this section do not exist in 0.19. This section describes how Gaggles worked in Goose 0.16.4, the last release with them; use that release if you need a distributed load test today.

Goose 0.16 also supported distributed load testing. A Gaggle is one Goose process running in Manager mode, and 1 or more Goose processes running in Worker mode. The Manager coordinates starting and stopping the Workers, and collects aggregated metrics. Gaggle support is a cargo feature that must be enabled at compile-time. To launch a Gaggle, you must copy your load test application to all servers from which you wish to generate load.

It is strongly recommended that the same load test application be copied to all servers involved in a Gaggle. By default, Goose will verify that the load test is identical by comparing a hash of all load test rules. Telling it to skip this check can cause the load test to panic (for example, if a Worker defines a different number of transactions or scenarios than the Manager).

Load Testing At Scale

Experimenting with running Goose load tests from AWS, Goose used all available system resources, so it was generally limited only by network speed. A smaller server instance was able to simulate 2,000 users generating over 6,500 requests per second and sustaining 2.5 Gbps. As more uplink speed was added, Goose was able to scale linearly: by distributing the test across two servers with faster uplinks, it simulated 12,000 active users generating over 41,000 requests per second and saturating 16 Gbps.

Generating this much traffic in and of itself is not fundamentally difficult, but with Goose each request is fully analyzed and validated. It not only confirms the response code for each response the server returns, but also inspects the returned HTML to confirm it contains all expected elements. Links to static elements such as images and CSS are extracted from each response and also loaded, with each simulated user behaving similar to how a real user would. Goose excels at providing consistent and repeatable load testing.

For full details and graphs, refer to the blog A Goose In The Clouds: Load Testing At Scale.

Gaggle Manager

NOTE: Goose 0.19 cannot run a Gaggle. Gaggle support was removed in Goose 0.17.0 and will be restored in Goose 0.20, before the 1.0 release. This page describes Goose 0.16.4, the last release with it; see the Gaggle overview.

To launch a Gaggle, you first must start a Goose application in Manager mode. All configuration happens in the Manager. To start, add the --manager flag and --expect-workers option, the latter necessary to tell the Manager process how many Worker processes it will be coordinating.

Example

Configure a Goose Manager to listen on all interfaces on the default port (0.0.0.0:5115), waiting for 2 Goose Worker processes.

cargo run --features gaggle --example simple -- --manager --expect-workers 2 --host http://local.dev/

Gaggle Worker

NOTE: Goose 0.19 cannot run a Gaggle. Gaggle support was removed in Goose 0.17.0 and will be restored in Goose 0.20, before the 1.0 release. This page describes Goose 0.16.4, the last release with it; see the Gaggle overview.

At this time, a Goose process can be either a Manager or a Worker, not both. Therefore, it usually makes sense to launch your first Worker on the same server that the Manager is running on. If not otherwise configured, a Goose Worker will try to connect to the Manager on the localhost.

Examples

Starting a Worker that connects to a Manager running on the same server:

cargo run --features gaggle --example simple -- --worker -v

In our earlier example, we expected 2 Workers. The second Goose process should be started on a different server. This will require telling it the host where the Goose Manager process is running. For example:

cargo run --example simple -- --worker --manager-host 192.168.1.55 -v

Once all expected Workers are running, the distributed load test will automatically start. We set the -v flag so Goose provides verbose output indicating what is happening. In our example, the load test will run until it is canceled. You can cancel the Manager or either of the Worker processes, and the test will stop on all servers.

Run-time Flags

NOTE: Goose 0.19 cannot run a Gaggle. Gaggle support was removed in Goose 0.17.0 and will be restored in Goose 0.20, before the 1.0 release. This page describes Goose 0.16.4, the last release with it; see the Gaggle overview.

  • --manager: starts a Goose process in Manager mode. There currently can only be one Manager per Gaggle.
  • --worker: starts a Goose process in Worker mode. How many Workers are in a given Gaggle is defined by the --expect-workers option, documented below.
  • --no-hash-check: tells Goose to ignore if the load test application doesn’t match between Worker(s) and the Manager. This is not recommended, and can cause the application to panic.

The --no-metrics, --no-reset-metrics, --no-status-codes, and --no-hash-check flags must be set on the Manager. Workers inherit these flags from the Manager

Run-time Options

  • --manager-bind-host <manager-bind-host>: configures the host that the Manager listens on. By default Goose will listen on all interfaces, or 0.0.0.0.
  • --manager-bind-port <manager-bind-port>: configures the port that the Manager listens on. By default Goose will listen on port 5115.
  • --manager-host <manager-host>: configures the host that the Worker will talk to the Manager on. By default, a Goose Worker will connect to the localhost, or 127.0.0.1. In a distributed load test, this must be set to the IP of the Goose Manager.
  • --manager-port <manager-port>: configures the port that a Worker will talk to the Manager on. By default, a Goose Worker will connect to port 5115.

The --users, --increase-time, --increase-rate, --host, and --run-time options must be set on the Manager. Workers inherit these options from the Manager.

The --throttle-requests option must be configured on each Worker, and can be set to a different value on each Worker if desired.

Gaggle Technical Details

NOTE: Goose 0.19 cannot run a Gaggle. Gaggle support was removed in Goose 0.17.0 and will be restored in Goose 0.20, before the 1.0 release. This page describes Goose 0.16.4, the last release with it; see the Gaggle overview.

Goose uses nng to send network messages between the Manager and all Workers. Serde and Serde CBOR are used to serialize messages into Concise Binary Object Representation.

Workers initiate all network connections, and push metrics to the Manager process.

Compile-time Feature

Gaggle support is a compile-time Cargo feature that must be enabled. Goose uses the nng library to manage network connections, and compiling nng requires that cmake be available.

The gaggle feature can be enabled from the command line by adding --features gaggle to your cargo command.

With Goose 0.16, a load test application could default to compiling in the Gaggle feature in the dependencies section of its Cargo.toml. This only works with Goose 0.16.4 or earlier: Goose 0.18 accepts the feature and ignores it, and Goose 0.17 and 0.19 have no gaggle feature, so Cargo refuses to build a dependency line that asks for it. For example, pinned to 0.16.4:

[dependencies]
goose = { version = "=0.16.4", features = ["gaggle"] }

What is Coordinated Omission?

Coordinated Omission is a measurement problem that accidentally hides how many people are actually affected by server slowdowns during load testing.

The Race Timer Problem

Imagine you’re timing runners in a race:

  • You’re supposed to time a runner every 10 seconds
  • But sometimes a runner gets stuck in mud for 30 seconds
  • Your timer waits for that stuck runner before timing the next one
  • So instead of timing 6 runners per minute, you only time 2

Result: Your data says “average time: 15 seconds” when reality is most runners take 10 seconds, but some get stuck for 30+ seconds!

The issue: You’re only measuring the runners who actually finish, missing all the runners who would have started during the delay.

How This Affects Load Testing

The Problem: Missing the Full Impact

Timeline of Goose User Thread:
Time 0s:  Send request ✅ (1 second response)
Time 1s:  Send request ✅ (1 second response)  
Time 2s:  Send request ✅ (1 second response)
Time 3s:  Send request... ⏳ (server freezes!)
Time 33s: Finally get response ❌ (30 seconds late!)
Time 34s: Send request ✅ (1 second response)

What traditional load testing records:

  • 4 requests total
  • Average response time: 8.25 seconds
  • “Looks like mostly good performance”

What really happened: During that 30-second freeze, 29 more requests should have been made but couldn’t because the thread was stuck waiting. So instead of 4 requests, there should have been 33 requests affected by the server problem.

Why This Matters

When your server freezes for 30 seconds, EVERY user trying to access it during those 30 seconds is affected. Traditional load testing makes it look like only 1 user had problems, when really 30+ users experienced the issue.

This leads to dangerously optimistic reports:

  • ❌ “99% of requests were fast” (hiding the freeze)
  • ✅ “Server had a 30-second outage affecting 87% of traffic” (reality)

How Goose Fixes This

Coordinated omission mitigation is off by default; --co-mitigation average, minimum or maximum turns it on. See Mitigation Strategies.

1. Detects Missing Requests

Goose tracks how long each user takes to run through all of its transactions. When a run takes more than twice the user’s usual cadence, as one held up by a 30 second freeze does, Goose recognizes: “During that run, this user should have made more requests but couldn’t.”

2. Synthetic Request Injection

Goose adds “synthetic requests” to represent the requests that would have been made if the server hadn’t frozen, giving you a complete picture of impact.

3. Clear Reporting

Goose shows the measured and the adjusted response times side by side, and sums up the events. Here is a real run like the freeze above: one user of a load test whose only transaction requests /, against a local server that answers in 1 second, with --co-mitigation minimum so the cadence is the user’s fastest run. 3.5 seconds into the run the server froze for 30 seconds:

cargo run --release -- --host http://127.0.0.1:8080/ -u1 -r1 -t36s --co-mitigation minimum --no-telnet

 === COORDINATED OMISSION METRICS ===
 Duration: 37 seconds
 Total CO Events: 1
 Events per minute: 1.62

 Request Breakdown:
   Actual requests: 8
   Synthetic requests: 28 (77.8%)

 Severity Distribution:
   Critical: 1

The user measured 8 requests, one of them held for 30 seconds, and Goose added 28 synthetic requests for those it could not send during the freeze, so the GET / row counts 36 requests. This tells you: “77.8% of your expected traffic was affected by server problems!”

Visual Comparison

Before (Traditional Load Testing):

Timeline: |--1s--|--1s--|--------30s--------|--1s--|
Requests:    ✅     ✅          ❌           ✅
Result: "4 requests, looks mostly fine" ❌ MISLEADING

After (Goose with CO Mitigation):

Timeline: |--1s--|--1s--|--------30s--------|--1s--|  
Real:        ✅     ✅          ❌           ✅
Synthetic:              ❌❌❌❌❌...❌❌❌    (about one for each second of the freeze)
Result: "30-second freeze affected most of the traffic" ✅ ACCURATE

The Key Insight

A server problem affects every request that should have been sent while it lasted, not only the one that was slow. With mitigation enabled, Goose counts those requests too.

Mitigation Strategies

Overview

Goose records the time of every request it makes. That alone does not prevent coordinated omission: each GooseUser waits for a response before it sends its next request, so while the server stalls, users send fewer requests and the stall is under counted. Coordinated omission mitigation, which is off by default, backfills the requests each user would have sent at its usual cadence.

What Goose Records

Complete Timing Capture

  1. Recording Every Request: Goose records the response time of every request it makes, however long it takes, up to the request timeout
  2. No Sampling: every response time is counted; none is sampled away
  3. One Request at a Time per User: a GooseUser waits for each response before it goes on, so a slow response delays that user’s next requests; this is the source of coordinated omission that mitigation corrects for
#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
// Example: Goose times this request however long it takes, up to --timeout.
async fn user_function(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    // Even if this takes 30 seconds, Goose records the 30 seconds,
    // and this user sends nothing else while it waits.
    let _goose = user.get("/slow-endpoint").await?;
    Ok(())
}
}

Accurate Percentile Calculation

Goose keeps response times in its own buckets:

  • Microsecond precision: times are taken from a Duration and kept to the microsecond
  • Bounded error: each bucket is never more than 1% off any time it holds, so percentiles are never more than 1% off
  • Exact extremes: the fastest and slowest times are kept exactly

Configuration Options

Request Timeout Settings

A request that times out fails, and its response time is about the timeout, so a timeout shorter than the server’s slowest responses cuts them off. Set one long enough to capture the slow responses you want measured:

# A 60 second request timeout (the default is 60)
cargo run --release -- --timeout 60

# For extremely slow endpoints, increase further
cargo run --release -- --timeout 300

Coordinated Omission Mitigation Mode

Mitigation is disabled by default. Enable it by naming the cadence to backfill at:

# Backfill at each user's average cadence
cargo run --release -- --co-mitigation average

--co-mitigation takes average, maximum, minimum or disabled, ignoring case. In code, set it with GooseDefault::CoordinatedOmissionMitigation and a GooseCoordinatedOmissionMitigation value. Any value but disabled is refused with --no-metrics. Mitigation has no other options.

When enabled, this mode:

  • Tracks how long each user takes to run through all of its transactions
  • Backfills synthetic response times for the requests a stalled user did not send
  • Logs an INFO message for each request that took longer than its user’s usual cadence

Understanding Your Results

With mitigation enabled and once it has backfilled a request, Goose shows two sets of metrics:

  • Raw Metrics: the response times Goose measured
  • Adjusted Metrics: the measured times together with the synthetic ones

A large gap between these metrics means a stall held users back during your test. The Metrics page shows where each appears.

Choosing Your Mitigation Strategy

Goose offers four values for the --co-mitigation flag:

ModeUse CaseBehavior
disabled (default)Custom analysis, external CO handlingNo adjustment, raw data only
averageGeneral performance testingExpected cadence is the user’s average time to run through its transactions
minimumMost sensitiveExpected cadence is the user’s fastest time to run through its transactions
maximumLeast sensitiveExpected cadence is the user’s slowest time to run through its transactions

When to Use Each Mode

Use minimum when:

  • Testing services with strict timing requirements
  • You want any slowdown against the fastest run through the transactions backfilled
  • Testing in controlled environments with little variance

Use average when:

  • Simulating realistic user behavior
  • Testing public-facing websites
  • You want balanced synthetic data generation
  • General performance regression testing

Use maximum when:

  • Response times vary widely and you only want the largest stalls backfilled

Use disabled when:

  • Implementing custom CO mitigation
  • Performing specialized statistical analysis
  • You need only actual measurements
  • Comparing with other tools’ raw output

Best Practices

1. Use Realistic User Counts

Avoid overwhelming your system with too few users:

# Better: More users with think time
cargo run --release -- --users 1000 --increase-rate 10

# Worse: Few users hammering the system
cargo run --release -- --users 10 --increase-rate 10

2. Monitor Response Time Distributions

Read the full distribution as well as the averages. The percentile tables of the console, HTML and Markdown reports show where the long tail starts: a median that looks good can sit beside a 99th percentile many times higher.

3. Set Appropriate Timeouts

Balance between capturing slow responses and test duration. --timeout sets the timeout for every request; to give one request its own, set it on the request builder:

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
use goose::prelude::*;
use std::time::Duration;

async fn slow_endpoint(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    // Give this one request 30 seconds, whatever --timeout says.
    let request_builder = user
        .get_request_builder(&GooseMethod::Get, "/endpoint")?
        .timeout(Duration::from_secs(30));
    // The path names the request in the metrics, as the RequestBuilder
    // already has its URL.
    let goose_request = GooseRequest::builder()
        .path("/endpoint")
        .set_request_builder(request_builder)
        .build();
    let _goose = user.request(goose_request).await?;

    Ok(())
}
}

4. Use Test Plans for Controlled Load

Test plans help maintain consistent request rates:

# Gradual ramp up prevents overwhelming the system: 100 users over 30 seconds,
# hold for 30 seconds, 200 users over 90 seconds, hold for 150 seconds, then
# stop every user over a minute.
cargo run --release -- --test-plan "100,30s;100,30s;200,1m30s;200,2m30s;0,1m"

How It Works

Each time a GooseUser starts another run through all the Transactions of its Scenario, Goose measures how long the previous run took, leaving out the time the user slept between transactions because of set_wait_time. It keeps each user’s fastest, slowest and average run. From the user’s fourth run on, the expected cadence is the average, slowest or fastest run, as --co-mitigation says; the average includes the run just measured. When the previous run took more than twice the expected cadence, Goose treats it as a stall.

Every request the user then makes during the run that follows backfills the stall. For each one, Goose records synthetic response times: the length of the stalled run, minus the cadence, minus the request’s own response time, then that value minus the cadence again, and so on for as long as the value is greater than the request’s own response time. These are the response_times the requests the user would have sent during the stall would have seen. These synthetic times estimate the effect of the slowdown.

Independently, from a user’s fourth run on, Goose logs an INFO message for each request whose response time is longer than the user’s expected cadence, its user_cadence. Such a request will likely trigger mitigation. INFO messages are shown on the console by default (-q hides them), and are written to the Goose log when it is configured with --goose-log and the log level is raised with -g.

Verification Techniques

1. Compare with Expected Throughput

Calculate theoretical vs actual request rates:

expected_rps = users * (1000 / average_ms_per_request_including_think_time)
actual_rps   = total_requests / test_duration_seconds

co_factor    = expected_rps / actual_rps   # well above 1 suggests users were held back

2. Analyze Response Time Variance

Look for these warning signs in the measured metrics:

  • A 99th percentile more than ten times the median
  • A maximum response time orders of magnitude higher than the median
  • A large gap between the measured and adjusted tables when mitigation is enabled

Examples

The following run is contrived. The drupal_memcache example ran with five users for 20 seconds against a local test server that answers each request in 5 to 25 ms, and that held every request for 3 seconds, 11 seconds into the run:

cargo run --release --example drupal_memcache -- --host http://127.0.0.1:8080/ \
    -u5 -r5 -t20s --co-mitigation average --no-telnet \
    --request-log requests.json --request-format json \
    --report-file report.html --report-file report.json --report-file report.md

Requests that took longer than their user’s cadence were logged:

19:44:45 [INFO] 11.004s into goose attack: "GET http://127.0.0.1:8080/misc/drupal.js" [200] took abnormally long (3026 ms), transaction name: "(Anon) front page"
19:44:45 [INFO] 10.993s into goose attack: "GET http://127.0.0.1:8080/themes/bartik/logo.png" [200] took abnormally long (3036 ms), transaction name: "(Anon) front page"
19:44:45 [INFO] 10.984s into goose attack: "GET http://127.0.0.1:8080/misc/drupal.js" [200] took abnormally long (3046 ms), transaction name: "(Auth) front page"
19:44:45 [INFO] 11.005s into goose attack: "GET http://127.0.0.1:8080/misc/drupal.js" [200] took abnormally long (3024 ms), transaction name: "(Anon) front page"
19:44:45 [INFO] 11.004s into goose attack: "GET http://127.0.0.1:8080/" [200] took abnormally long (3026 ms), transaction name: "(Anon) front page"

The time before each message is when the request started, in seconds since the load test started, and the number in parentheses is its response time. With --request-log enabled, you can find each of these requests in the request log by its elapsed, the same start time in milliseconds, and its response_time. The first three, which started 11,004, 10,993 and 10,984 ms in and took 3,026, 3,036 and 3,046 ms:

{"elapsed":11004,"scenario_index":0,"scenario_name":"anonbrowsinguser","transaction_index":0,"transaction_name":{"TransactionOnly":"(Anon) front page"},"raw":{"method":"Get","url":"http://127.0.0.1:8080/misc/drupal.js","headers":[],"body":"","custom_method":""},"name":"static asset","final_url":"http://127.0.0.1:8080/misc/drupal.js","redirected":false,"response_time":3026,"status_code":200,"success":true,"update":false,"user":1,"error":"","coordinated_omission_elapsed":0,"user_cadence":930,"response_time_fraction_us":360}
{"elapsed":10993,"scenario_index":0,"scenario_name":"anonbrowsinguser","transaction_index":0,"transaction_name":{"TransactionOnly":"(Anon) front page"},"raw":{"method":"Get","url":"http://127.0.0.1:8080/themes/bartik/logo.png","headers":[],"body":"","custom_method":""},"name":"static asset","final_url":"http://127.0.0.1:8080/themes/bartik/logo.png","redirected":false,"response_time":3036,"status_code":200,"success":true,"update":false,"user":0,"error":"","coordinated_omission_elapsed":0,"user_cadence":896,"response_time_fraction_us":748}
{"elapsed":10984,"scenario_index":1,"scenario_name":"authbrowsinguser","transaction_index":1,"transaction_name":{"TransactionOnly":"(Auth) front page"},"raw":{"method":"Get","url":"http://127.0.0.1:8080/misc/drupal.js","headers":[],"body":"","custom_method":""},"name":"static asset","final_url":"http://127.0.0.1:8080/misc/drupal.js","redirected":false,"response_time":3046,"status_code":200,"success":true,"update":false,"user":3,"error":"","coordinated_omission_elapsed":0,"user_cadence":1046,"response_time_fraction_us":50}

All five user threads were held up by the same stall, each loading the front page or one of its static assets: users 0, 1, 2 and 4 the anonymous front page, and user 3 the authenticated one. The front page is the transaction each user runs most often, with a weight of 15 against the node page’s 10 and the user page’s 3, and each front page load also requests its static assets, so most requests caught by a stall belong to it. The user_cadence of each line is that user’s expected cadence: user 1 took on average 930 ms to run through its transactions, user 0 896 ms and user 3 1,046 ms, so a 3 second stall is more than twice any of them.

The request log also holds the requests that carry a backfill. During the run after a stall, each request a user makes appears twice: once as measured, with a coordinated_omission_elapsed of 0, and once more with coordinated_omission_elapsed set to how long the stalled run took, in milliseconds. Here is the line with the backfill for the first request of user 1’s run after the stall, a front page request that took 12 ms, after a run that took 3,946 ms against a cadence of 1,182 ms, the average now that it includes the stalled run:

{"elapsed":14191,"scenario_index":0,"scenario_name":"anonbrowsinguser","transaction_index":0,"transaction_name":{"TransactionOnly":"(Anon) front page"},"raw":{"method":"Get","url":"http://127.0.0.1:8080/","headers":[],"body":"","custom_method":""},"name":"/","final_url":"http://127.0.0.1:8080/","redirected":false,"response_time":12,"status_code":200,"success":true,"update":false,"user":1,"error":"","coordinated_omission_elapsed":3946,"user_cadence":1182,"response_time_fraction_us":534}

From this line Goose recorded three synthetic times for GET /: 2,752 ms (3,946 less 1,182 less 12), then 1,570 and 388 ms, each 1,182 less than the one before, stopping before the next would be 12 or less. Each synthetic time also counts as a request in # reqs; the line carrying the backfill does not, as the same request is counted once as measured. The synthetic times themselves are not written to the request log; normal requests have a coordinated_omission_elapsed of 0.

Advanced Techniques

Custom Metrics Collection

Log slow responses for your own analysis:

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
use goose::prelude::*;

async fn monitored_request(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let goose = user.get("/endpoint").await?;

    // Write slow responses to the debug log, if --debug-log is set.
    if goose.request.response_time > 100 {
        user.log_debug(
            &format!("slow response: {} ms", goose.request.response_time),
            Some(&goose.request),
            None,
            None,
        )?;
    }

    Ok(())
}
}

Real-time Monitoring

Use Goose’s controllers or the live dashboard to watch a test while it runs. The Telnet controller is on by default, the WebSocket controller with --websocket, and their metrics command returns the metrics so far:

# Turn the WebSocket controller on, and keep it on this machine
cargo run --release -- --websocket --websocket-host 127.0.0.1 --websocket-port 5117

# Monitor for:
# - Sudden drops in request rate
# - Spikes in response times

Statistical Analysis Note

While Goose provides comprehensive data for analysis, determining statistical significance of performance changes requires additional tools and expertise. Goose produces the raw data you need, but interpretation remains your responsibility.

For detailed analysis, consider:

  • Kolmogorov-Smirnov or Anderson-Darling tests for distribution comparison
  • Note that CO-adjusted data is derived from raw data (not statistically independent)
  • Export data via --request-log for external analysis

Summary

Goose accounts for coordinated omission with:

  1. Every request timed: Goose times every request it makes
  2. Percentiles within 1%: no bucket is more than 1% off the times it holds
  3. Configuration: a request timeout and three mitigation modes
  4. Live metrics: the Controllers and the dashboard show the metrics while the test runs

Enable mitigation where stalls are likely, so the results show how the system behaves under load.

Metrics

With coordinated omission mitigation on, Goose keeps two sets of response times for each request name. The measured times, which other pages call raw, are the ones Goose timed. The adjusted times are the measured times together with the synthetic times that mitigation adds for the requests that a delayed user did not make. Goose also counts CO events. This page shows where each view reports them; Was my run affected? says how to read them for your own run.

The run on this page

Every image and excerpt on this page comes from runs against a small local server that answers each request after a random delay of about 5 to 15 ms, and stalls every 1,000th request, counted across all connections, for one second. The GraphQL example ran 10 users for two minutes against it, with no wait between requests:

cargo run --release --features dashboard --example graphql_loadtest -- \
  -H http://127.0.0.1:18900 --dashboard -u 10 -r 10 -t 2m --co-mitigation average \
  --report-file report.html --report-file report.md --report-file report.json

Each user runs the example’s four requests in a loop, and one time through the loop is an iteration. The time a user’s iteration is expected to take is its cadence: with --co-mitigation average, the average time of the user’s iterations. A stalled request delays the rest of its user’s iteration. Of about 111,000 requests in two minutes, the server stalled 111 for a second each, and the console logged each of them as taking abnormally long.

The live dashboard

Once mitigation records its first event, the dashboard shows adjusted times where it shows response times:

  • the p95 and average KPI cards say (whole run, adjusted);
  • the Whole run card shows adjusted and measured times side by side and, once there is an event, lists Coordinated omission as active;
  • the requests table shows adjusted times, with the measured time on hover;
  • the response time heatmap opens on Adjusted, with a Measured button beside it.

The RPS card, and the Requests and RPS figures on the Whole run card, then count the synthetic requests too, so the RPS figures read higher than the requests per second chart: 1,001 against 922.70 in the image below. Before the first event, and with mitigation off, every time on the dashboard is measured. The dashboard shows no count of CO events; the reports and the console do.

The adjusted p95 on the KPI card is 391 ms, while the heatmap’s densest band sits near 10 ms: almost everything above that band is synthetic, spread from about 20 ms up to the one second stall, for requests that the delayed users did not make.

Two seconds later, the card shows that stalling one request in 1,000 for a second lifts the adjusted p99 to 889 ms, while the measured p99 stays at 15 ms.

PDF and HTML reports

The PDF and HTML reports add two adjusted tables once mitigation has added synthetic times, and an events section whenever mitigation is on. Request Metrics With Coordinated Omission Mitigation follows Request Metrics: the adjusted average, a standard deviation and the maximum for each request name that has synthetic times, then the aggregate. Mitigation never changes the minimum, so this table shows in its place the standard deviation of two numbers, the measured and the adjusted average, which is their difference divided by the square root of 2.

The adjusted averages are 50 to 51 ms; a standard deviation of 28 puts the measured average about 40 ms lower, at the 11 ms the Whole run card shows as Avg ms (measured).

Response Time Metrics With Coordinated Omission Mitigation follows Response Time Metrics, with the same percentile columns.

The measured table, untitled here, on top and the adjusted one below stay within a millisecond of each other up to the 90th percentile; above it the adjusted times climb toward the one second stall.

Coordinated Omission Event Metrics ends the report whenever mitigation is on, even with no events: the events, the events per minute, the actual and synthetic requests, and a count of each severity that had an event.

The 440 events are four for each stalled request that had an iteration after it, as Reading the event summary explains.

Markdown report

The Markdown report has no adjusted tables. It has the event summary, with all four severities, and, when there are events, tables of events by user and by scenario. From this run, without the per user and per scenario tables:

## Coordinated Omission Metrics

**Overall Metrics:**
- Total CO Events: 440
- Synthetic Requests Generated: 9,770
- Actual Requests: 111,106
- Synthetic Percentage: 8.1%

**Severity Breakdown:**
- Minor (2-5x): 0
- Moderate (5-10x): 0
- Severe (10-20x): 24
- Critical (>20x): 416

JSON report

co_request_metrics and co_response_metrics hold the two adjusted tables, beside the measured raw_request_metrics and raw_response_metrics. Each request name in raw_metrics.requests holds its adjusted timing data in coordinated_omission_data, null when it has none. coordinated_omission_metrics holds the event summary, shown here without its per_user_events and per_scenario_events:

{
  "total_co_events": 440,
  "actual_requests": 111106,
  "synthetic_requests": 9770,
  "synthetic_percentage": 8.082664,
  "severity_breakdown": {
    "Critical": 416,
    "Severe": 24
  },
  "duration_secs": 121,
  "events_per_minute": 218.1818181818182,
  "minor_count": 0,
  "moderate_count": 0,
  "severe_count": 24,
  "critical_count": 416
}

With mitigation on, Goose logs each request that took longer than the user’s cadence, starting with the user’s fourth iteration; with mitigation off, it logs none:

16:24:37 [INFO] 1.447s into goose attack: "POST http://127.0.0.1:18900/graphql" [200] took abnormally long (1000 ms), transaction name: "Simple Query"
16:24:38 [INFO] 2.546s into goose attack: "POST http://127.0.0.1:18900/graphql" [200] took abnormally long (999 ms), transaction name: "Complex Query"
16:24:39 [INFO] 3.654s into goose attack: "POST http://127.0.0.1:18900/graphql" [200] took abnormally long (1001 ms), transaction name: "Mutation"
