The event summary comes just before === OVERVIEW === in the final metrics. A copy is also printed at the metrics reset, when every user has started; it covers only the ramp up, so read the final one:

 === COORDINATED OMISSION METRICS ===
 Duration: 121 seconds
 Total CO Events: 440
 Events per minute: 218.18

 Request Breakdown:
   Actual requests: 111106
   Synthetic requests: 9770 (8.1%)

 Severity Distribution:
   Critical: 416
   Severe: 24

Reading the event summary

  • Total CO Events counts requests, not stalled requests. When an iteration takes more than twice the user’s cadence (here, one with a stalled request), each request of the user’s next iteration that mitigation adds synthetic times for is one event. Here 110 of the 111 stalled requests led to four events each, one for each request of the user’s next iteration; the last came as the run ended, with no iteration after it.
  • Duration is the time in whole seconds since the counts started, and Events per minute is Total CO Events divided by that time in minutes: 440 in 121 seconds is 218.18.
  • Actual requests are the requests Goose timed; synthetic requests are the times mitigation added. Together they are the Aggregated # reqs of the request table in the reports and the console, 120,876 here. The percentage is the synthetic requests’ share of that total: 9,770 of 120,876 is 8.1%.
  • The counts start again at the metrics reset after the users start, as the other metrics do; in a run with no reset (--no-reset-metrics, --iterations or a test plan) they cover the whole run.
  • Severity is the delayed iteration’s length as a multiple of the user’s cadence: Minor below 5 times, Moderate from 5 to 10, Severe from 10 to 20, and Critical from 20 times. Mitigation starts only above 2 times. The PDF and HTML reports and the console list only the severities with events, in no fixed order.
  • In the Markdown report’s table of events by user, users are numbered from 0 and listed in no fixed order; the console’s [user 1]: launching user lines number them from 1.

Was my run affected?

If your run had mitigation off, as it is by default (--co-mitigation disabled), look at its measured times: a maximum or a 99.99th percentile many times the median is the sign that requests were stalled. The disabled run in the table below had a maximum of 1,002 ms against a median of 9.92 ms in its console. Mitigation adds nothing to the load Goose sends, so run again with --co-mitigation average to see what the measured times leave out.

With mitigation on, check for events first. On the live dashboard, Coordinated omission active on the Whole run card means at least one event. In the reports, and in the console’s final event summary (not the copy printed at the metrics reset), read Total CO Events. When it is 0, mitigation added no synthetic times and Goose shows no adjusted tables. With average or minimum, that means no iteration judged since the metrics reset ran far enough over twice its user’s cadence for mitigation to add a synthetic time. A user’s first two iterations are never judged, so a stall in them, as on a cold start, counts no events. A user’s last iteration is not judged either, so a stall as the run ends counts none. With maximum, which counts no events in Goose 0.19, a 0 says nothing; run it again with average.

When there are events, compare the measured and adjusted times from the 95th percentile up:

  • the dashboard’s Whole run card: p95 and p99, adjusted beside measured;
  • the PDF or HTML report: its two percentile tables;
  • the JSON report: raw_response_metrics against co_response_metrics;
  • the console: its two percentile tables, from the 98% column.

The Markdown report has no adjusted tables. If the adjusted times climb, as from 15 to 889 ms at the 99th percentile here, stalled requests kept users from sending requests that the measured times leave out. With only a few events, only the highest percentiles change, but the run was still affected; the synthetic percentage (8.1% here) says by how much. If your run used minimum, read its note below the table first.

The setting decides what counts. The average row is the run above; the others ran the same command against a second copy of the server on port 18901, with --no-telnet in place of --dashboard:

--co-mitigationCO eventsSynthetic requestsMeasured p99Adjusted p99
disabled (the default)not countednone15 msnone
average4409,770 (8.1%)15 ms889 ms
minimum18,25838,288 (25.7%)15 ms938 ms
maximum00 (0.0%)15 msnone
  • average compares each iteration with the user’s average iteration; here it counted events after every stalled request but the last.
  • minimum compares with the fastest iteration. Iterations of ordinary length led to events too. The fastest iteration in the run took 20 ms and the median 40 ms, so an iteration of ordinary length could run over twice the cadence of a user whose fastest iteration was near 20 ms; 17,818 of the 18,258 events were Minor and 440 Critical. With minimum, judge a run by its events above Minor (Moderate, Severe and Critical), or run it again with average.
  • maximum compares with the slowest iteration Goose has recorded. Goose records a slowest iteration only once an iteration is no faster than the fastest before it; until then the user’s cadence is 0, and Goose logs every request but adds no synthetic times. Once there is a slowest iteration, a longer iteration becomes the slowest before Goose judges it, so no iteration is ever more than twice the slowest, and in Goose 0.19 maximum counts no events. Once one of a user’s iterations included a stalled request, that user’s cadence was over a second, and Goose stopped logging the user’s stalled requests. It logged 10 of that run’s 111 stalled requests, all in the first 18 seconds. It also logged 12 ordinary requests in the run’s first 0.6 seconds, from users whose every iteration so far had been faster than the one before it.

Goose does not test whether a change in the distribution is statistically significant; see the statistical analysis note.

Practical Examples

Each example shows when to use a Coordinated Omission mitigation strategy. Each example includes the command to run against a load test of your own, an illustration of the summary Goose prints when mitigation is enabled, and interpretation guidance. The figures are illustrations, not results of real runs; the Metrics page explains each line.

Example 1: Microservice SLA Validation

Scenario: You’re testing a payment processing microservice that must respond within 100ms for 99% of requests.

Goal: Detect any stall, no matter how brief.

Strategy: Use minimum cadence to catch even momentary slowdowns.

# Test command
cargo run --release -- \
    --host https://payments.api.company.com \
    --users 20 \
    --run-time 5m \
    --co-mitigation minimum \
    --report-file payment_test.html

# Expected healthy output:
# === COORDINATED OMISSION METRICS ===
# Duration: 300 seconds
# Total CO Events: 2
# Events per minute: 0.40
#
# Request Breakdown:
#   Actual requests: 18450
#   Synthetic requests: 12 (0.1%)
#
# Severity Distribution:
#   Minor: 2

Interpretation:

  • ✅ Excellent: Only 2 minor CO events in 5 minutes
  • ✅ Minimal Impact: only 0.1% of the response times are synthetic
  • ✅ No Critical Issues: No severe or critical events

Red Flag Example:

# Problematic output:
# === COORDINATED OMISSION METRICS ===
# Duration: 300 seconds
# Total CO Events: 45
# Events per minute: 9.00
#
# Request Breakdown:
#   Actual requests: 18450
#   Synthetic requests: 892 (4.6%)
#
# Severity Distribution:
#   Minor: 38
#   Moderate: 6
#   Severe: 1

Action Required: 4.6% synthetic requests and frequent CO events indicate the service stalls often enough to check its response time percentiles against the SLA.

Example 2: E-commerce Website Load Testing

Scenario: Testing an e-commerce site during Black Friday preparation. Users can tolerate some variability, but you want to understand overall performance.

Goal: Simulate realistic user behavior while detecting significant performance issues.

Strategy: Use average cadence for balanced detection.

# Test command
cargo run --release -- \
    --host https://shop.company.com \
    --users 500 \
    --run-time 15m \
    --co-mitigation average \
    --report-file blackfriday_test.html

# Expected healthy output:
# === COORDINATED OMISSION METRICS ===
# Duration: 900 seconds
# Total CO Events: 12
# Events per minute: 0.80
#
# Request Breakdown:
#   Actual requests: 145230
#   Synthetic requests: 234 (0.2%)
#
# Severity Distribution:
#   Minor: 8
#   Moderate: 3
#   Severe: 1

Interpretation:

  • ✅ Good Performance: Low CO event rate (0.8/minute)
  • ✅ Minimal Impact: Only 0.2% synthetic requests
  • ⚠️ Monitor: One severe event warrants investigation

Concerning Example:

# Problematic output:
# === COORDINATED OMISSION METRICS ===
# Duration: 900 seconds
# Total CO Events: 156
# Events per minute: 10.40
#
# Request Breakdown:
#   Actual requests: 145230
#   Synthetic requests: 12450 (7.9%)
#
# Severity Distribution:
#   Minor: 89
#   Moderate: 45
#   Severe: 18
#   Critical: 4

Action Required: High CO event rate and 7.9% synthetic requests indicate the site will struggle under Black Friday load. Scale up resources or optimize performance.

Example 3: API Gateway Performance Testing

Scenario: Testing an API gateway that routes requests to multiple backend services. You want to understand how backend slowdowns affect the gateway.

Goal: Detect when backend issues cause gateway performance degradation.

Strategy: Use average cadence with longer test duration to capture intermittent issues.

# Test command
cargo run --release -- \
    --host https://gateway.api.company.com \
    --users 100 \
    --run-time 30m \
    --co-mitigation average \
    --report-file gateway_test.html

# Healthy distributed system output:
# === COORDINATED OMISSION METRICS ===
# Duration: 1800 seconds
# Total CO Events: 23
# Events per minute: 0.77
#
# Request Breakdown:
#   Actual requests: 324500
#   Synthetic requests: 445 (0.1%)
#
# Severity Distribution:
#   Minor: 18
#   Moderate: 4
#   Severe: 1

Interpretation:

  • ✅ Stable Gateway: Low synthetic percentage indicates good overall performance
  • ✅ Resilient: Minor events suggest the gateway handles backend hiccups well
  • ✅ Scalable: Consistent performance over 30 minutes

Example 4: Database Connection Pool Testing

Scenario: Testing an application’s database connection pool under load to ensure it doesn’t become a bottleneck.

Goal: Detect connection pool exhaustion or database slowdowns.

Strategy: Use minimum cadence to catch any database-related delays immediately.

# Test command
cargo run --release -- \
    --host https://app.company.com \
    --users 200 \
    --run-time 10m \
    --co-mitigation minimum \
    --report-file db_pool_test.html

# Healthy connection pool output:
# === COORDINATED OMISSION METRICS ===
# Duration: 600 seconds
# Total CO Events: 8
# Events per minute: 0.80
#
# Request Breakdown:
#   Actual requests: 89450
#   Synthetic requests: 67 (0.1%)
#
# Severity Distribution:
#   Minor: 6
#   Moderate: 2

Pool Exhaustion Example:

# Connection pool exhaustion:
# === COORDINATED OMISSION METRICS ===
# Duration: 600 seconds
# Total CO Events: 234
# Events per minute: 23.40
#
# Request Breakdown:
#   Actual requests: 89450
#   Synthetic requests: 8920 (9.1%)
#
# Severity Distribution:
#   Minor: 45
#   Moderate: 123
#   Severe: 56
#   Critical: 10

Action Required: High CO event rate and 9.1% synthetic requests are consistent with connection pool exhaustion. Increase pool size or optimize database queries.

Example 5: CDN Performance Validation

Scenario: Testing how your application performs when the CDN is slow or unavailable.

Goal: Understand the impact of CDN issues on user experience.

Strategy: Use average cadence to simulate realistic user tolerance.

# Test command with CDN issues simulated
cargo run --release -- \
    --host https://app.company.com \
    --users 150 \
    --run-time 10m \
    --co-mitigation average \
    --report-file cdn_impact_test.html

# CDN issues detected:
# === COORDINATED OMISSION METRICS ===
# Duration: 600 seconds
# Total CO Events: 89
# Events per minute: 8.90
#
# Request Breakdown:
#   Actual requests: 67230
#   Synthetic requests: 2340 (3.4%)
#
# Severity Distribution:
#   Minor: 34
#   Moderate: 38
#   Severe: 15
#   Critical: 2

Interpretation:

  • ⚠️ CDN Impact: 3.4% synthetic requests show CDN issues affect user experience
  • ⚠️ User Frustration: Moderate and severe events indicate noticeable delays
  • 📊 Business Impact: Use this data to justify CDN redundancy or optimization

Example 6: Baseline Testing (No CO Expected)

Scenario: Testing a well-optimized system under normal load to establish performance baselines.

Goal: Confirm the system performs consistently without CO events.

Strategy: Use disabled to get pure measurements, then compare with average mode.

# First, test with CO disabled for baseline
cargo run --release -- \
    --host https://optimized.company.com \
    --users 100 \
    --run-time 10m \
    --co-mitigation disabled \
    --report-file baseline_raw.html

# Then test with CO detection enabled
cargo run --release -- \
    --host https://optimized.company.com \
    --users 100 \
    --run-time 10m \
    --co-mitigation average \
    --report-file baseline_co.html

# Expected output (CO enabled):
# === COORDINATED OMISSION METRICS ===
# Duration: 600 seconds
# Total CO Events: 0
# Events per minute: 0.00
#
# Request Breakdown:
#   Actual requests: 45670
#   Synthetic requests: 0 (0.0%)

Perfect Baseline: Zero CO events and 0% synthetic requests indicate the system performs consistently under this load level.

Interpreting Results Across Examples

Green Flags (Healthy System)

  • CO events per minute < 2
  • Synthetic request percentage < 1%
  • Mostly Minor severity events
  • Consistent performance across test duration

Yellow Flags (Monitor Closely)

  • CO events per minute 2-10
  • Synthetic request percentage 1-5%
  • Some Moderate severity events
  • Occasional performance dips

Red Flags (Action Required)

  • CO events per minute > 10
  • Synthetic request percentage > 5%
  • Frequent Severe or any Critical events
  • Degrading performance over time

Using CO Metrics for Capacity Planning

  1. Find Breaking Point: Gradually increase load until CO events spike
  2. Set Alerts: Monitor CO metrics in production to detect issues early
  3. Compare Environments: Use CO metrics to validate staging vs production performance
  4. Track Trends: Monitor CO metrics over time to detect performance regression

Best Practices Summary

  1. Choose the Right Mode:

    • minimum for the most sensitive detection
    • average for realistic user simulation
    • disabled for baseline measurements

  2. Set Appropriate Test Duration:

    • Short tests (5-10 min) for quick validation
    • Long tests (30+ min) for stability assessment

  3. Monitor Key Metrics:

    • Events per minute rate
    • Synthetic request percentage
    • Severity distribution
    • Trends over time

  4. Take Action Based on Results:

    • < 1% synthetic: System healthy
    • 1-5% synthetic: Monitor and investigate

    • 5% synthetic: Performance issues need attention

These examples provide a foundation for understanding how CO metrics help identify and quantify performance issues in different scenarios. Use them as templates for your own testing strategies.

Configuration

Configuration of Goose load tests is done in Rust code within the load test plan. Complete documentation of all load test configuration can be found in the developer documentation.

Defaults

All run-time options can be configured with custom defaults. For example, you may want to default to the host name of your local development environment, only requiring that --host be set when running against a production environment. Assuming your local development environment is at “http://local.dev/” you can do this as follows:

    GooseAttack::initialize()?
        .register_scenario(scenario!("LoadtestTransactions")
            .register_transaction(transaction!(loadtest_index))
        )
        .set_default(GooseDefault::Host, "http://local.dev/")?
        .execute()
        .await?;

    Ok(())

The following defaults can be configured with a &str:

  • host: GooseDefault::Host
  • per-request timeout, in seconds: GooseDefault::Timeout
  • users to start per second: GooseDefault::IncreaseRate
  • users to stop per second: GooseDefault::DecreaseRate
  • test plan: GooseDefault::TestPlan
  • only run the named scenarios: GooseDefault::Scenarios
  • a pass/fail threshold rule, one rule for each call: GooseDefault::Threshold
  • report file name: GooseDefault::ReportFile
  • earlier report to compare against: GooseDefault::BaselineFile
  • the run’s folder: GooseDefault::OutputDir
  • notes stored with the run’s reports: GooseDefault::Notes
  • goose log file name: GooseDefault::GooseLog
  • request log file name: GooseDefault::RequestLog
  • transaction log file name: GooseDefault::TransactionLog
  • scenario log file name: GooseDefault::ScenarioLog
  • error log file name: GooseDefault::ErrorLog
  • debug log file name: GooseDefault::DebugLog
  • host to bind telnet Controller to, 127.0.0.1 unless set: GooseDefault::TelnetHost
  • host to bind WebSocket Controller to, 127.0.0.1 unless set: GooseDefault::WebSocketHost
  • host to bind the dashboard to: GooseDefault::DashboardHost
  • dashboard auth token: GooseDefault::DashboardAuthToken

The following defaults can be configured with a usize integer:

  • total users to start: GooseDefault::Users
  • seconds to start all users: GooseDefault::IncreaseTime
  • seconds to stop all users: GooseDefault::DecreaseTime
  • seconds for the test to run: GooseDefault::RunTime
  • times each user runs its scenario: GooseDefault::Iterations
  • how often to print running metrics, in seconds: GooseDefault::RunningMetrics
  • log level: GooseDefault::LogLevel
  • quiet: GooseDefault::Quiet
  • verbosity: GooseDefault::Verbose
  • maximum requests per second: GooseDefault::ThrottleRequests
  • seconds to wait before a threshold can stop the run: GooseDefault::ThresholdAbortDelay
  • port to bind telnet Controller to: GooseDefault::TelnetPort
  • port to bind WebSocket Controller to: GooseDefault::WebSocketPort
  • port to bind the dashboard to: GooseDefault::DashboardPort
  • maximum live dashboard clients: GooseDefault::DashboardMaxClients
  • gzip every file in the run’s folder, at a level from 1 to 9: GooseDefault::Compress
  • seconds to wait for a PDF report, with the pdf-reports feature: GooseDefault::PdfTimeout

The following default can be configured with an f64 float:

  • PDF report scale, with the pdf-reports feature: GooseDefault::PdfScale

The following defaults can be configured with a bool:

  • do not reset metrics after all users start: GooseDefault::NoResetMetrics
  • do not print metrics: GooseDefault::NoPrintMetrics
  • do not track metrics: GooseDefault::NoMetrics
  • do not track transaction metrics: GooseDefault::NoTransactionMetrics
  • do not track scenario metrics: GooseDefault::NoScenarioMetrics
  • do not track status codes: GooseDefault::NoStatusCodes
  • do not display the error summary: GooseDefault::NoErrorSummary
  • judge thresholds only at the end: GooseDefault::NoThresholdAbort
  • add request bodies to the request log: GooseDefault::RequestBody
  • do not log the response body in the debug log: GooseDefault::NoDebugBody
  • do not start telnet Controller thread: GooseDefault::NoTelnet
  • start the WebSocket Controller thread, which is off by default: GooseDefault::WebSocket
  • do not start WebSocket Controller thread, which only warns now that it is off by default, and is refused with GooseDefault::WebSocket: GooseDefault::NoWebSocket
  • do not autostart load test, wait instead for a Controller to start: GooseDefault::NoAutoStart
  • do not ask for gzip compressed responses: GooseDefault::NoGzip
  • follow redirect of base_url: GooseDefault::StickyFollow
  • accept invalid TLS certificates: GooseDefault::AcceptInvalidCerts
  • graph only totals in reports: GooseDefault::NoGranularData
  • add graph data to the JSON report: GooseDefault::ReportGraphData
  • serve the live web dashboard: GooseDefault::Dashboard
  • allow control from the dashboard: GooseDefault::DashboardControl
  • allow dashboard access and control without a token: GooseDefault::DashboardNoAuth
  • do not track per-second response times: GooseDefault::NoPercentilesOverTime

A default names a path, never the run’s folder. GooseDefault::OutputDir needs the dashboard, or an output flag given without a path on the command line. Without either, Goose refuses it with an error before the load test starts.

The following defaults can be configured with a GooseLogFormat:

  • request log file format: GooseDefault::RequestFormat
  • transaction log file format: GooseDefault::TransactionFormat
  • scenario log file format: GooseDefault::ScenarioFormat
  • error log file format: GooseDefault::ErrorFormat
  • debug log file format: GooseDefault::DebugFormat

The following defaults can be configured with a GooseCoordinatedOmissionMitigation:

  • default Coordinated Omission Mitigation strategy: GooseDefault::CoordinatedOmissionMitigation

Setting a default with a value of the wrong type returns an error that names the type the default expects.

An option given on the command line takes the place of the default. A value of 0 undoes a default too: --run-time 0, --increase-time 0, --decrease-time 0, --iterations 0 and --throttle-requests 0 undo GooseDefault::RunTime, IncreaseTime, DecreaseTime, Iterations and ThrottleRequests, so the load test can, for example, run --iterations where the default set a run time. An empty file name turns off a log a default set: --request-log "" turns off the request log GooseDefault::RequestLog names, and the same goes for --goose-log, --transaction-log, --scenario-log, --error-log and --debug-log.

For example, without any run-time options the following load test would automatically run against http://local.dev/, logging requests to goose-requests.log and debug to goose-debug.log. It will automatically launch 20 users at 4 users per second, so in 5 seconds, and run the load test for 15 minutes. Metrics will be displayed every minute during the test, and the status code table will be disabled. The order the defaults are set is not important.

    GooseAttack::initialize()?
        .register_scenario(scenario!("LoadtestTransactions")
            .register_transaction(transaction!(loadtest_index))
        )
        .set_default(GooseDefault::Host, "http://local.dev/")?
        .set_default(GooseDefault::RequestLog, "goose-requests.log")?
        .set_default(GooseDefault::DebugLog, "goose-debug.log")?
        .set_default(GooseDefault::Users, 20)?
        .set_default(GooseDefault::IncreaseRate, "4")?
        .set_default(GooseDefault::RunTime, 900)?
        .set_default(GooseDefault::RunningMetrics, 60)?
        .set_default(GooseDefault::NoStatusCodes, true)?
        .execute()
        .await?;

    Ok(())

Find a complete list of all configuration options that can be configured with custom defaults in the developer documentation, as well as complete details on how to configure defaults.

Scheduling Scenarios And Transactions

When starting a load test, Goose assigns one Scenario to each GooseUser thread. By default, it assigns Scenario (and then Transaction within the scenario) in a round robin order. As new GooseUser threads are launched, the first will be assigned the first defined Scenario, the next will be assigned the next defined Scenario, and so on, looping through all available Scenario. Weighting is respected during this process, so if one Scenario is weighted heavier than others, that Scenario will get assigned to GooseUser more at the end of the launching process.

The GooseScheduler can be configured to instead launch Scenario and Transaction in a Serial or a Random order. When configured to allocate in a Serial order, Scenario and Transaction are launched in the exact order they are defined in the load test (see below for more detail on how this works). When configured to allocate in a Random order, Scenarios are assigned to users at random and each Scenario’s transactions run in a random order, so running the same load test multiple times can lead to different amounts of load being generated.

Prior to Goose 0.10.6 Scenario were allocated in a serial order. Prior to Goose 0.11.1 Transaction were allocated in a serial order. To restore the old behavior, you can use the GooseAttack::set_scheduler() method as follows:

    GooseAttack::initialize()?
        .set_scheduler(GooseScheduler::Serial);

To instead assign Scenario to users at random, and to run each Scenario’s transactions in a random order, you can instead configure as follows:

    GooseAttack::initialize()?
        .set_scheduler(GooseScheduler::Random);

The following configuration is possible but superfluous because it is the scheduling default, and is therefore how Goose behaves even if the .set_scheduler() method is not called at all:

    GooseAttack::initialize()?
        .set_scheduler(GooseScheduler::RoundRobin);

Scheduling Example

The following simple example helps illustrate how the different schedulers work.

use goose::prelude::*;

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), GooseError> {
    GooseAttack::initialize()?
        .register_scenario(scenario!("Scenario1")
            .register_transaction(transaction!(transaction1).set_weight(2)?)
            .register_transaction(transaction!(transaction2))
            .set_weight(2)?
        )
        .register_scenario(scenario!("Scenario2")
            .register_transaction(transaction!(transaction1))
            .register_transaction(transaction!(transaction2).set_weight(2)?)
        )
        .execute()
        .await?;

    Ok(())
}

Round Robin Scheduler

This first example assumes the default of .set_scheduler(GooseScheduler::RoundRobin).

If Goose is told to launch only two users, one GooseUser will run Scenario1 and the other will run Scenario2. Even though Scenario1 has a weight of 2 GooseUser are allocated round-robin so with only two users the second instance of Scenario1 is never launched. Goose launches the allocated users from the end of the list, so the first user launched runs Scenario2 and the second runs Scenario1. The first user launched is "user":0 in the request, transaction and scenario logs, and [user 1]: launching user in the console.

The GooseUser running Scenario1 will then launch transactions repeatedly in the following order: transaction1, transaction2, transaction1. If it runs through twice, then it runs all of the following transactions in the following order: transaction1, transaction2, transaction1, transaction1, transaction2, transaction1.

Serial Scheduler

This second example assumes the manual configuration of .set_scheduler(GooseScheduler::Serial).

If Goose is told to launch only two users, then both GooseUser will launch Scenario1 as it has a weight of 2. Scenario2 will not get assigned to either of the users.

Both GooseUser running Scenario1 will then launch transactions repeatedly in the following order: transaction1, transaction1, transaction2. If it runs through twice, then it runs all of the following transactions in the following order: transaction1, transaction1, transaction2, transaction1, transaction1, transaction2.

Random Scheduler

This third example assumes the manual configuration of .set_scheduler(GooseScheduler::Random).

Goose fills its list of users by picking a Scenario at random and taking one of that Scenario’s weighted places, until every place is taken. With weights of 2 and 1 the list holds Scenario1 twice and Scenario2 once, in a random order. If Goose is told to launch only two users, they may both run Scenario1, or one may run each, but never both Scenario2. If the load test is stopped and run again, the users are randomly re-assigned; there is no consistency between load test runs.

Goose builds each Scenario’s list of transactions as the Serial scheduler does, then shuffles the whole list once when the load test starts. Every GooseUser running that Scenario runs the same shuffled order, over and over, and the next run of the load test gets a new order. With Scenario1 the order is one of transaction1, transaction1, transaction2; transaction1, transaction2, transaction1; or transaction2, transaction1, transaction1.

Sequenced transactions are shuffled only within their sequence, so every transaction of a lower sequence still runs before any of a higher one. Transactions set with set_on_start() or set_on_stop() keep the Serial order.

Proxy Configuration

Goose supports routing traffic through HTTP, HTTPS, and SOCKS proxies by leveraging reqwest’s proxy capabilities. This is useful for testing applications through corporate proxies, debugging with tools like Burp Suite or OWASP ZAP, or routing traffic through specific network paths.

HTTP/HTTPS Proxies (No Setup Required)

HTTP and HTTPS proxies work out of the box with Goose - no compile-time changes needed. Simply set environment variables:

Important: HTTP_PROXY controls proxying for http:// URLs and HTTPS_PROXY for https:// URLs, whatever port they use. ALL_PROXY covers both, and HTTP_PROXY or HTTPS_PROXY wins over it when both are set. The proxy server itself typically runs on HTTP regardless of which requests it proxies.

# Set both to handle HTTP and HTTPS traffic
export HTTPS_PROXY=http://proxy.example.com:8080
export HTTP_PROXY=http://proxy.example.com:8080

# With authentication
export HTTPS_PROXY=http://username:password@proxy.example.com:8080
export HTTP_PROXY=http://username:password@proxy.example.com:8080

# Run your load test (no Cargo.toml changes needed)
cargo run --example simple -- --host https://example.com

SOCKS Proxy Support (Requires Setup)

To use SOCKS proxies with Goose, you need to enable SOCKS support in your project’s reqwest dependency.

Dependencies Setup

Add reqwest with the socks feature to your Cargo.toml:

[dependencies]
goose = "^0.19"
tokio = "^1"
reqwest = { version = "0.13", default-features = false, features = ["socks"] }

Note: Cargo automatically unifies features across all dependencies. Goose turns off reqwest’s default features and enables default-tls, form, gzip and json (and cookies with Goose’s default cookies feature), so adding socks in your own dependency results in reqwest being compiled with those features plus socks. Keep default-features = false on your reqwest line if you don’t want reqwest’s own defaults, such as http2 and system-proxy, added to the build.

Environment Variables

Configure SOCKS proxy using environment variables:

# Set both to handle HTTP and HTTPS traffic
export HTTPS_PROXY=socks5h://127.0.0.1:1080
export HTTP_PROXY=socks5h://127.0.0.1:1080

# Run your load test
cargo run --example simple -- --host https://example.com

SOCKS5 vs SOCKS5h

  • socks5:// - DNS resolution happens locally, only network traffic goes through proxy
  • socks5h:// - Both DNS resolution and network traffic go through the proxy (recommended)

Use socks5h:// when you need DNS queries to be resolved through the SOCKS proxy, which is typically the case for accessing internal networks or when you want all traffic routed through the proxy.

Common Use Cases

Security Testing with Burp Suite

export HTTPS_PROXY=http://127.0.0.1:8080
export HTTP_PROXY=http://127.0.0.1:8080
cargo run --example simple -- --host https://target.example.com

SSH SOCKS Tunnel

# Set up SSH tunnel with SOCKS proxy
ssh -D 1080 -N user@jump-server.example.com

# In another terminal, run load test through tunnel
export HTTPS_PROXY=socks5h://127.0.0.1:1080
export HTTP_PROXY=socks5h://127.0.0.1:1080
cargo run --example simple -- --host https://internal-app.example.com

Corporate Proxy

# Set both to proxy all traffic through corporate proxy
export HTTPS_PROXY=http://corporate-proxy.example.com:8080
export HTTP_PROXY=http://corporate-proxy.example.com:8080
cargo run --example simple -- --host https://external-site.com

Example Load Test

HTTP/HTTPS Proxy Example (Simple)

Cargo.toml (no special dependencies needed):

[package]
name = "my-loadtest"
version = "0.1.0"
edition = "2021"

[dependencies]
goose = "^0.19"
tokio = { version = "^1", features = ["macros", "rt-multi-thread"] }
# No special reqwest configuration needed for HTTP/HTTPS proxies

src/main.rs:

use goose::prelude::*;

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), GooseError> {
    GooseAttack::initialize()?
        .register_scenario(scenario!("LoadTest")
            .register_transaction(transaction!(homepage))
        )
        .set_default(GooseDefault::Host, "https://example.com")?
        .execute()
        .await?;

    Ok(())
}

async fn homepage(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let _goose_metrics = user.get("/").await?;
    Ok(())
}

Running with HTTP proxy:

export HTTPS_PROXY=http://proxy.example.com:8080
export HTTP_PROXY=http://proxy.example.com:8080
cargo run

SOCKS Proxy Example (Requires Setup)

Cargo.toml (with socks feature):

[package]
name = "my-loadtest"
version = "0.1.0"
edition = "2021"

[dependencies]
goose = "^0.19"
tokio = { version = "^1", features = ["macros", "rt-multi-thread"] }
# Add the socks feature; Cargo unifies it with the features Goose enables
reqwest = { version = "0.13", default-features = false, features = ["socks"] }

src/main.rs (same as above):

use goose::prelude::*;

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), GooseError> {
    GooseAttack::initialize()?
        .register_scenario(scenario!("LoadTest")
            .register_transaction(transaction!(homepage))
        )
        .set_default(GooseDefault::Host, "https://example.com")?
        .execute()
        .await?;

    Ok(())
}

async fn homepage(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let _goose_metrics = user.get("/").await?;
    Ok(())
}

Running with SOCKS proxy:

export HTTPS_PROXY=socks5h://127.0.0.1:1080
export HTTP_PROXY=socks5h://127.0.0.1:1080
cargo run

Troubleshooting

DNS Resolution Issues

If you see errors like “failed to lookup address information: No address associated with hostname”, try:

  • Use socks5h:// instead of socks5:// to route DNS through the proxy
  • Verify your SOCKS proxy is running and accessible
  • Test with curl: curl --proxy socks5h://127.0.0.1:1080 https://example.com

Authentication Issues

For proxies requiring authentication:

  • Include credentials in the proxy URL: socks5h://username:password@proxy:1080
  • Ensure special characters in credentials are URL-encoded

Feature Not Available

If you get compilation errors about SOCKS features:

  • Verify reqwest = { version = "0.13", default-features = false, features = ["socks"] } is in your Cargo.toml
  • Run cargo clean and rebuild your project

Additional Resources

For more detailed proxy configuration options, see the official reqwest documentation:

  • Reqwest Proxy Documentation - Complete guide to proxy configuration in reqwest
  • Environment Variables: HTTP_PROXY, HTTPS_PROXY, ALL_PROXY, NO_PROXY (and lowercase variants)
  • Programmatic Configuration: Using reqwest::Proxy for advanced proxy setups

Notes

  • All GooseUsers in your load test will route through the configured proxy, including users given their own client with GooseUser::set_client_builder, unless that client calls .no_proxy() or sets its own reqwest::Proxy
  • The proxy configuration applies to the entire load test
  • Both HTTP and HTTPS requests will use the proxy when configured
  • Environment variables (HTTP_PROXY, HTTPS_PROXY, NO_PROXY) are standard system proxy variables automatically recognized by reqwest, the HTTP client library that Goose uses internally
  • Goose does not define or manage these environment variables - they are set by you in your shell environment and consumed by reqwest
  • Proxies from these environment variables need no reqwest feature: reqwest reads them for every client unless the client calls .no_proxy(). Reqwest’s separate system-proxy feature, which reads the macOS and Windows system proxy settings, is one of reqwest’s default features and Goose turns it off, so those system settings are not used unless your own reqwest dependency enables system-proxy
  • SOCKS proxy support requires the socks feature to be explicitly enabled in your project’s reqwest dependency

Cookies

Goose’s cookies feature is one of its default features. With it, each GooseUser gets its own Reqwest client with its own cookie store, so a cookie the server sets for one user, such as a session cookie after a login, is sent back with that user’s later requests and never with another user’s.

Default Behavior

No configuration is needed:

use goose::prelude::*;

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), GooseError> {
    // No configuration needed: each user has its own cookie store.
    GooseAttack::initialize()?
        .register_scenario(
            scenario!("WebsiteUser")
                .register_transaction(transaction!(login).set_on_start())
                .register_transaction(transaction!(load_homepage)),
        )
        .execute()
        .await?;

    Ok(())
}

async fn login(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let params = [("username", "test_user"), ("password", "")];
    // A session cookie the server sets here is sent with this user's later requests.
    let _goose = user.post_form("/login", &params).await?;
    Ok(())
}

async fn load_homepage(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let _goose = user.get("/").await?;
    Ok(())
}

Goose builds each user’s client with the user agent goose/0.19.0, the --timeout, gzip unless --no-gzip, invalid certificates accepted only with --accept-invalid-certs, and a cookie store when the cookies feature is enabled. Every user has a client of its own whether or not it keeps cookies. In a run of examples/simple, 1,000 users used about 65 MB more memory than 10 users.

A Client Without Cookies

To change the client of a user, build a reqwest::ClientBuilder and pass it to GooseUser::set_client_builder, usually in a transaction run once with set_on_start(). Goose does not re-export Reqwest, so the load test needs its own reqwest = { version = "0.13", default-features = false } dependency. A client built without .cookie_store(true) keeps no cookies:

use goose::prelude::*;
use std::time::Duration;

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), GooseError> {
    GooseAttack::initialize()?
        .register_scenario(
            scenario!("ApiUser")
                .register_transaction(transaction!(setup_client).set_on_start())
                .register_transaction(transaction!(api_call)),
        )
        .execute()
        .await?;

    Ok(())
}

async fn setup_client(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    // Without .cookie_store(true) this user's client keeps no cookies.
    let builder = reqwest::Client::builder()
        .user_agent("my-loadtest/1.0")
        .timeout(Duration::from_secs(10));
    user.set_client_builder(builder).await?;
    Ok(())
}

async fn api_call(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let _goose = user.get("/api/status").await?;
    Ok(())
}

The new client replaces all of Goose’s defaults for that user, so set again any of them you need, such as the timeout. See the Basic Authentication example for a custom client that keeps cookies and adds default headers.

Building Without Cookie Support

To leave cookie support out of the build, turn off Goose’s default features:

[dependencies]
goose = { version = "^0.19", default-features = false }

Reqwest is then built without its cookies feature, unless another dependency turns it on, and no user’s client keeps cookies. ClientBuilder::cookie_store and cookie_provider only exist with Reqwest’s cookies feature, so a load test that calls them needs it, for example through its own reqwest = { version = "0.13", default-features = false, features = ["cookies"] } dependency. Leave Reqwest’s default features off: they include http2, which would let HTTPS requests use HTTP/2 where Goose keeps to HTTP/1.1.

The Type-Safe Client Builder

The goose::client module, which is not part of the prelude, has a GooseClientBuilder that records its cookie choice in its type: it starts in the CookiesEnabled state, without_cookies() moves it to CookiesDisabled and with_cookies() moves it back. timeout, user_agent, gzip and accept_invalid_certs are available in both states. GooseAttack::set_client_builder_with_cookies takes a CookiesEnabled builder and set_client_builder_without_cookies a CookiesDisabled one, and both return a Result:

use goose::client::GooseClientBuilder;
use goose::prelude::*;
use std::time::Duration;

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), GooseError> {
    // Starts in the CookiesEnabled state: each user gets its own client and cookie jar.
    let builder = GooseClientBuilder::new()
        .timeout(Duration::from_secs(30))
        .user_agent("my-loadtest/1.0");

    GooseAttack::initialize()?
        .set_client_builder_with_cookies(builder)?
        .register_scenario(
            scenario!("WebsiteUser").register_transaction(transaction!(load_homepage)),
        )
        .execute()
        .await?;

    Ok(())
}

async fn load_homepage(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let _goose = user.get("/").await?;
    Ok(())
}

set_client_builder_with_cookies gives every user a client of its own, built from the builder, with its own cookie jar. Without the cookies feature it returns GooseError::FeatureNotEnabled, as the clients could not keep cookies.

set_client_builder_without_cookies builds one client without a cookie store before any user starts, and every user shares it. Clones of a Reqwest client share one connection pool, so this suits a load test with many users that needs no cookies. If the client can’t be built, the load test fails before any user starts.

use goose::client::GooseClientBuilder;
use goose::prelude::*;
use std::time::Duration;

fn main() -> Result<(), GooseError> {
    // without_cookies() moves the builder to the CookiesDisabled state.
    let builder = GooseClientBuilder::new()
        .without_cookies()
        .timeout(Duration::from_secs(15))
        .user_agent("my-loadtest/1.0");

    let _attack = GooseAttack::initialize()?.set_client_builder_without_cookies(builder)?;

    Ok(())
}

With either setter:

  • The builder’s user agent replaces goose/0.19.0.
  • The timeout, gzip and invalid certificate settings each come from the first of: the option given on the command line (--timeout, --no-gzip, --accept-invalid-certs), the builder, a GooseDefault for the option, and Goose’s built in default. A setting the builder was not given keeps its GooseDefault or built in default.
  • The users that run the test_start and test_stop transactions, and users a Controller adds, get the builder’s clients too.
  • The two setters replace each other, so the last call wins.
  • A transaction can still give its user a different client with GooseUser::set_client_builder, as shown above. Under set_client_builder_without_cookies that moves the user off the shared client.

Creating a GooseUser Directly

Load tests don’t normally create a GooseUser, but code that does, such as a test of a transaction function, passes the scenario’s name to GooseUser::new as an Arc<str>; it was a String in 0.18. The last argument is an optional reqwest::Client; with None the user gets the default client.

use goose::prelude::*;
use goose::config::GooseConfiguration;
use std::sync::Arc;

fn main() -> Result<(), GooseError> {
    let configuration = GooseConfiguration::default();
    let base_url = reqwest::Url::parse("http://127.0.0.1:8080/").unwrap();
    let _user = GooseUser::new(0, Arc::from("websiteuser"), base_url, &configuration, 0, None)?;
    Ok(())
}

Examples

Goose ships eight example load tests. Each entry below says what the example demonstrates and what it needs, and, except for Drupal Memcache, shows a piece of a real run; select the picture to open the example’s page. Simple is the smallest place to start, and Umami is the closest to a real site.

Simple

GET and POST requests, a login that runs once per user, and a wait after each transaction except the login. Needs: any web server that answers POST /login, / and /about/ with 200. Source: examples/simple.rs

Closure

Transactions built in a loop, one closure per path. Needs: any web server that answers /, /about and /our-team with 200. Source: examples/closure.rs

Session

A token from a signup request, kept per user with set_session_data and sent as a bearer token. Needs: a server whose POST /signup answers JSON with a jwtToken, and whose / answers 200 to a request that carries it. Source: examples/session.rs

Basic Authentication

Three ways to send HTTP Basic auth, one scenario each, and which of them covers static assets. Needs: the httpbin.org paths, or a server that answers them. Source: examples/basic_auth.rs

GraphQL

GraphQL queries and a mutation sent with post_graphql and post_graphql_named, and how each names its request. Needs: a server at POST /graphql that answers the example’s four operations with 200; a real GraphQL server needs the user, posts and createPost fields the queries ask for. Source: examples/graphql_loadtest.rs

TCP Load Test

A raw TCP round trip timed by the transaction and recorded in Goose’s metrics with record_custom_request_duration. Needs: a TCP echo server. Source: examples/tcp_loadtest.rs

Drupal Memcache

A Locust load test ported to Goose: anonymous and logged in users, form tokens read from pages, and comments posted. Needs: a Drupal 7 site prepared as the drupal-loadtest repository describes, since the example posts Drupal 7 forms. Its page has no picture of a run: against the Drupal 11 Umami site that the Umami example uses, nearly all of the example’s node and profile requests answered 404. Source: examples/drupal_memcache.rs

Open the Drupal Memcache page

Umami

Three kinds of visitor (English, Spanish and admin), most of the site’s page types, forms, and each page’s images and scripts. Needs: a Drupal site installed with the Umami demo profile, with CSS and JS aggregation off and an admin account the example can log in with. Source: examples/umami/

Simple Example

The examples/simple.rs example copies the simple load test documented on the locust.io web page, rewritten in Rust for Goose. It shows how to GET and POST pages, with one scenario.

cargo run --release --features dashboard --example simple -- --host http://127.0.0.1:8080 -u 1024 -r 64 -t 3m --no-reset-metrics --dashboard -q, against a local nginx server that answers POST /login, / and /about/ with 200, 120 seconds in.

Each user logs in once with POST /login, then loads / and /about/ in turn, pausing 5 to 15 seconds after each of those two but not after the login. During the ramp each new user sends its login and its first page before its first pause, which is why the chart starts near 128 a second (64 new users a second, each sending two requests). From about 5 seconds in, the first users come back from their pauses and add to that, so it climbs to about 200 until all 1,024 users have started, then drops when the new users stop. After that the pause holds 1,024 users to about 100 requests a second.

A separate run measured Goose itself: -u 1024 -r 64 -t 120s --no-telnet -q, without the dashboard or the telnet Controller, against the same server on an Apple M5 Max. While all 1,024 users ran, Goose held at 77 MB of memory and used under 3% of one core. It made 12,245 requests in the 120 seconds after the last user started, and none of them failed.

With -q Goose does not print the dashboard’s address when it starts: open http://127.0.0.1:5118 while the test runs. If that tab is still open when the run ends, Goose prints the address and waits for Ctrl-C.

Complete Source Code

//! Simple Goose load test example. Duplicates the simple example on the
//! Locust project page (<https://locust.io/>).
//!
//! ## License
//!
//! Copyright 2020-2022 Jeremy Andrews
//!
//! Licensed under the Apache License, Version 2.0 (the "License");
//! you may not use this file except in compliance with the License.
//! You may obtain a copy of the License at
//!
//! <http://www.apache.org/licenses/LICENSE-2.0>
//!
//! Unless required by applicable law or agreed to in writing, software
//! distributed under the License is distributed on an "AS IS" BASIS,
//! WITHOUT WARRANTIES OR CONDITIONS OF ANY KIND, either express or implied.
//! See the License for the specific language governing permissions and
//! limitations under the License.

use goose::prelude::*;
use std::time::Duration;

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), GooseError> {
    GooseAttack::initialize()?
        // In this example, we only create a single scenario, named "WebsiteUser".
        .register_scenario(
            scenario!("WebsiteUser")
                // After each transactions runs, sleep randomly from 5 to 15 seconds.
                .set_wait_time(Duration::from_secs(5), Duration::from_secs(15))?
                // This transaction only runs one time when the user first starts.
                .register_transaction(transaction!(website_login).set_on_start())
                // These next two transactions run repeatedly as long as the load test is running.
                .register_transaction(transaction!(website_index))
                .register_transaction(transaction!(website_about)),
        )
        .execute()
        .await?;

    Ok(())
}

/// Demonstrates how to log in when a user starts. We flag this transaction as an
/// on_start transaction when registering it above. This means it only runs one time
/// per user, when the user thread first starts.
async fn website_login(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let params = [("username", "test_user"), ("password", "")];
    let _goose = user.post_form("/login", &params).await?;

    Ok(())
}

/// A very simple transaction that simply loads the front page.
async fn website_index(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let _goose = user.get("/").await?;

    Ok(())
}

/// A very simple transaction that simply loads the about page.
async fn website_about(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let _goose = user.get("/about/").await?;

    Ok(())
}

Closure Example

The examples/closure.rs example loads three different pages on a web site. Instead of defining a hard coded Transaction function for each, the paths are passed in via a vector and the TransactionFunction is dynamically created in a closure.

cargo run --release --features dashboard --example closure -- --host http://127.0.0.1:8080 -u 200 -r 50 -t 3m --no-reset-metrics --dashboard -q, against a local nginx server that answers /, /about and /our-team with 200, 100 seconds in: one request row for each path in the vector.

Each user pauses 5 to 15 seconds after each transaction, so 200 users make about 20 requests a second; each user loads the three paths in turn, so the counts fall in the vector’s order. The closures carry no names, so the dashboard’s Transactions table lists them by number alone, 1.1 to 1.3; the request names are the paths. With -q Goose does not print the dashboard’s address when it starts: open http://127.0.0.1:5118 while the test runs. If that tab is still open when the run ends, Goose prints the address and waits for Ctrl-C.

Details

The paths to be loaded are first defined in a vector:

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
    let paths = vec!["/", "/about", "/our-team"];
}

A transaction function for each path is then dynamically created as a closure:

    for request_path in paths {
        let path = request_path;

        let closure: TransactionFunction = Arc::new(move |user| {
            Box::pin(async move {
                let _goose = user.get(path).await?;

                Ok(())
            })
        });

Complete Source Code

//! Simple Goose load test example using closures.
//!
//! ## License
//!
//! Copyright 2020 Fabian Franz
//!
//! Licensed under the Apache License, Version 2.0 (the "License");
//! you may not use this file except in compliance with the License.
//! You may obtain a copy of the License at
//!
//! <http://www.apache.org/licenses/LICENSE-2.0>
//!
//! Unless required by applicable law or agreed to in writing, software
//! distributed under the License is distributed on an "AS IS" BASIS,
//! WITHOUT WARRANTIES OR CONDITIONS OF ANY KIND, either express or implied.
//! See the License for the specific language governing permissions and
//! limitations under the License.

use goose::prelude::*;
use std::boxed::Box;
use std::sync::Arc;
use std::time::Duration;

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), GooseError> {
    let mut scenario = scenario!("WebsiteUser")
        // After each transaction runs, sleep randomly from 5 to 15 seconds.
        .set_wait_time(Duration::from_secs(5), Duration::from_secs(15))?;

    let paths = vec!["/", "/about", "/our-team"];
    for request_path in paths {
        let path = request_path;

        let closure: TransactionFunction = Arc::new(move |user| {
            Box::pin(async move {
                let _goose = user.get(path).await?;

                Ok(())
            })
        });

        let transaction = Transaction::new(closure);
        // We need to do the variable dance as scenario.register_transaction returns self and hence moves
        // self out of `scenario`. By storing it in a new local variable and then moving it over
        // we can avoid that error.
        let new_scenario = scenario.register_transaction(transaction);
        scenario = new_scenario;
    }

    GooseAttack::initialize()?
        // In this example, we only create a single scenario, named "WebsiteUser".
        .register_scenario(scenario)
        .execute()
        .await?;

    Ok(())
}

Session Example

The examples/session.rs example demonstrates how you can add JWT authentication support to your load test, making use of the GooseUserData trait. In this example, the session is recorded in the GooseUser object with set_session_data, and retrieved with get_session_data_unchecked.

The HTML report’s Request Metrics, from cargo run --release --features dashboard --example session -- --host http://127.0.0.1:8081 -u 100 -r 20 -t 2m --no-reset-metrics --report-file --dashboard -q against a local nginx server whose POST /signup answers {"jwtToken":"goose-example-token"}; the run writes report.html and report.json to goose-runs/<YYYYMMDD-HHMMSS>/.

POST /signup ran 100 times, once for each of the 100 users, because it is the on start transaction. The server answers / with 401 to any request without the token, and Goose counts a 401 as a failed request, so 0 fails on GET / means every one of its 1,285 requests carried the token the user stored. With -q Goose does not print the dashboard’s address when it starts: open http://127.0.0.1:5118 while the test runs. If that tab is still open when the run ends, Goose prints the address and waits for Ctrl-C.

Details

In this example, the GooseUserData is a simple struct containing a string. Goose implements GooseUserData for every type that is Clone, Send, Sync and 'static, so the struct only needs to derive Clone:

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
#[derive(Clone)]
struct Session {
    jwt_token: String,
}
}

Passing set_session_data a type that is not Clone does not compile.

Each user builds the struct from the JSON its signup request returns, and stores it in its own GooseUser:

    user.set_session_data(Session {
        jwt_token: response.jwt_token,
    });

The session data is retrieved from the GooseUser object with each subsequent request. The example does no validation:

async fn authenticated_index(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    // This will panic if the session is missing or if the session is not of the right type.
    // Use `get_session_data` to handle a missing session.
    let session = user.get_session_data_unchecked::<Session>();

    // Create a Reqwest RequestBuilder object and configure bearer authentication when making
    // a GET request for the index.
    let reqwest_request_builder = user
        .get_request_builder(&GooseMethod::Get, "/")?
        .bearer_auth(&session.jwt_token);

    // Add the manually created RequestBuilder and build a GooseRequest object. The
    // path names the request in the metrics, as the RequestBuilder already has its URL.
    let goose_request = GooseRequest::builder()
        .path("/")
        .set_request_builder(reqwest_request_builder)
        .build();

    // Make the actual request.
    user.request(goose_request).await?;

    Ok(())
}

If POST /signup does not answer with JSON holding a jwtToken, the user stores no session data, and get_session_data_unchecked panics with Missing session data!.

Complete Source Code

//! Goose load test example, leveraging the per-GooseUser `GooseUserData` field
// to store a per-user session JWT authentication token.
//!
//! ## License
//!
//! Copyright 2020-2022 Jeremy Andrews
//!
//! Licensed under the Apache License, Version 2.0 (the "License");
//! you may not use this file except in compliance with the License.
//! You may obtain a copy of the License at
//!
//! <http://www.apache.org/licenses/LICENSE-2.0>
//!
//! Unless required by applicable law or agreed to in writing, software
//! distributed under the License is distributed on an "AS IS" BASIS,
//! WITHOUT WARRANTIES OR CONDITIONS OF ANY KIND, either express or implied.
//! See the License for the specific language governing permissions and
//! limitations under the License.

use goose::prelude::*;
use serde::Deserialize;
use std::time::Duration;

#[derive(Clone)]
struct Session {
    jwt_token: String,
}

#[derive(Deserialize)]
#[serde(rename_all = "camelCase")]
struct AuthenticationResponse {
    jwt_token: String,
}

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), GooseError> {
    GooseAttack::initialize()?
        // In this example, we only create a single scenario, named "WebsiteUser".
        .register_scenario(
            scenario!("WebsiteUser")
                // After each transaction runs, sleep randomly from 5 to 15 seconds.
                .set_wait_time(Duration::from_secs(5), Duration::from_secs(15))?
                // This transaction only runs one time when the user first starts.
                .register_transaction(transaction!(website_signup).set_on_start())
                // These next two transactions run repeatedly as long as the load test is running.
                .register_transaction(transaction!(authenticated_index)),
        )
        .execute()
        .await?;

    Ok(())
}

/// Demonstrates how to log in and set a session when a user starts. We flag this transaction as an
/// on_start transaction when registering it above. This means it only runs one time
/// per user, when the user thread first starts.
async fn website_signup(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let params = [("username", "test_user"), ("password", "")];
    let response = match user.post_form("/signup", &params).await?.response {
        Ok(r) => match r.json::<AuthenticationResponse>().await {
            Ok(j) => j,
            Err(e) => return Err(Box::new(e.into())),
        },
        Err(e) => return Err(Box::new(e.into())),
    };

    user.set_session_data(Session {
        jwt_token: response.jwt_token,
    });

    Ok(())
}

/// A very simple transaction that simply loads the front page.
async fn authenticated_index(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    // This will panic if the session is missing or if the session is not of the right type.
    // Use `get_session_data` to handle a missing session.
    let session = user.get_session_data_unchecked::<Session>();

    // Create a Reqwest RequestBuilder object and configure bearer authentication when making
    // a GET request for the index.
    let reqwest_request_builder = user
        .get_request_builder(&GooseMethod::Get, "/")?
        .bearer_auth(&session.jwt_token);

    // Add the manually created RequestBuilder and build a GooseRequest object. The
    // path names the request in the metrics, as the RequestBuilder already has its URL.
    let goose_request = GooseRequest::builder()
        .path("/")
        .set_request_builder(reqwest_request_builder)
        .build();

    // Make the actual request.
    user.request(goose_request).await?;

    Ok(())
}

Basic Authentication Example

The basic_auth.rs example demonstrates different approaches to handling HTTP Basic Authentication in Goose load tests. It answers issue #608, how to test a site behind Basic Auth, including when static assets (CSS, JS, images) need the credentials too.

The HTML report’s Request Metrics, from cargo run --release --features dashboard --example basic_auth -- --host http://127.0.0.1:8080 -u 60 -r 20 -t 2m --no-reset-metrics --report-file --dashboard -q against a local nginx server that answers the example’s four httpbin.org paths; the run writes report.html and report.json to goose-runs/<YYYYMMDD-HHMMSS>/.

The server answers /basic-auth/user/passwd with 401 unless the request has Basic auth for the user user with the password passwd, and Goose counts a 401 as a failed request. No request failed, including all 1,854 to the protected path, so all three approaches below sent the credentials. With -q Goose does not print the dashboard’s address when it starts: open http://127.0.0.1:5118 while the test runs. If that tab is still open when the run ends, Goose prints the address and waits for Ctrl-C.

The same run’s Transaction Metrics: one group for each approach.

The example names no transaction, so the report lists each by number alone, scenario then transaction, from 0; the dashboard and the console count from 1. Row 0.0 is the on start setup_basic_auth_client, which sends no request of its own. It ran 30 times, once for each CustomClientAuth user: the weights 3, 2 and 1 give 30, 20 and 10 of the 60. The other rows follow the order of the table under Solutions Demonstrated.

Problem Statement

When load testing a site protected by HTTP Basic Authentication:

  1. Static assets: credentials added to one request do not reach the CSS, JavaScript and image requests its page leads to, so those fail when they need Basic Auth too
  2. Code duplication: adding the credentials to every request by hand repeats the same code

Solutions Demonstrated

The example shows three different approaches to handle Basic Auth, one per scenario:

ScenarioWeightTransactionsRequests
CustomClientAuth3setup_basic_auth_client (on start), test_basic_auth_endpoint, test_user_agent_endpoint, test_json_endpointGET /basic-auth/user/passwd, GET /user-agent, GET /json
HelperFunctionAuth2test_with_helper_function, test_post_with_helperGET /basic-auth/user/passwd, POST /post
ManualPerRequestAuth1test_manual_basic_auth, test_manual_with_validationGET /basic-auth/user/passwd, twice

Every scenario waits 1 to 3 seconds after each transaction except the on start one. The paths are those of httpbin.org, where /basic-auth/user/passwd answers 200 only to the user user with the password passwd.

The excerpts below are taken from the example, where they sit inside a module compiled only with the cookies feature. That feature is on by default; built without it, the example prints an error and exits.

1. Custom Client with Default Headers (Recommended)

This approach sets up the HTTP client once per user with default Basic Auth headers. All subsequent requests automatically include the authentication headers, including requests for static assets.

async fn setup_basic_auth_client(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    // Get credentials from environment variables
    let (username, password) = get_basic_auth_credentials()?;

    // Use reqwest's built-in basic_auth() to generate a properly encoded
    // Authorization header, then extract it for use as a default header.
    let temp_request = reqwest::Client::new()
        .get("http://localhost")
        .basic_auth(&username, Some(&password))
        .build()
        .map_err(TransactionError::Reqwest)?;

    let mut headers = header::HeaderMap::new();
    if let Some(auth_header) = temp_request.headers().get(header::AUTHORIZATION) {
        headers.insert(header::AUTHORIZATION, auth_header.clone());
    }

    // Set up the client builder with default headers and other common settings
    // Enable cookie store for maintaining session state with Basic Auth
    let builder = Client::builder()
        .user_agent("Goose/1.0 BasicAuth Example")
        .default_headers(headers)
        .timeout(Duration::from_secs(30))
        .cookie_store(true);

    // Apply the custom client to this user
    user.set_client_builder(builder).await?;

    Ok(())
}

Advantages:

  • Set up once per user
  • Applies to every request the user makes, static assets included
  • Works with helpers that load a page’s static assets, such as validate_and_load_static_assets in the Umami example

2. Helper Function Approach

This approach uses a helper function that adds Basic Auth to each request.

async fn send_authenticated_request(
    user: &mut GooseUser,
    method: &GooseMethod,
    path: &str,
) -> Result<GooseResponse, Box<TransactionError>> {
    let mut reqwest_request_builder = user.get_request_builder(method, path)?;

    // Check for Basic Auth credentials in environment
    if let Ok((username, password)) = get_basic_auth_credentials() {
        reqwest_request_builder = reqwest_request_builder.basic_auth(username, Some(password));
    }

    // Build the GooseRequest and make the request. The path names the request in
    // the metrics, as the RequestBuilder already has its URL.
    let goose_request = GooseRequest::builder()
        .path(path)
        .set_request_builder(reqwest_request_builder)
        .build();

    let goose_response = user.request(goose_request).await?;
    Ok(goose_response)
}

Advantages:

  • Control over each request
  • Can apply authentication to some requests and not others

Disadvantages:

  • Must be called for every request
  • Does not reach static asset requests

The request is built from a Reqwest RequestBuilder, which already holds the URL, so the helper also calls .path(path) on the GooseRequest builder: the path names the request in the metrics, as GET /basic-auth/user/passwd and POST /post. Without .path() or .name("..."), Goose would name it by its method alone, as GET or POST.

3. Manual Per-Request Authentication

This approach manually adds Basic Auth to each individual request using the request builder.

async fn test_manual_basic_auth(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let (username, password) = get_basic_auth_credentials()?;

    let reqwest_request_builder = user
        .get_request_builder(&GooseMethod::Get, "/basic-auth/user/passwd")?
        .basic_auth(username, Some(password));

    let goose_request = GooseRequest::builder()
        .path("/basic-auth/user/passwd")
        .set_request_builder(reqwest_request_builder)
        .build();

    let _goose_metrics = user.request(goose_request).await?;

    Ok(())
}

Disadvantages:

  • Repeats the most code of the three approaches
  • Does not reach static asset requests, the problem issue #608 describes
  • Must name each request with .path() or .name(), as in the helper function approach, or it is named by its method alone in the metrics

Configuration

Environment Variables

Separate variables:

export BASIC_AUTH_USERNAME="your_username"
export BASIC_AUTH_PASSWORD="your_password"

Combined format:

export BASIC_AUTH="username:password"

BASIC_AUTH_USERNAME and BASIC_AUTH_PASSWORD are used when both are set; otherwise BASIC_AUTH is used, split at its first colon, so the password may contain colons. A BASIC_AUTH without a colon is an error. The manual approach’s transactions then fail immediately, because each returns the error, while the helper function sends its requests without credentials. The custom client’s on start transaction fails too, but Goose goes on with its standard client for that user. Those requests also go out without credentials, and the protected path answers them with 401.

Default Credentials

If neither the pair nor BASIC_AUTH is set, the example uses the credentials user:passwd, which httpbin.org accepts. Each time it reads them it logs three warning lines, even with -q, and it reads them once per helper function request, once per manual transaction and once per custom client user. Setting the variables quiets those; test_manual_with_validation still warns after every 200 that static assets would fail.

Running the Example

# Using httpbin.org for testing (uses default credentials)
cargo run --example basic_auth -- --host http://httpbin.org --users 5 --run-time 30s

# With custom credentials
BASIC_AUTH_USERNAME="myuser" BASIC_AUTH_PASSWORD="mypass" \
cargo run --example basic_auth -- --host https://your-protected-site.com --users 10 --run-time 60s

# Using combined format
BASIC_AUTH="myuser:mypass" \
cargo run --example basic_auth -- --host https://your-protected-site.com --users 10 --run-time 60s

Against another site the example still requests the httpbin.org paths in the table above, so change them to paths on your site first.

Key Takeaways

  1. Use the custom client approach for most sites: it sends credentials with every request the user makes, CSS, JS and image requests included
  2. Set up authentication once per user, in an on start transaction, rather than in every request
  3. Use environment variables for credentials to keep them out of your code
  4. Try your approach against httpbin.org before pointing it at your own site

Related Issues

This example addresses GitHub issue #608: “How can I test a site behind Basic Auth”.

GraphQL

Goose 0.19 adds two helper methods that send GraphQL queries and mutations.

cargo run --release --features dashboard --example graphql_loadtest -- --host http://127.0.0.1:4400 -u 5 -r 5 -t 3m --no-reset-metrics --dashboard -q, against a local Node server that answers each of the example’s four operations at POST /graphql with a 200 and a JSON data body without delay, 100 seconds in.

The transactions are named with set_name(), and three of their requests with post_graphql_named(). Simple Query’s request is sent with post_graphql(), which names it by its path, POST /graphql. No transaction waits, and this server answers without delay, so 5 users send about 72,000 requests a second; the Sample Output below comes from a slower server. With -q Goose does not print the dashboard’s address when it starts: open http://127.0.0.1:5118 while the test runs. If that tab is still open when the run ends, Goose prints the address and waits for Ctrl-C.

GraphQL Helper Methods

  • post_graphql(): POST a GraphQL query as JSON to a path; the metrics name the request by its path, for example POST /graphql
  • post_graphql_named(): the same, with a name for the request in the metrics, for example POST get user by id

Both take the request body as anything that implements serde::Serialize, usually a serde_json::Value built with json!(), and return a GooseResponse, like the other request helpers.

GraphQL Endpoint Configuration

There is no configured GraphQL endpoint: the path is the first argument of each call, and Goose prepends the host as it does for every request. By convention, most GraphQL APIs use /graphql, but you can specify any path. In this fragment query is a serde_json::Value:

// Using the conventional /graphql endpoint
let _goose = user.post_graphql("/graphql", &query).await?;

// Using a custom endpoint
let _goose = user.post_graphql("/api/graphql", &query).await?;

Example: GraphQL Load Test

The examples/graphql_loadtest.rs example defines one scenario, GraphQL Load Test, with four transactions. No transaction sets a weight, and the scenario sets no wait time, so each user runs the four in turn, without pausing, for as long as the load test runs. Each one POSTs to /graphql:

TransactionSendsRequest name in the metrics
Simple Querythe query { __typename }, with post_graphql()POST /graphql
User Querya GetUser query with the variable idPOST get user by id
Complex Querya GetUserWithPosts query for a user’s posts and their comments, with the variables userId and firstPOST complex user posts query
Mutationa CreatePost mutation with an input variablePOST create post

Key Features

JSON Query Construction

Each query is a serde_json::Value built with json!().

Variables

Variables go in the JSON value’s variables field, beside its query field, as User Query, Complex Query and Mutation show.

Named Requests

post_graphql_named() gives each operation its own row in the metrics. Every request made with post_graphql() to the same path shares one row.

Weighted Transactions

set_weight() makes one operation run more often than another, such as more reads than writes. The example leaves every transaction at the default weight of 1.

Advanced Usage

Error Handling

Goose judges a GraphQL request by its HTTP status code, like any other request, so a GraphQL error returned with a 200 counts as a success. To catch those, read the response body and call set_failure:

async fn validated_graphql_transaction(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let query = json!({
        "query": "query GetUser($id: ID!) { user(id: $id) { id name } }",
        "variables": { "id": "invalid-id" }
    });

    let mut goose = user
        .post_graphql_named("/graphql", &query, "get user with validation")
        .await?;

    if let Ok(response) = goose.response {
        match response.json::<serde_json::Value>().await {
            Ok(json) => {
                if json.get("errors").is_some() {
                    return user.set_failure("GraphQL errors returned", &mut goose.request, None, None);
                }
            }
            Err(_) => {
                return user.set_failure("Invalid JSON response", &mut goose.request, None, None);
            }
        }
    }

    Ok(())
}

Custom Headers

If your GraphQL API requires authentication or custom headers, you can use the lower-level request building approach:

async fn authenticated_graphql_transaction(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let query = json!({
        "query": "query GetPrivateData { privateData { id value } }"
    });

    // Build custom request with authentication header
    let request_builder = user
        .get_request_builder(&GooseMethod::Post, "/graphql")?
        .header("Authorization", "Bearer your-token-here")
        .json(&query);

    let goose_request = GooseRequest::builder()
        .set_request_builder(request_builder)
        .name("authenticated query")
        .build();

    let _goose = user.request(goose_request).await?;
    Ok(())
}

Both are fragments: they need use goose::prelude::*; and use serde_json::json;, and a scenario that registers them.
