Key Observations

Each request made with post_graphql_named() appears under its own name, such as POST get user by id, and the one made with post_graphql() under its path, POST /graphql. With no weights set, each transaction runs about as often as the others, so the four requests have about the same counts. Every request returned 200.

Complete Source Code

//! Example demonstrating GraphQL load testing with Goose.
//!
//! This example shows how to use Goose's GraphQL helper methods to load test
//! a GraphQL API. The GraphQL helpers use the existing HTTP infrastructure
//! and provide convenient methods for making GraphQL queries.
//!
//! ## Usage
//!
//! To compile and run this example:
//!
//! ```bash
//! cargo run --example graphql_loadtest -- --host https://api.example.com
//! ```
//!
//! ## Configuration Options
//!
//! - `--host`: Target host to load test
//! - `--users`: Number of concurrent users (default: one per CPU)
//! - `--run-time`: Duration to run the test (e.g., "30s", "5m")
//!
//! ## GraphQL Helper Methods
//!
//! Goose provides two GraphQL helper methods:
//! - `post_graphql()`: Basic GraphQL queries
//! - `post_graphql_named()`: Named GraphQL queries for better metrics tracking
//!
//! ## Example Commands
//!
//! Basic GraphQL load test:
//! ```bash
//! cargo run --example graphql_loadtest -- --host https://api.github.com --users 5 --run-time 30s
//! ```
//!
//! Using a custom GraphQL endpoint path:
//! ```bash
//! cargo run --example graphql_loadtest -- --host https://api.example.com --users 10 --run-time 1m
//! ```

use goose::prelude::*;
use serde_json::json;

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), GooseError> {
    GooseAttack::initialize()?
        .register_scenario(
            scenario!("GraphQL Load Test")
                .set_weight(1)?
                .register_transaction(transaction!(simple_query).set_name("Simple Query"))
                .register_transaction(transaction!(user_query).set_name("User Query"))
                .register_transaction(transaction!(complex_query).set_name("Complex Query"))
                .register_transaction(transaction!(mutation_example).set_name("Mutation")),
        )
        .execute()
        .await?;

    Ok(())
}

/// Simple GraphQL query example
async fn simple_query(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let query = json!({
        "query": "{ __typename }"
    });

    let _goose = user.post_graphql("/graphql", &query).await?;
    Ok(())
}

/// User query with variables
async fn user_query(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let query = json!({
        "query": "query GetUser($id: ID!) { user(id: $id) { id name email } }",
        "variables": {
            "id": "123"
        }
    });

    // Use named query for better metrics tracking
    let _goose = user
        .post_graphql_named("/graphql", &query, "get user by id")
        .await?;
    Ok(())
}

/// Complex query with multiple fields and nested data
async fn complex_query(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let query = json!({
        "query": r#"
            query GetUserWithPosts($userId: ID!, $first: Int!) {
                user(id: $userId) {
                    id
                    name
                    email
                    posts(first: $first) {
                        edges {
                            node {
                                id
                                title
                                content
                                createdAt
                                comments(first: 5) {
                                    edges {
                                        node {
                                            id
                                            content
                                            author {
                                                name
                                            }
                                        }
                                    }
                                }
                            }
                        }
                    }
                }
            }
        "#,
        "variables": {
            "userId": "456",
            "first": 10
        }
    });

    let _goose = user
        .post_graphql_named("/graphql", &query, "complex user posts query")
        .await?;
    Ok(())
}

/// GraphQL mutation example
async fn mutation_example(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let mutation = json!({
        "query": r#"
            mutation CreatePost($input: CreatePostInput!) {
                createPost(input: $input) {
                    id
                    title
                    content
                    author {
                        id
                        name
                    }
                }
            }
        "#,
        "variables": {
            "input": {
                "title": "Load Test Post",
                "content": "This post was created during a load test",
                "authorId": "789"
            }
        }
    });

    let _goose = user
        .post_graphql_named("/graphql", &mutation, "create post")
        .await?;
    Ok(())
}

#[cfg(test)]
mod tests {
    use super::*;

    #[tokio::test]
    async fn test_graphql_transactions() {
        // Test that transactions can be created without errors
        let simple_transaction = transaction!(simple_query).set_name("Simple Query");
        let user_transaction = transaction!(user_query).set_name("User Query");
        let complex_transaction = transaction!(complex_query).set_name("Complex Query");
        let mutation_transaction = transaction!(mutation_example).set_name("Mutation");

        assert_eq!(simple_transaction.name, "Simple Query");
        assert_eq!(user_transaction.name, "User Query");
        assert_eq!(complex_transaction.name, "Complex Query");
        assert_eq!(mutation_transaction.name, "Mutation");
    }

    #[test]
    fn test_scenario_creation() {
        // Test that the scenario can be created properly
        let scenario = scenario!("GraphQL Load Test")
            .set_weight(1)
            .unwrap()
            .register_transaction(transaction!(simple_query).set_name("Simple Query"))
            .register_transaction(transaction!(user_query).set_name("User Query"))
            .register_transaction(transaction!(complex_query).set_name("Complex Query"))
            .register_transaction(transaction!(mutation_example).set_name("Mutation"));

        assert_eq!(scenario.name, "GraphQL Load Test");
        assert_eq!(scenario.weight, 1);
        assert_eq!(scenario.transactions.len(), 4);
    }

    #[test]
    fn test_json_query_creation() {
        // Test that JSON queries can be created properly
        let query = json!({
            "query": "{ __typename }"
        });

        assert!(query.is_object());
        assert!(query.get("query").is_some());
        assert_eq!(query["query"], "{ __typename }");
    }

    #[test]
    fn test_complex_json_query() {
        // Test complex query with variables
        let query = json!({
            "query": "query GetUser($id: ID!) { user(id: $id) { id name } }",
            "variables": {
                "id": "123"
            }
        });

        assert!(query.is_object());
        assert!(query.get("query").is_some());
        assert!(query.get("variables").is_some());
        assert_eq!(query["variables"]["id"], "123");
    }
}

TCP Load Test

The examples/tcp_loadtest.rs example demonstrates how to use Goose to load test a raw TCP server. While Goose is primarily an HTTP load testing framework, its metrics pipeline supports any protocol through GooseUser::record_custom_request and GooseUser::record_custom_request_duration, both new in 0.19. The example uses record_custom_request_duration.

cargo run --release --features dashboard --example tcp_loadtest -- --host tcp://127.0.0.1:9000 -u 10 -r 10 -t 3m --no-reset-metrics --dashboard -q, against a small local Node echo server that writes each connection’s bytes back to it, about 100 seconds in.

The latency image shows the left part of the dashboard’s Response times by second card, the run’s first 77 seconds; the summary under its chart is the mean of the last 10 drawn seconds, which fall to the right of that part. Each user pauses 100 to 500 ms after every round trip, so 10 users make about 33 round trips a second. The example records each round trip as a Duration, so Goose keeps the fraction of a millisecond: most fall between 0.2 and 0.5 ms, and they average 0.31 ms. as_millis() truncates, so with record_custom_request every round trip under 1 ms would have been recorded as 0 ms. With -q Goose does not print the dashboard’s address when it starts: open http://127.0.0.1:5118 while the test runs. If that tab is still open when the run ends, Goose prints the address and waits for Ctrl-C.

Load testing other protocols

Some services communicate over raw TCP, UDP, WebSocket, gRPC, or other protocols rather than HTTP. Before 0.19, the only way to record metrics for these operations was to construct fake HTTP GooseRequest objects, which produced misleading results (spurious 404 errors). With record_custom_request, you time the operation yourself and hand the result to Goose.

The API

user.record_custom_request(
    method,        // &str: protocol label shown in metrics (e.g. "TCP", "GRPC", "WS")
    name,          // &str: identifies this operation in metrics output
    response_time, // u64: how long the operation took, in milliseconds
    success,       // bool: whether the operation succeeded
    status_code,   // Option<u16>: protocol-specific status code, or None if not applicable
    error,         // Option<&str>: failure reason; ignored when success is true
).await?;

This is a fragment: it runs inside a transaction function, with the six values already set.

Recorded metrics appear in all Goose reports under the method label you provide. These are the final request tables of the command under Running the example, a different run from the dashboard run above, against a different echo server, so their times differ:

 === PER REQUEST METRICS ===
 ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 Name                     |        # reqs |        # fails |    req/s |  fail/s
 ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 TCP tcp_echo             |         1,194 |         0 (0%) |    29.85 |    0.00
 ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 Name                     |    Avg (ms) |        Min |         Max |     Median
 ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 TCP tcp_echo             |        0.95 |       0.29 |          15 |       0.91
 ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 Slowest page load within specified percentile of requests (in ms):
 ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 Name                     |    50% |    75% |    98% |    99% |  99.9% | 99.99%
 ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 TCP tcp_echo             |   0.91 |   1.05 |   1.64 |    1.8 |    4.9 |     15
 ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 Name                     |                                        Status codes 
 ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 TCP tcp_echo             |                                           1,194 [0]
 -------------------------+----------------------------------------------------
 Aggregated               |                                           1,194 [0]

A custom request is counted like an HTTP request: in the # reqs, # fails, req/s and fail/s columns, in the Aggregated rows (the request tables print one only when there is more than one request name; the status code table always does), and in its response times and status codes.

Parameters

  • method: A short label for the protocol (e.g. "TCP", "GRPC", "WS", "MQTT"). Must not be empty or contain whitespace. Displayed in place of the HTTP method in all metrics output.
  • name: Identifies the specific operation within that protocol. Must not be empty or only whitespace. Combined with method to form the metrics key ("TCP tcp_echo").
  • response_time: The measured duration in milliseconds. as_millis() truncates to whole milliseconds, so a sub-millisecond operation is recorded as 0 ms; to keep its microseconds, as Goose does for HTTP requests, call record_custom_request_duration (below).
  • success: Whether the operation succeeded. Unlike HTTP requests where Goose infers success from the status code, for custom protocols you control this directly.
  • status_code: None when the protocol has no concept of status codes (e.g. raw TCP); Some(code) to record a protocol-specific code (e.g. a gRPC status code). Internally, None is stored as 0, so status code tables show [0] for these requests unless --no-status-codes turns those tables off.
  • error: An optional error description for failed operations. When success is false and error is None, Goose records <method> <name>: request failed.

record_custom_request_duration takes the same arguments, with the time as a Duration such as started.elapsed(), and keeps its microseconds.

Both return an error, and record nothing, when method or name breaks the rules above. They return Ok(()) even when success is false: recording a failure does not fail the transaction. To fail it as well, return an error from the transaction function after recording, for example Err(Box::new(TransactionError::Custom(message))). With --no-metrics nothing is recorded.

Coordinated Omission Mitigation applies to custom requests the same way it does to HTTP requests.

Goose records a custom request with the method GooseMethod::Custom and keeps the label you passed in raw.custom_method. GooseMethod::Custom is new in 0.19, so a match on GooseMethod with no wildcard arm needs an arm for it. It is not an HTTP method: get_request_builder(&GooseMethod::Custom, path) returns TransactionError::Custom with the text GooseMethod::Custom cannot be used with HTTP request builders; use record_custom_request() instead.

Running the example

Start a local TCP echo server:

ncat -l 9000 -k -e /bin/cat

Then run the load test, pointing --host at the server. Goose requires a URL with a scheme for validation, and any scheme works (e.g. tcp://, grpc://, ws://). The example reads the host and port from it for the TCP connection, and fails each transaction when the URL has no port:

cargo run --example tcp_loadtest -- \
  --host tcp://localhost:9000 \
  --users 10 \
  --run-time 30s \
  --no-reset-metrics

Adapting to other protocols

The same pattern works for any protocol: replace the TcpStream logic with UDP sockets, a WebSocket client, a gRPC stub, or any other I/O:

async fn my_custom_operation(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let started = std::time::Instant::now();

    // Perform the actual operation here (TCP, UDP, gRPC, etc.)
    let result: Result<_, _> = do_my_protocol_operation().await;

    let response_time = started.elapsed();

    match result {
        Ok(_) => {
            user.record_custom_request_duration(
                "GRPC",
                "operation_name",
                response_time,
                true,
                None,
                None,
            )
            .await?;
        }
        Err(e) => {
            user.record_custom_request_duration(
                "GRPC",
                "operation_name",
                response_time,
                false,
                None,
                Some(&e.to_string()),
            )
            .await?;
        }
    }

    Ok(())
}

This is a fragment: do_my_protocol_operation() stands for your own code.

Complete Source Code

//! Example showing how to load test a raw TCP server with Goose.
//!
//! Goose is primarily an HTTP load testing framework, but its metrics system can
//! track any protocol. This example demonstrates using
//! [`GooseUser::record_custom_request_duration`] to record timing and
//! success/failure information for TCP operations.
//!
//! # How it works
//!
//! 1. The target host and port are read from `--host` (e.g. `tcp://localhost:9000`).
//! 2. Each simulated user opens a TCP connection, sends a payload, and reads the echo.
//! 3. Timing is measured manually with [`std::time::Instant`].
//! 4. [`GooseUser::record_custom_request_duration`] records the result, to the
//!    microsecond. It appears in the Goose metrics output under the method
//!    label you provide (e.g. `TCP`).
//!
//! # Running the example
//!
//! Start a local TCP echo server (e.g. `ncat -l 9000 -k -e /bin/cat`), then:
//!
//! ```text
//! cargo run --example tcp_loadtest -- --host tcp://localhost:9000 --users 10 --run-time 30s --no-reset-metrics
//! ```
//!
//! The `--host` flag controls which server is targeted. Goose requires a URL with a
//! scheme for validation — any scheme works (e.g. `tcp://`, `grpc://`, `ws://`). The
//! host and port are extracted and used directly for the TCP connection.
//!
//! ## License
//!
//! Copyright 2020-2026 Jeremy Andrews
//!
//! Licensed under the Apache License, Version 2.0 (the "License");
//! you may not use this file except in compliance with the License.
//! You may obtain a copy of the License at
//!
//! <http://www.apache.org/licenses/LICENSE-2.0>
//!
//! Unless required by applicable law or agreed to in writing, software
//! distributed under the License is distributed on an "AS IS" BASIS,
//! WITHOUT WARRANTIES OR CONDITIONS OF ANY KIND, either express or implied.
//! See the License for the specific language governing permissions and
//! limitations under the License.

use goose::prelude::*;
use std::time::{Duration, Instant};
use tokio::io::{AsyncReadExt, AsyncWriteExt};
use tokio::net::TcpStream;

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), GooseError> {
    GooseAttack::initialize()?
        .register_scenario(
            scenario!("TcpUser")
                .set_wait_time(Duration::from_millis(100), Duration::from_millis(500))?
                .register_transaction(transaction!(tcp_echo).set_name("tcp_echo")),
        )
        .execute()
        .await?;

    Ok(())
}

/// Connects to the TCP server specified by `--host`, sends a payload, reads the
/// echo response, and records the round-trip as a Goose `TCP` metric.
///
/// # Persistent connections
///
/// This example opens a new TCP connection for each transaction. For production
/// load tests you may want to keep connections persistent across transactions
/// using Goose session data. Because `TcpStream` is not `Clone`, wrap it in
/// `Arc<tokio::sync::Mutex<TcpStream>>`:
///
/// ```ignore
/// // In an on_start transaction:
/// user.set_session_data(Arc::new(tokio::sync::Mutex::new(stream)));
///
/// // In subsequent transactions:
/// let stream = user.get_session_data_unchecked::<Arc<tokio::sync::Mutex<TcpStream>>>();
/// let mut guard = stream.lock().await;
/// ```
///
/// See `examples/session.rs` for the full session data pattern.
async fn tcp_echo(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let host = user.base_url.host_str().unwrap_or("127.0.0.1");
    let port = user.base_url.port().ok_or_else(|| {
        Box::new(TransactionError::Custom(
            "--host must include a port number (e.g. --host tcp://127.0.0.1:9000)".to_string(),
        ))
    })?;
    let addr = format!("{}:{}", host, port);

    let payload = b"Hello, TCP!\n";

    let started = Instant::now();

    let result = async {
        let mut stream = TcpStream::connect(&addr).await?;
        stream.write_all(payload).await?;

        // Read echoed response (same length as what we sent).
        let mut buf = vec![0u8; payload.len()];
        stream.read_exact(&mut buf).await?;

        Ok::<_, std::io::Error>(buf)
    }
    .await;

    // A Duration keeps the round trip's microseconds, as Goose keeps an HTTP
    // request's.
    let response_time = started.elapsed();

    match result {
        Ok(_response) => {
            // Pass None for status_code — TCP has no HTTP-style status codes.
            user.record_custom_request_duration("TCP", "tcp_echo", response_time, true, None, None)
                .await?;
        }
        Err(e) => {
            let error_msg = e.to_string();
            user.record_custom_request_duration(
                "TCP",
                "tcp_echo",
                response_time,
                false,
                None, // TCP has no status codes
                Some(&error_msg),
            )
            .await?;
        }
    }

    Ok(())
}

Drupal Memcache Example

The examples/drupal_memcache.rs example is used to validate the performance of each release of the Drupal Memcache Module.

This page has no picture of a run, because the example needs its own test site to run cleanly. It requests random nodes up to /node/9999 and profiles up to /user/5000, and logs in as user3 to user5001 with the password 12345. It also posts Drupal 7 forms: the login form user_login, and comments with the field comment_body[und][0][value]. It needs a Drupal 7 site prepared as the drupal-loadtest repository describes. A 30 second run with 10 users against the Drupal 11 Umami site that the Umami page uses, a site with 19 nodes, made 17,083 requests. Of those, 1,435 failed with 404s, nearly all of its node and profile requests; the front page and all 14,076 static asset requests succeeded.

Background

Prior to every release of the Drupal Memcache Module, Tag1 Consulting has run a load test to check that the release performs as well as the last; the module runs on tens of thousands of Drupal websites.

The load test began as a JMeter testplan, was converted to a Locust testplan, and is now a Goose testplan.

The Goose port is kept here as an example of a real load test.

Details

The load test defines two scenarios. AnonBrowsingUser has a weight of 4 and AuthBrowsingUser a weight of 1, so four of every five users browse anonymously. Each scenario loads the front page (weight 15), a random node from /node/1 to /node/9999 (weight 10) and a random user profile from /user/2 to /user/5000 (weight 3); the authenticated scenario also posts comments (weight 3). The front page transaction then loads each image and script the page references whose path contains /misc or /themes, as requests named static asset. Transactions are named (Anon) front page, (Auth) node page and so on.

Users of the AuthBrowsingUser scenario log in once, when each starts:

            scenario!("AuthBrowsingUser")
                .set_weight(1)?
                .register_transaction(
                    transaction!(drupal_memcache_login)
                        .set_on_start()
                        .set_name("(Auth) login"),
                )

Each AuthBrowsingUser logs in as a random user from user3 to user5001, accounts that exist only on a site prepared for this test:

                    let uid: usize = rand::rng().random_range(3..5_002);
                    let username = format!("user{uid}");
                    let params = [
                        ("name", username.as_str()),
                        ("pass", "12345"),
                        ("form_build_id", &form_build_id[1]),
                        ("form_id", "user_login"),
                        ("op", "Log+in"),
                    ];
                    let _goose = user.post_form("/user", &params).await?;
                    // @TODO: verify that we actually logged in.

The test also includes an example of how to post a comment during a load test, after reading the form’s form_build_id, form_token and form_id from the node page:

                    let comment_body = "this is a test comment body";
                    let params = [
                        ("subject", "this is a test comment subject"),
                        ("comment_body[und][0][value]", comment_body),
                        ("comment_body[und][0][format]", "filtered_html"),
                        ("form_build_id", &form_build_id[1]),
                        ("form_token", &form_token[1]),
                        ("form_id", &form_id[1]),
                        ("op", "Save"),
                    ];

                    // Post the comment.
                    let mut goose = user.post_form(&comment_path, &params).await?;

The Goose Eggs library has Drupal helpers that shorten much of this.

Complete Source Code

//! Conversion of Locust load test used for the Drupal memcache module, from
//! <https://github.com/tag1consulting/drupal-loadtest/>
//!
//! To run, you must set up the load test environment as described in the above
//! repository, and then run the example. You'll need to set --host and may want
//! to set other command line options as well, starting with:
//!      cargo run --release --example drupal_memcache --
//!
//! ## License
//!
//! Copyright 2020-2022 Jeremy Andrews
//!
//! Licensed under the Apache License, Version 2.0 (the "License");
//! you may not use this file except in compliance with the License.
//! You may obtain a copy of the License at
//!
//! <http://www.apache.org/licenses/LICENSE-2.0>
//!
//! Unless required by applicable law or agreed to in writing, software
//! distributed under the License is distributed on an "AS IS" BASIS,
//! WITHOUT WARRANTIES OR CONDITIONS OF ANY KIND, either express or implied.
//! See the License for the specific language governing permissions and
//! limitations under the License.

use goose::prelude::*;

use rand::Rng;
use regex::Regex;

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), GooseError> {
    GooseAttack::initialize()?
        .register_scenario(
            scenario!("AnonBrowsingUser")
                .set_weight(4)?
                .register_transaction(
                    transaction!(drupal_memcache_front_page)
                        .set_weight(15)?
                        .set_name("(Anon) front page"),
                )
                .register_transaction(
                    transaction!(drupal_memcache_node_page)
                        .set_weight(10)?
                        .set_name("(Anon) node page"),
                )
                .register_transaction(
                    transaction!(drupal_memcache_profile_page)
                        .set_weight(3)?
                        .set_name("(Anon) user page"),
                ),
        )
        .register_scenario(
            scenario!("AuthBrowsingUser")
                .set_weight(1)?
                .register_transaction(
                    transaction!(drupal_memcache_login)
                        .set_on_start()
                        .set_name("(Auth) login"),
                )
                .register_transaction(
                    transaction!(drupal_memcache_front_page)
                        .set_weight(15)?
                        .set_name("(Auth) front page"),
                )
                .register_transaction(
                    transaction!(drupal_memcache_node_page)
                        .set_weight(10)?
                        .set_name("(Auth) node page"),
                )
                .register_transaction(
                    transaction!(drupal_memcache_profile_page)
                        .set_weight(3)?
                        .set_name("(Auth) user page"),
                )
                .register_transaction(
                    transaction!(drupal_memcache_post_comment)
                        .set_weight(3)?
                        .set_name("(Auth) comment form"),
                ),
        )
        .execute()
        .await?;

    Ok(())
}

/// View the front page.
async fn drupal_memcache_front_page(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let mut goose = user.get("/").await?;

    match goose.response {
        Ok(response) => {
            // Copy the headers so we have them for logging if there are errors.
            let headers = &response.headers().clone();
            match response.text().await {
                Ok(t) => {
                    let re = Regex::new(r#"src="(.*?)""#).unwrap();
                    // Collect copy of URLs to run them async
                    let mut urls = Vec::new();
                    for url in re.captures_iter(&t) {
                        if url[1].contains("/misc") || url[1].contains("/themes") {
                            urls.push(url[1].to_string());
                        }
                    }
                    for asset in &urls {
                        let _ = user.get_named(asset, "static asset").await;
                    }
                }
                Err(e) => {
                    // This will automatically get written to the error log if enabled, and will
                    // be displayed to stdout if `-v` is enabled when running the load test.
                    return user.set_failure(
                        &format!("front_page: failed to parse page: {e}"),
                        &mut goose.request,
                        Some(headers),
                        None,
                    );
                }
            }
        }
        Err(e) => {
            // This will automatically get written to the error log if enabled, and will
            // be displayed to stdout if `-v` is enabled when running the load test.
            return user.set_failure(
                &format!("front_page: no response from server: {e}"),
                &mut goose.request,
                None,
                None,
            );
        }
    }

    Ok(())
}

/// View a node from 1 to 10,000, created by preptest.sh.
async fn drupal_memcache_node_page(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let nid = rand::rng().random_range(1..10_000);
    let _goose = user.get(format!("/node/{}", nid).as_str()).await?;

    Ok(())
}

/// View a profile from 2 to 5,001, created by preptest.sh.
async fn drupal_memcache_profile_page(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let uid = rand::rng().random_range(2..5_001);
    let _goose = user.get(format!("/user/{}", uid).as_str()).await?;

    Ok(())
}

/// Log in.
async fn drupal_memcache_login(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let mut goose = user.get("/user").await?;

    match goose.response {
        Ok(response) => {
            // Copy the headers so we have them for logging if there are errors.
            let headers = &response.headers().clone();
            match response.text().await {
                Ok(html) => {
                    let re = Regex::new(r#"name="form_build_id" value=['"](.*?)['"]"#).unwrap();
                    let form_build_id = match re.captures(&html) {
                        Some(f) => f,
                        None => {
                            // This will automatically get written to the error log if enabled, and will
                            // be displayed to stdout if `-v` is enabled when running the load test.
                            return user.set_failure(
                                "login: no form_build_id on page: /user page",
                                &mut goose.request,
                                Some(headers),
                                Some(&html),
                            );
                        }
                    };

                    // Log the user in.
                    let uid: usize = rand::rng().random_range(3..5_002);
                    let username = format!("user{uid}");
                    let params = [
                        ("name", username.as_str()),
                        ("pass", "12345"),
                        ("form_build_id", &form_build_id[1]),
                        ("form_id", "user_login"),
                        ("op", "Log+in"),
                    ];
                    let _goose = user.post_form("/user", &params).await?;
                    // @TODO: verify that we actually logged in.
                }
                Err(e) => {
                    // This will automatically get written to the error log if enabled, and will
                    // be displayed to stdout if `-v` is enabled when running the load test.
                    return user.set_failure(
                        &format!("login: unexpected error when loading /user page: {e}"),
                        &mut goose.request,
                        Some(headers),
                        None,
                    );
                }
            }
        }
        // Goose will catch this error.
        Err(e) => {
            // This will automatically get written to the error log if enabled, and will
            // be displayed to stdout if `-v` is enabled when running the load test.
            return user.set_failure(
                &format!("login: no response from server: {e}"),
                &mut goose.request,
                None,
                None,
            );
        }
    }

    Ok(())
}

/// Post a comment.
async fn drupal_memcache_post_comment(user: &mut GooseUser) -> TransactionResult {
    let nid: i32 = rand::rng().random_range(1..10_000);
    let node_path = format!("node/{}", nid);
    let comment_path = format!("/comment/reply/{}", nid);

    let mut goose = user.get(&node_path).await?;

    match goose.response {
        Ok(response) => {
            // Copy the headers so we have them for logging if there are errors.
            let headers = &response.headers().clone();
            match response.text().await {
                Ok(html) => {
                    // Extract the form_build_id from the user login form.
                    let re = Regex::new(r#"name="form_build_id" value=['"](.*?)['"]"#).unwrap();
                    let form_build_id = match re.captures(&html) {
                        Some(f) => f,
                        None => {
                            // This will automatically get written to the error log if enabled, and will
                            // be displayed to stdout if `-v` is enabled when running the load test.
                            return user.set_failure(
                                &format!("post_comment: no form_build_id found on {}", node_path),
                                &mut goose.request,
                                Some(headers),
                                Some(&html),
                            );
                        }
                    };

                    let re = Regex::new(r#"name="form_token" value=['"](.*?)['"]"#).unwrap();
                    let form_token = match re.captures(&html) {
                        Some(f) => f,
                        None => {
                            // This will automatically get written to the error log if enabled, and will
                            // be displayed to stdout if `-v` is enabled when running the load test.
                            return user.set_failure(
                                &format!("post_comment: no form_token found on {}", node_path),
                                &mut goose.request,
                                Some(headers),
                                Some(&html),
                            );
                        }
                    };

                    let re = Regex::new(r#"name="form_id" value=['"](.*?)['"]"#).unwrap();
                    let form_id = match re.captures(&html) {
                        Some(f) => f,
                        None => {
                            // This will automatically get written to the error log if enabled, and will
                            // be displayed to stdout if `-v` is enabled when running the load test.
                            return user.set_failure(
                                &format!("post_comment: no form_id found on {}", node_path),
                                &mut goose.request,
                                Some(headers),
                                Some(&html),
                            );
                        }
                    };
                    // Optionally uncomment to log form_id, form_build_id, and form_token, together with
                    // the full body of the page. This is useful when modifying the load test.
                    /*
                    user.log_debug(
                        &format!(
                            "form_id: {}, form_build_id: {}, form_token: {}",
                            &form_id[1], &form_build_id[1], &form_token[1]
                        ),
                        Some(&goose.request),
                        Some(headers),
                        Some(&html),
                    );
                    */

                    let comment_body = "this is a test comment body";
                    let params = [
                        ("subject", "this is a test comment subject"),
                        ("comment_body[und][0][value]", comment_body),
                        ("comment_body[und][0][format]", "filtered_html"),
                        ("form_build_id", &form_build_id[1]),
                        ("form_token", &form_token[1]),
                        ("form_id", &form_id[1]),
                        ("op", "Save"),
                    ];

                    // Post the comment.
                    let mut goose = user.post_form(&comment_path, &params).await?;

                    // Verify that the comment posted.
                    match goose.response {
                        Ok(response) => {
                            // Copy the headers so we have them for logging if there are errors.
                            let headers = &response.headers().clone();
                            match response.text().await {
                                Ok(html) => {
                                    if !html.contains(comment_body) {
                                        // This will automatically get written to the error log if enabled, and will
                                        // be displayed to stdout if `-v` is enabled when running the load test.
                                        return user.set_failure(
                                            &format!("post_comment: no comment showed up after posting to {}", comment_path),
                                            &mut goose.request,
                                            Some(headers),
                                            Some(&html),
                                        );
                                    }
                                }
                                Err(e) => {
                                    // This will automatically get written to the error log if enabled, and will
                                    // be displayed to stdout if `-v` is enabled when running the load test.
                                    return user.set_failure(
                                        &format!(
                                            "post_comment: unexpected error when posting to {}: {}",
                                            comment_path, e
                                        ),
                                        &mut goose.request,
                                        Some(headers),
                                        None,
                                    );
                                }
                            }
                        }
                        Err(e) => {
                            // This will automatically get written to the error log if enabled, and will
                            // be displayed to stdout if `-v` is enabled when running the load test.
                            return user.set_failure(
                                &format!(
                                    "post_comment: no response when posting to {}: {}",
                                    comment_path, e
                                ),
                                &mut goose.request,
                                None,
                                None,
                            );
                        }
                    }
                }
                Err(e) => {
                    // This will automatically get written to the error log if enabled, and will
                    // be displayed to stdout if `-v` is enabled when running the load test.
                    return user.set_failure(
                        &format!("post_comment: no text when loading {}: {}", node_path, e),
                        &mut goose.request,
                        None,
                        None,
                    );
                }
            }
        }
        Err(e) => {
            // This will automatically get written to the error log if enabled, and will
            // be displayed to stdout if `-v` is enabled when running the load test.
            return user.set_failure(
                &format!(
                    "post_comment: no response when loading {}: {}",
                    node_path, e
                ),
                &mut goose.request,
                None,
                None,
            );
        }
    }

    Ok(())
}

Umami Example

The examples/umami example load tests the Umami demonstration profile included with Drupal core.

cargo run --release --features dashboard --example umami -- --host http://localhost:18888 -u 50 -r 5 -t 3m --no-reset-metrics --dashboard -q, against a Drupal 11 site installed with the Umami demo profile, with CSS and JS aggregation turned off and the admin account admin with the password P@ssw0rd1234, 120 seconds in.

The Scenarios table shows that with 50 users the weights 40, 9 and 1 give exactly 40, 9 and 1 users. The Transactions table goes on past the six English rows shown here to 23 rows in all: the rest of the English user’s, the Spanish user’s and the admin user’s. In about the first two minutes of the run, the 50 users made 61,652 requests with none failed; 57,772 of them were the images and scripts the pages name, which the example requests under the one name static asset. With -q Goose does not print the dashboard’s address when it starts: open http://127.0.0.1:5118 while the test runs. If that tab is still open when the run ends, Goose prints the address and waits for Ctrl-C.

Turn CSS and JS aggregation off on the site: the example does not decode &amp; in the aggregated asset links it parses, so the URLs it requests still hold &amp; where the page meant &. With aggregation off it loads no CSS, because it looks for stylesheets only under the aggregated path.

Overview

The Umami profile installs a sample food magazine site in English and Spanish, with articles, recipes, basic pages, several taxonomies, search and a contact form.

The included example simulates three different types of users: an anonymous user browsing the site in English (scenario Anonymous English user, weight 40), an anonymous user browsing the site in Spanish (Anonymous Spanish user, weight 9), and an administrative user that logs into the site (Admin user, weight 1). The anonymous users load most of the site’s page types. The anonymous user browsing the site in English loads the front page, the About Umami basic page, the article listing and a random article, the recipe listing and a random recipe, a random node by node id, a random term listing, runs searches using words from the site’s content, and fills out the site’s contact form. The Spanish user does the same in Spanish, except loading a node by node id. The admin user logs in once, then loads the front page, the article listing, and edits and saves a random article. Goose counts any response that is not a 2xx as a failure. On each page the example also checks the HTML for the expected title and loads the images and scripts the page references.

The example load tests https://drupal-9.ddev.site/ unless --host names another site. The admin user logs in as admin with the password P@ssw0rd1234; set the ADMIN_USERNAME or ADMIN_PASSWORD environment variable to change either.

Read the blog A Goose In The Clouds: Load Testing At Scale for a demonstration of using this example, and learn more about the testplan from the README.

Alternative

The Goose Eggs library contains a variation of the Umami example.

Complete Source Code

This example is more complex than the other examples, and is split into multiple files, all of which can be found within examples/umami.

Upgrading From 0.18

Change the dependency to goose = "^0.19". The changes below can break a load test written for Goose 0.18, or change what it does.

Removed options

0.180.19
--hatch-rate (-r)--increase-rate (-r), see launching users
--startup-time (-s)--increase-time (-s), see launching users
GooseDefault::HatchRateGooseDefault::IncreaseRate, see Defaults
GooseDefault::StartupTimeGooseDefault::IncreaseTime, see Defaults
GooseConfiguration fields hatch_rate and startup_timeincrease_rate and increase_time, see Custom options
goose::util::get_hatch_rate()goose::util::parse_rate()
Controller commands hatchrate and startuptimeincreaserate and increasetime, see Controller commands
Crate feature gaggleNone: Gaggle was removed in 0.17.0 and will be restored in 0.20, before the 1.0 release. For a distributed load test today, use 0.16.4; see Gaggle.

The new --decrease-rate and --decrease-time control the ramp down; without them Goose stops all users at once, as 0.18 did. See the ramp down rate. Neither is allowed with --iterations: the run fails at startup with `configuration.decrease_rate` can not be set with `configuration.iterations`., or the same for decrease_time. Drop the decrease option, as each user stops after its iterations anyway.

API changes

  • API change: the command line is parsed with clap. Remove use gumdrop::Options;: GooseConfiguration::parse_args_default() is now a method of its own, and GooseConfiguration implements clap::Parser. See Custom options.
  • API change: every GooseConfiguration field for an option that takes a single value, such as host, run_time, increase_time or iterations, is an Option, None when not given; set one with Some, as in configuration.run_time = Some("30s".to_string()). The help field is gone. See Custom options.
  • API change: session data must implement Clone; add #[derive(Clone)] to the struct. See the Session example.
  • API change: GooseConfiguration::parse_args_default() returns GooseError::InvalidOption for -h and --help, with the flag as option and the detail help was requested, where 0.18 returned the configuration with its help field set to true. See Building the configuration yourself.
  • API change: TransactionError::RequestFailed { raw_request } holds a Box<GooseRequestMetric>, TransactionError::LoggerFailed { source } a boxed SendError, and the new TransactionError::Custom(String) lets a transaction return Err("reason".into()). See Transaction error types.
  • API change: a match without a wildcard arm needs new arms for TransactionError::Custom (see Transaction error types), GooseError::ThresholdsFailed (see The end of the run), GooseMethod::Custom (see The API) and the new GooseDefault variants (see Defaults).
  • API change: scenario and transaction names are Arc<str> in place of String: Scenario.name, Scenario.machine_name, the name of ScenarioMetric, TransactionMetric and ScenarioMetricAggregate, TransactionMetricAggregate.scenario_name, GooseRequestMetric.name and GooseRequestMetric.scenario_name, and the scenario_name argument of GooseUser::new(). Reading one as &str works unchanged. See the API documentation.
  • API change: Transaction.name and the transaction_name of GooseRequestMetric and TransactionMetricAggregate are a goose::goose::TransactionName in place of a String: TransactionOnly(Arc<str>) for a name set with set_name(), or InheritNameByRequests(Arc<str>) for one set with set_name_for_transaction_and_requests(). Read the name with name_for_transaction(). GooseRequestMetric.transaction_index is a usize in place of a String. See Naming Transactions and the API documentation.
  • API change: times are kept in microseconds. GooseRequestMetricTimingData has times_us, minimum_time_us, maximum_time_us and total_time_us in place of the millisecond times, minimum_time, maximum_time and total_time; TransactionMetricAggregate and ScenarioMetricAggregate keep their times in run_times; a struct literal of GooseRequestMetric, TransactionMetric or ScenarioMetric must set the new response_time_fraction_us or run_time_fraction_us. See Response times and the API documentation of GooseRequestMetricTimingData, TransactionMetricAggregate and ScenarioMetricAggregate.
  • goose-eggs 0.6 is built for Goose 0.18. A load test that uses goose-eggs moves to Goose 0.19 with goose-eggs 0.7: set goose-eggs = "0.7". See Goose Eggs.
  • API change: the default crate features are now cookies, so default-features = false turns off cookie support, where in 0.18 it turned off TLS, and the rustls-tls feature no longer changes the TLS that load test requests use.
  • API change: controller::ControllerCommand carries each command’s parsed value, and util::parse_timespan() returns Option<usize>. See Controller commands and the API documentation of ControllerCommand and parse_timespan().
  • API change: TestPlan::total_users() stops at usize::MAX in place of overflowing, and GooseError::InvalidControllerCommand is no longer returned, though the variant is kept. See the API documentation of TestPlan.
  • API change: TransactionDetail has no lifetime parameter: its scenario_name is an Arc<str>, its transaction_index an Option<usize> and its transaction_name a TransactionName, where 0.18 held &strs. GooseRequestMetric.transaction_index is usize::MAX for a request made outside a transaction. See the API documentation of TransactionDetail.
  • API change: a transaction error is recorded. GooseMetrics has the new public field transaction_errors, a GooseTransactionErrorMetrics of GooseTransactionErrorMetricAggregates, and GooseTransactionErrorMetric is an error log entry. GooseMetric has the new variants All, Batch and TransactionError, and GooseLog the new variants TransactionError and FailedTransaction, so a match on either without a wildcard arm needs new arms. See Error log.
  • API change: GooseMetrics has the new public field thresholds, each rule’s ThresholdResult, and the new goose::threshold module holds Threshold and ThresholdResult. See Thresholds.
  • API change: GooseConfiguration has new public fields: threshold, no_threshold_abort, threshold_abort_delay, output_dir, compress, notes, report_graph_data, render_report and websocket, among others, and, with the pdf-reports feature, pdf_scale and pdf_timeout, an Option<f64> and an Option<u64> set in code with GooseDefault::PdfScale and GooseDefault::PdfTimeout. See Custom options and Output files.
  • API change: config::RUN_FOLDER and reset_killswitch() are public. See Output files and Killswitch.

Behavior changes

  • behavior change: Transaction::set_name() names only the transaction; its requests keep their own names. Use set_name_for_transaction_and_requests() for the 0.18 behavior. See Naming transactions.
  • behavior change: --scenarios matches machine names exactly, where 0.18 matched any part of the name; use * as a wildcard, as in --scenarios "anon*". See Limiting scenarios.
  • behavior change: HTTPS uses rustls in place of the system’s native TLS library. Certificates are still checked by the operating system’s verifier, so a CA the system trusts is trusted, and --accept-invalid-certs skips the check in every build. See TLS.
  • behavior change: an option value that begins with a hyphen goes after =, as in --goose-log=-run.log. Given as its own argument it is read as options, so --goose-log -run.log reads as --goose-log and -r un.log, and fails because un.log is not a number. When the command line itself does not parse, as with --goose-log -x.log, the error ends with a tip to use =. A negative number is still read as a value: --timeout -5 reaches the timeout check and is refused there, and --users -1 is refused with a tip that --users takes no negative number. An output option followed by another option, as in --goose-log --quiet, is given without a path and writes goose.log into the run’s folder. See Output files.
  • behavior change: a report or log file whose name ends in .gz is written gzipped. See Compression.
  • behavior change: --report-file reports are written even with --no-print-metrics. See Output files.
  • behavior change: -h prints a short summary and --help the full reference, both to stdout. See Run-time options.
  • behavior change: --run-time 0, --increase-time 0, --decrease-time 0 and --throttle-requests 0 undo the matching GooseDefault, and an empty log path, as in --request-log "", turns off a log a GooseDefault sets; GooseDefault::Iterations and GooseDefault::NoGranularData now take effect. See Defaults.
  • behavior change: values that started no users or broke Goose are refused before the load test starts: a test plan with no users, a time span too large to hold, an increase or decrease rate below one user a day, a rate or --timeout that is not a finite number, and a --timeout that rounds to less than 1 ms, including a negative one. A fractional --timeout is rounded to the nearest millisecond, so 0.0005 runs as 1 ms and anything below it is refused. See Test plan and Run-time options.
  • behavior change: a time span, as given to --run-time, --increase-time, --decrease-time, --threshold-abort-delay, a test plan step or a Controller command, is only hours, minutes and seconds written as h, m and s, as in 1h30m or 45s, in either case. 0.18 read 15mins, 5hours and 2h3min by their leading part, as 15 minutes, 5 hours and 2 hours 3 minutes; 0.19 refuses them, so write 15m, 5h and 2h3m. See Run-time options.
  • behavior change: times under 10 ms are shown to the hundredth of a millisecond, and percentiles are never more than 1% off, so figures differ from the whole milliseconds 0.18 printed. See Response times.
  • behavior change: the JSON report’s timing data holds times_us in place of times, its averages are means of the microseconds, it includes the test plan history, and each transaction’s transaction_name is an object such as {"TransactionOnly":"login"}, where 0.18 wrote a string. See JSON report.
  • behavior change: the CSV request log ends with a response_time_us column, the scenario log with run_time_us, and the transaction log with run_time_us and then error; the JSON, raw and pretty formats add response_time_fraction_us or run_time_fraction_us. In JSON logs the keys follow the order of the fields instead of being sorted, transaction_name is an object such as {"TransactionOnly":"login"}, where 0.18 wrote a string (the error log’s new transaction error entries write it as a string); the CSV request log’s transaction_name column is empty unless the name was set with set_name_for_transaction_and_requests(); and the request, debug and error logs write transaction_index as a number, such as 0, where 0.18 wrote the string "0". See Logging and Request log.
  • behavior change: elapsed in the request, error, transaction and scenario logs counts milliseconds since the load test started, where 0.18 counted from when each user was built; GooseUser.started is likewise the time the load test started, the same for every user, and the HTML report’s active users graph shows the increase ramp. See Logging, Request log and Users.
  • behavior change: GooseRawRequest.headers, as read through goose.request.raw.headers or a TransactionError::RequestFailed, is empty unless the request log, debug log or error log is enabled. See Request log.
  • behavior change: Controller users takes 1 to 100000, and on a running load test keeps its run time and ramp down; runtime changes a running users ramp; a value that can’t be used is answered with what it must be; config-json (and the WebSocket Controller’s config) shows an unset option as null and has no help key, and telnet config shows None for an unset option and Some(...) around a set one. See Controller commands.
  • behavior change: the telnet Controller’s metrics-json and the WebSocket Controller’s metrics replies hold times_us in place of times and write each transaction’s transaction_name as an object, as the JSON report does, and a WebSocket message over 131,072 bytes closes the connection. See WebSocket Controller.
  • behavior change: the killswitch keeps the first reason it is given, and is cleared when a run starts. See Killswitch.
  • behavior change: users start and stop on time at any rate. 0.18 rounded the time between two users up to the next half second when it was over 500 ms, so --increase-rate 1.5 started 1 user a second and a test plan step of 500 users in 5 minutes took 8 minutes 20 seconds. In 0.19 --increase-rate 1.5 starts a user every 667 ms, and that step takes 5 minutes. This applies to --increase-rate, --decrease-rate, test plan steps and users changed by a Controller. See Specifying the increase rate.
  • behavior change: GooseScheduler::Random shuffles transactions. 0.18 ran them in Serial order. 0.19 shuffles each scenario’s whole list once, so every user of a scenario runs the same random order; sequenced transactions are shuffled only within their sequence, and on_start and on_stop transactions run as before. See Random Scheduler.
  • behavior change: a transaction’s error is recorded. Each line of the transaction log, in every format, ends with an error field, empty for a transaction that succeeded. The error log gets an entry for each transaction error, and its CSV header gains the columns scenario_index, scenario_name, transaction_index, transaction_name and run_time after error; a request error row leaves them empty. The console summary and the HTML, Markdown, PDF and JSON reports add a Transaction Errors table when there are any, which --no-error-summary leaves out. See Transaction log and Error log.
  • behavior change: the JSON report keeps each request’s times by status code in status_code_timings, under raw_metrics.requests, and holds transaction errors in transaction_errors. Its raw_request_metrics rows are each request and the Aggregated row. See JSON report.
  • behavior change: with --co-mitigation, the Aggregated # reqs is the “Actual requests” plus the “Synthetic requests” of the coordinated omission summary, and those counts start again when the metrics are reset after the ramp up. See Coordinated omission metrics.
  • behavior change: the errors and transaction errors tables start again when the metrics are reset after the ramp up, as the other tables do, where 0.18 kept the ramp up’s errors in them. --no-reset-metrics keeps them, and the error log still records every error. See Metrics.
  • behavior change: a scenario counts every user that ran it in the measured time, including users that had not completed an iteration, and the HTML, Markdown and JSON reports’ Aggregated scenario Iterations is all runs divided by all users, as on the console, where 0.18’s reports added up each scenario’s iterations. See Scenarios.
  • behavior change: an average with no times to average, such as a scenario no user ran, is 0, where it was NaN (null in the JSON report). See Metrics.
  • behavior change: --scenarios accepts _, so name_1 selects a second scenario registered as name, and a pattern that matches no scenario logs a warning. See Limiting scenarios.
  • behavior change: a telnet or WebSocket Controller that can’t listen on its port logs an error, and the load test runs without it; --no-autostart is then refused when no Controller listens and dashboard control is off, as nothing could start the run. See Controlling a running load test and Printer-friendly HTML report example.
  • behavior change: a load test that a Controller starts again after it stopped counts its requests from zero. See Controlling a running load test.
  • behavior change: the first column of the Markdown report’s scenario table is headed Scenario, where 0.18 headed it Transaction. See Markdown report.
  • behavior change: --telnet-port 0 and --websocket-port 0 are refused, where 0.18 read them as the default ports 5116 and 5117. Leave the option out for the default. See Controlling a running load test.
  • behavior change: the WebSocket Controller is off by default, as it takes no authentication and any web page a browser opens could reach it. A load test that used it adds --websocket or GooseDefault::WebSocket; --no-websocket and GooseDefault::NoWebSocket still run, with a warning, and are refused with --websocket. --no-autostart --no-telnet now needs --websocket or --dashboard-control. See WebSocket Controller.
  • behavior change: the telnet and WebSocket Controllers listen on 127.0.0.1 by default, where 0.18 listened on 0.0.0.0, as neither has authentication. A load test controlled over telnet or WebSocket from another machine, or from outside its container, now needs --telnet-host 0.0.0.0 or --websocket-host 0.0.0.0 (or GooseDefault::TelnetHost or GooseDefault::WebSocketHost), or an ssh tunnel to 127.0.0.1:5116; a Controller listening on any address that is not loopback prints a warning at startup. See Controlling a running load test.
  • behavior change: a Controller’s config and config-json show a dashboard auth token that is set as (set), never the token, as does {:?} of a GooseConfiguration. See Controller commands.
